Accurate waveforms for generic planar-orbit binary black holes: The multipolar effective-one-body model SEOBNRv6EHM
Aldo Gamboa, Alessandra Buonanno, Lorenzo Pompili, Raffi Enficiaud, Michael Boyle, Lawrence E. Kidder, Oliver Long, Peter James Nee, Harald P. Pfeiffer, Antoni Ramos-Buades, Mark A. Scheel

Abstract
Accurate and computationally efficient waveform models are required to infer the parameters of compact binaries from their gravitational wave (GW) emission. Among these parameters, orbital eccentricity serves as a smoking gun for dynamical formation channels and must be accounted for to avoid systematic errors in GW analyses. Here, we present SEOBNRv6EHM , a time-domain, multipolar waveform model for binaries on generic planar orbits, calibrated to quasi-circular (QC) numerical-relativity (NR) simulations from the SXS collaboration. In addition to the dominant ( 2 , 2 ) (2,2) mode, the model provides the ( 2 , 1 ) (2,1) , ( 3 , 3 ) (3,3) , ( 3 , 2 ) (3,2) , ( 4 , 4 ) (4,4) , and ( 4 , 3 ) (4,3) multipoles for the full inspiral-merger-ringdown process of coalescing binaries, as well as for dynamical captures and scattering encounters. The model is built within the effective-one-body (EOB) framework, and it employs novel resummations of the radiation-reaction force and waveform modes. We validate its accuracy through comparisons against 592 QC, 319 eccentric, one dynamical-capture, and two scattering SXS NR waveforms, and through scattering-angle comparisons against 61 SXS NR simulations. For QC and small-eccentricity binaries, its accuracy is comparable to previous-generation SEOBNRv5 models. For highly eccentric systems, however, SEOBNRv6EHM attains unprecedented accuracy, with waveform mismatches remaining below or close to 2 % 2\% across the total mass range 20 − 200 ​ M ⊙ 20-200\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot} for eccentricities up to ∼ 0.9 \sim 0.9 at 14 periastron passages before merger. Additionally, SEOBNRv6EHM achieves waveform-generation walltimes that are 2 − 6 2-6 times faster than other state-of-the-art EOB eccentric models, enabling efficient and accurate applications in GW astronomy.



I
Introduction

Over 200 gravitational-wave (GW) signals from compact binary coalescences have been observed by the LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA (LVK) collaboration
Aasi et al. 2015
;
Acernese et al. 2015
;
Akutsu et al. 2021
;
Abbott et al. 2016a
;
Abbott et al. 2019a
;
Abbott et al. 2021
;
Abbott et al. 2024
;
Abbott et al. 2023
;
LIG 2025a
;
LIG 2025b
.
These GW events are typically associated with binaries composed of black holes (BHs) or neutron stars (NSs) moving on inspiraling quasi-circular (QC) orbits.
Such orbits are astrophysically expected, as GW emission circularizes the binary
Peters and Mathews 1963
;
Peters 1964
;
Hinder et al. 2008
;
Sperhake et al. 2008
over the long lifetime predicted for systems formed in isolation
Bethe and Brown 1998
;
Belczynski et al. 2001
;
Belczynski et al. 2014
;
Belczynski et al. 2016
;
Stevenson et al. 2017
;
Giacobbo and Mapelli 2018
;
Mandel and Farmer 2022
.

However, astrophysical formation channels also predict a small but non-negligible population of merging binaries with detectable
orbital eccentricity
.
Specifically, the dynamical formation channels are expected to produce eccentric binaries that merge within the sensitive frequency band of current LVK detectors
Sadowski et al. 2008
;
O’Leary et al. 2009
;
Rodriguez et al. 2015
;
Stone et al. 2017
;
Gondán et al. 2018
;
Takátsy et al. 2019
;
Fragione and Kocsis 2018
;
Rasskazov and Kocsis 2019
;
Sedda et al. 2024
;
Singh et al. 2025
and future GW observatories
Punturo et al. 2010
;
Luo et al. 2016
;
Reitze et al. 2019
;
Colpi et al. 2024
.
These dynamical formation mechanisms are viable in dense stellar environments where different types of gravitational interactions become feasible, including von Zeipel-Kozai-Lidov oscillations in hierarchical triple systems
von Zeipel 1910
;
Kozai 1962
;
Lidov 1962
;
Kimpson et al. 2016
;
VanLandingham et al. 2016
;
Hoang et al. 2018
;
Fragione et al. 2019
;
Britt et al. 2021
;
Grishin et al. 2025
and
n
-body processes
Portegies Zwart and McMillan 2000
;
Miller and Hamilton 2002
;
Gultekin et al. 2006
;
Samsing et al. 2014
;
Samsing and Ramirez-Ruiz 2017
;
Samsing 2018
;
Rodriguez et al. 2018
;
Zevin et al. 2019
;
Arca-Sedda et al. 2021
;
Tagawa et al. 2021
.
In this way, the presence of eccentricity in GWs serves as a clean discriminator (a
smoking gun
) between the isolated and dynamical formation channels
Breivik et al. 2016
;
Nishizawa et al. 2017
;
Samsing and D’Orazio 2018
;
Zevin et al. 2021
;
DePorzio et al. 2024
;
Sedda et al. 2026
.

Neglecting orbital eccentricity has widespread consequences across different GW analyses.
A substantial fraction of potential eccentric GW signals may be missed by standard matched-filtering searches relying on QC waveform templates
Brown and Zimmerman 2010
;
Huerta and Brown 2013
;
Wang and Nitz 2021
;
Gadre et al. 2024
, motivating the inclusion of eccentricity in GW search strategies
Abbott et al. 2019b
;
Abac et al. 2023
;
Nitz et al. 2019
;
Nitz and Wang 2021
;
Lenon et al. 2021
;
Favata et al. 2022
;
Pal and Nayak 2023
;
Ravichandran et al. 2023
;
Phukon et al. 2024
;
Khalouei et al. 2025
;
Wang and Nitz 2025
;
Canizares et al. 2026
.
Similarly, analyzing data containing an eccentric signal with QC templates can lead to biases in the inferred binary parameters (e.g., masses and spins)
Favata 2014
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2020
;
O’Shea and Kumar 2023
;
Cho 2022
;
Gil Choi et al. 2024
;
Das et al. 2024
;
Divyajyoti et al. 2024
;
Divyajyoti et al. 2025
;
RoyChowdhury et al. 2026
;
Yang et al. 2026
, as well as in the evidence for gravitational lensing
Ezquiaga et al. 2021
;
Mishra and Ganguly 2025
, and in tests of general relativity
Saini et al. 2022
;
Saini et al. 2024
;
Narayan et al. 2023
;
Gupta et al. 2024
;
Shaikh et al. 2024
;
Bhat et al. 2023
;
Bhat et al. 2024
;
Roy and Janquart 2025
;
Mahapatra et al. 2025
;
Gupta et al. 2025
;
Wang and Shao 2026
.

Several GW events have been identified as potential eccentric candidates;
however, their interpretation remains debated, as different analyses often yield conflicting conclusions.
Some interesting candidates include:
1) GW190701_203306
Gupte et al. 2024
;
Planas et al. 2025a
, GW200129_065458
Gupte et al. 2024
;
Planas et al. 2025a
, and GW231223_032836
Xu et al. 2025
;
Gupte et al. 2026
, which have support for the eccentric binary black-hole (BBH) hypothesis but is dependent on the specific glitch mitigation method
Gupte et al. 2024
;
Gupte et al. 2026
;
2) GW230712_090405
Xu et al. 2025
, whose support for the eccentric aligned-spin BBH hypothesis reduces significantly when compared to the QC precessing-spin hypothesis, thus pointing toward the known degeneracy between eccentricity and spin precession
Calderón Bustillo et al. 2021
;
Romero-Shaw et al. 2023
;
Xu and Hamilton 2023
;
Divyajyoti et al. 2024
;
Divyajyoti et al. 2025
;
3) GW200208_222617, associated with an eccentric BBH signal by several studies
Romero-Shaw et al. 2022
;
Gupte et al. 2024
;
Romero-Shaw et al. 2025a
;
McMillin et al. 2025
;
Planas et al. 2025a
, but with high false alarm rate;
4) GW190521_030229, for which some studies find evidence supporting eccentric
Romero-Shaw et al. 2020
;
Gayathri et al. 2022
or dynamical-capture
Gamba et al. 2023a
;
Lange et al. 2026
BBH hypotheses, while others find no conclusive evidence
Gupte et al. 2024
;
Iglesias et al. 2022
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2023a
;
and 5) GW200105_162426, a signal from a neutron-star–black-hole (NSBH) binary with evidence for eccentricity
Fei and Yang 2024
;
Morras et al. 2025a
;
Phukon et al. 2025
;
Tiwari et al. 2025
, for which there is a contrasting set of results reported by different follow-up analyses
Planas et al. 2025b
;
Kacanja et al. 2025
;
Jan et al. 2025
;
Clarke et al. 2026
.
The astrophysical implications of these potential eccentric detections have been discussed, e.g., in Refs.
Romero-Shaw et al. 2025b
;
Morras et al. 2026a
;
Gupte et al. 2026
;
Zeeshan et al. 2026
;
Malagon and O’Shaughnessy 2026
.

Disagreements concerning the presence of eccentricity in GW events highlight the need to develop
accurate
waveform models (and better eccentric parameter-estimation techniques).
Numerical relativity (NR) provides the most accurate eccentric GW templates by directly solving Einstein’s field equations
Hinder et al. 2010
;
Mroue et al. 2010
;
Lewis et al. 2017
;
Hinder et al. 2018
;
Huerta et al. 2019
;
Habib and Huerta 2019
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2020
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2022a
;
Healy and Lousto 2022
;
Ferguson et al. 2025
;
Bonino et al. 2024
;
Trenado et al. 2025
.
However, producing enough NR simulations to satisfy the demand required by GW analyses is a highly expensive computational task, which is further complicated by the two additional parameters needed to characterize an eccentric binary
Clarke et al. 2022
;
Carullo et al. 2024
;
Carullo 2024
;
Nee et al. 2025a
;
Wang et al. 2023a
;
Knapp et al. 2024
.
Only recently, enough NR simulations have been produced to allow for the construction of NR surrogate waveform models for eccentric nonspinning BBHs
Islam et al. 2021
;
Nee et al. 2025b
;
Ravichandran et al. 2026
.
Nevertheless, it remains crucial to complement NR simulations with semi-analytical waveform models.

Different approaches have been taken to develop eccentric waveform models for comparable-mass binaries.
Using post-Newtonian (PN) results for eccentric binaries
Damour and Deruelle 1985
;
Damour and Schaefer 1988
;
Schäfer and Wex 1993
;
Wex 1995
;
Memmesheimer et al. 2004
;
Cho et al. 2022
;
Junker and Schäfer 1992
;
Gopakumar and Iyer 1997
;
Gopakumar and Iyer 2002
;
Damour et al. 2004
;
Konigsdorffer and Gopakumar 2006
;
Arun et al. 2008a
;
Arun et al. 2008b
;
Arun et al. 2009
;
Mishra et al. 2015
;
Boetzel et al. 2019
;
Ebersold et al. 2019
;
Henry and Khalil 2023
;
Boetzel et al. 2017
;
Paul and Mishra 2023
;
Bhattacharyya and Sridhar 2025
;
Morras 2025
, Refs.
Yunes et al. 2009
;
Cornish and Shapiro Key 2010
;
Shapiro Key and Cornish 2011
;
Huerta et al. 2014
;
Loutrel and Yunes 2017
;
Tanay et al. 2016
;
Tanay et al. 2019
;
Tiwari and Gopakumar 2020
;
Tiwari et al. 2019
;
Moore et al. 2018
;
Moore and Yunes 2019
;
Sridhar et al. 2024
;
Phurailatpam et al. 2025
built inspiral-only eccentric models, including spin-precession effects
Klein et al. 2018
;
Klein 2021
;
Arredondo et al. 2024
;
Morras et al. 2025b
;
Morras et al. 2026b
.
Based on such results and information from the merger-ringdown of QC NR simulations, Refs.
Huerta et al. 2017
;
Huerta et al. 2018
;
Hinder et al. 2018
;
Islam 2024a
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2020
;
Chattaraj et al. 2022
;
Manna et al. 2024
;
Paul et al. 2024
constructed hybrid inspiral-merger-ringdown (IMR) eccentric models.
Alternative methods use empirical properties of NR waveforms to construct eccentric templates from existing QC models
Setyawati and Ohme 2021
;
Wang et al. 2023b
;
Islam 2024b
;
Islam et al. 2024
;
Islam and Venumadhav 2024
;
Islam et al. 2025a
;
Islam et al. 2025b
;
Islam et al. 2026
.
Computationally efficient eccentric waveform approximants have been constructed within the
IMRPhenom
family of models
Planas et al. 2025c
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2026
.
Work on small-mass-ratio eccentric waveform modeling has been done, e.g., in Refs.
Hinderer and Flanagan 2008
;
Hughes et al. 2021
;
Speri et al. 2023
;
Chapman-Bird et al. 2025
;
Becker et al. 2025
.

Another approach to build eccentric waveforms is based on the effective-one-body (EOB) formalism
Buonanno and Damour 1999
;
Buonanno and Damour 2000
;
Damour et al. 2000
;
Damour 2001
;
Buonanno et al. 2006
;
Buonanno et al. 2007
.
EOB models combine analytical results of the two-body problem [from the
PN, post-Minkowskian, and gravitational self-force (GSF) approximations] with calibrations to NR simulations to produce highly accurate waveforms.
Two families of EOB models exist in the literature,

SEOBNR
(e.g., Refs.
Bohé et al. 2017
;
Cotesta et al. 2018
;
Ossokine et al. 2020
;
Cotesta et al. 2020
;
Mihaylov et al. 2021
;
Pompili et al. 2023
;
Khalil et al. 2023
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2023b
;
van de Meent et al. 2023
;
Mihaylov et al. 2023
;
Julié et al. 2025
;
Pompili et al. 2024
;
Buonanno et al. 2024
;
Haberland et al. 2025
;
Leather et al. 2025
;
Pompili et al. 2025
;
Estellés et al. 2025
;
Nishimura et al. 2026
;
Pompili 2025
;
Haberland and Buonanno 2026
;
Foo et al. 2026
;
Estelles et al. 2026
)
and
TEOBResumS
(e.g., Refs.
Nagar et al. 2018
;
Nagar et al. 2020a
;
Nagar et al. 2020b
;
Gamba et al. 2022
;
Riemenschneider et al. 2021
;
Albertini et al. 2022
;
Gamba et al. 2023b
),
and both have been extended to model eccentric binaries (independent efforts have been made in Refs.
Hinderer and Babak 2017
;
Manzini and Babak 2025
).
Particularly, the
TEOBResumS
family has done studies to improve eccentric waveforms both in the comparable-mass and test-mass limits
Chiaramello and Nagar 2020
;
Albanesi et al. 2021
;
Albanesi et al. 2022a
;
Albanesi et al. 2022b
;
Albanesi et al. 2023
;
Nagar et al. 2021a
;
Nagar et al. 2021b
;
Nagar and Rettegno 2021
;
Placidi et al. 2022
;
Nagar et al. 2023
;
Placidi et al. 2023
;
Albertini et al. 2024
;
Andrade et al. 2024
;
Nagar et al. 2024
;
Nagar et al. 2025
;
Grilli et al. 2024
;
Albanesi et al. 2025a
;
Chiaramello and Gamba 2025
;
Fontbuté et al. 2025
;
Albanesi et al. 2026
;
Gamba et al. 2026
, including extensions to spin-precession effects for BBHs
Gamba et al. 2024
, for NSs
Gonzalez et al. 2025
;
Albanesi et al. 2025b
, and in parametrized deviations of general relativity
Chiaramello et al. 2025
.
The model encapsulating all these developments is collectively known as
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.

For the
SEOBNR
family, past eccentric models include
SEOBNRE

Cao and Han 2017
;
Liu et al. 2020
;
Liu et al. 2022
;
Liu et al. 2023a
(extended to spin-precessing BBHs
Liu et al. 2023b
),
SEOBNRv4EHM

Khalil et al. 2021
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2022b
, and
SEOBNRv5EHM

Gamboa et al. 2025a
;
Gamboa et al. 2025b
(extended to a machine-learning surrogate model
Shi et al. 2025
).
Additional test-mass-limit studies have been done in Refs.
Faggioli et al. 2025a
;
Islam et al. 2025c
;
Islam et al. 2025d
;
Faggioli et al. 2025b
;
Faggioli et al. 2026
.

SEOBNRv5EHM
models aligned-spin BBHs on
bound
eccentric orbits, and is about an order of magnitude more accurate than
SEOBNRv4EHM
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
In the large eccentricity limit, however, the performance of these models degrades, with mismatches close to or above
20\%
for highly eccentric, long NR waveforms.
Extending the applicability of
SEOBNRv5EHM
to generic planar orbits and improving its performance across parameter space motivated the development of a new model.

By leveraging novel results for the EOB radiation-reaction (RR) force and waveform modes, we develop
SEOBNRv6EHM
,
a new time-domain, multipolar waveform model that is capable of modeling the dynamics and GW emission of BBHs on generic planar orbits (see Fig.
1
).
IMR, dynamical-capture, and scattering signals are modeled with the multipoles
(\ell,|m|)\in\{(2,2),\,(3,3),\,(2,1),\,(4,4),\,(3,2),\,(4,3)\}
, with respect to a
-2
spin-weighted spherical harmonic decomposition.

SEOBNRv6EHM
is calibrated only using QC simulations from the
SXS
catalog
Scheel et al. 2025
, and follows the same methods for constructing IMR waveforms as
SEOBNRv5EHM
, including a QC prescription for the merger-ringdown phase.

SEOBNRv6EHM
has improved accuracy, particularly for high eccentricities.
We quantify the accuracy of
SEOBNRv6EHM
by comparing its predictions against waveforms and scattering angles extracted from
SXS
NR simulations.
For QC and low-eccentricity binaries,
SEOBNRv6EHM
shows the same overall accuracy as
SEOBNRv5EHM
, but removes high-mismatch outliers that are present for
SEOBNRv5
models.
For bound eccentric binaries,
SEOBNRv6EHM
is the
first and only
model whose mismatches stay below
\sim 2\%
for
all
eccentricity values when comparing against 319 eccentric NR waveforms.
This represents an order of magnitude improvement in accuracy for the highest eccentricities with respect to
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
For all eccentricities,
SEOBNRv6EHM
is overall an order of magnitude more accurate than
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
For unbound binaries and a dynamical capture, the waveforms and scattering angles predicted by
SEOBNRv6EHM
are in better agreement with NR results than those of
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.

SEOBNRv6EHM
has a better computational performance and has no inherent parameter space restrictions.
Thanks to code optimizations and a simpler analytical structure,
SEOBNRv6EHM
approaches the speed of
SEOBNRv5HM
in the QC-orbit limit, and achieves faster waveform-evaluation walltimes by factors of
\sim 2-6
with respect to
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
, depending on the binary configuration.
In Bayesian parameter estimation, this translates into an overall speedup of approximately a factor of three relative to
SEOBNRv5EHM
, as shown in the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
.
Moreover,
SEOBNRv6EHM
introduces a new set of equations of motion that substantially enlarges the accessible parameter space with respect to
SEOBNRv5EHM
.

EOB dynamics (left panels) and the associated GW emission (right panels), both in geometric units, for different orbital configurations of a BBH system with mass ratio
q=1
, dimensionless spin components
\chi_{1}=0.3
and
\chi_{2}=-0.1
, and inclination
\iota=0
, as predicted by
SEOBNRv6EHM
.

M\Omega=0.017
.
The second row depicts an eccentric binary initialized at an orbit-averaged orbital frequency of
\langle M\Omega\rangle=0.015
, eccentricity
e=0.4
, and relativistic anomaly
\zeta=0
.
The third row shows a dynamical capture with initial energy
E/M=1.000001
, total angular momentum
J/M^{2}=1.225
, and separation
r/M=500
.
Finally, the fourth row displays a scattering encounter with initial energy
E/M=1.001
, total angular momentum

, and separation

.

The structure of this work is as follows.
In Sec.
II
, we provide an overview of
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
In Sec.
III
, we derive a new resummation of the RR force.
Prescriptions for initial conditions are given in Sec.
IV
.
The construction of multipolar waveforms of BBHs on generic planar orbits is presented in Sec.
V
.
The calibration to QC NR waveforms is explained in Sec.
VI
.
The accuracy, robustness, and computational speed of
SEOBNRv6EHM
are assessed in Sec.
VII
.
We conclude in Sec.
VIII
and discuss future work.
We leave secondary results to appendices:
Appendix
A
provides expressions for various quantities derived in this work, and Appendix
B
presents the fits employed for the calibration parameters of
SEOBNRv6EHM
.

Notation

We use geometric units in which Newton’s constant and the speed of light equal one,
G\!=\!c\!=\!1
.
In PN expansions, the
n
PN order refers to terms of order
v^{2n}\epsilon^{2n}
, where
v
is the binary’s relative velocity, and
\epsilon=1
is a power-counting parameter.

The component masses are labeled as
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
such that
m_{1}\geq\nolinebreak m_{2}
, and we define the following combinations:

\begin{gathered}M\equiv m_{1}+m_{2},\qquad\mu\equiv\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{M},\qquad\nu\equiv\frac{\mu}{M},\\
\delta\equiv\frac{m_{1}-m_{2}}{M},\qquad q\equiv\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}},\qquad X_{i}=\frac{m_{i}}{M},\quad i\in\{1,2\}.\end{gathered}

(1)

The total energy of the binary is denoted as
E
, from which we define the relative Lorentz factor
\gamma
as

\gamma\equiv\frac{E^{2}-m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}}{2\hskip 0.5ptm_{1}\hskip 0.5ptm_{2}}=1+\frac{E^{2}/M^{2}-1}{2\,\nu}.

(2)

The orbital angular momentum vector of the binary is denoted as
\bm{L}
, with magnitude
L
and direction
\bm{l}=\bm{L}/L
.

The impact parameter for unbound binaries is defined as

\frac{b}{M}\equiv\frac{L/(\hskip 0.5pt\mu\hskip 0.5ptM)\,\,E/M}{\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}}.

(3)

The total angular momentum vector of the binary is

\bm{J}\equiv\bm{L}+\bm{S}_{1}+\bm{S}_{2}.

(4)

where
\bm{S}_{1}
and
\bm{S}_{2}
are the component spin vectors, with magnitudes
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, respectively.

We work with aligned-spin binaries whose spins
\bm{S}_{1}
and
\bm{S}_{2}
are always parallel to
\bm{L}
.
Under this assumption, we define the dimensionless spin components as

\chi_{i}\equiv\frac{\bm{S}_{i}\cdot\bm{l}}{m_{i}^{2}}=\pm\frac{S_{i}}{m_{i}^{2}}\equiv\frac{a_{i}}{m_{i}}\hskip 0.5pt,\qquad i\in\{1,2\},

(5)

which take values in the range
(-1,1)
, and where positive spins point to the same direction as
\bm{L}
.

We define the following combinations of the spins:

\begin{gathered}\chi_{\text{S}}\equiv\frac{\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}}{2},\qquad\chi_{\text{A}}\equiv\frac{\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}}{2},\\
\chi_{\mathrm{eff}}\equiv\frac{m_{1}\,\chi_{1}+m_{2}\,\chi_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}},\quad a_{\pm}\equiv a_{1}\pm a_{2}=m_{1}\,\chi_{1}\pm m_{2}\,\chi_{2}\hskip 0.5pt.\end{gathered}

(6)

We work in the binary’s center of mass, and denote the relative position and momentum vectors as
\bm{r}
and
\bm{p}
, with

p^{2}=p_{r}^{2}+\frac{L^{2}}{r^{2}},\quad p_{r}=\bm{n}\cdot\bm{p},\quad\bm{L}=\bm{r}\times\bm{p},

(7)

where
\bm{n}\equiv\bm{r}/r
.
The unit vector perpendicular to
\bm{l}
and
\bm{n}
is denoted
\bm{\lambda}\equiv\bm{l}\times\bm{n}
.
The aligned-spin hypothesis allows us to restrict the motion to equatorial orbits.
Therefore, we use polar-coordinates phase-space variables
(r,\phi,p_{r},p_{\phi})
, where the angular momentum reduces to
L=p_{\phi}
.

We denote the far-zone energy and angular momentum fluxes by
\Phi_{E}
and
\Phi_{J}
, respectively, and the RR force by
\bm{\mathcal{F}}
, with radial and azimuthal components,
\mathcal{F}_{r}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}
, such that

\bm{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{F}_{r}\,\bm{n}+\frac{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}{r}\hskip 0.5pt\bm{\lambda}.

(8)

In Appendix
A
and the Supplemental Material, we use dimensionless quantities to simplify notation.
To restore units, one can use the following replacements:

\begin{gathered}t\to\frac{t}{M},\quad r\to\frac{r}{M},\quad p\to\frac{p}{\mu},\quad p_{r}\to\frac{p_{r}}{\mu},\\
p_{\phi}\to\frac{p_{\phi}}{\mu M},\quad\dot{p}_{r}\to\frac{\dot{p}_{r}}{\nu},\quad E\to\frac{E}{\mu},\quad\Omega\to M\Omega,\\
\mathcal{F}_{r}\to\frac{\mathcal{F}_{r}}{\nu},\quad\mathcal{F}_{\phi}\to\frac{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}{\nu M},\quad\Phi_{E}\to\frac{\Phi_{E}}{\nu},\quad\Phi_{J}\to\frac{\Phi_{J}}{\nu M}.\end{gathered}

(9)

II
Overview of the
SEOBNRv6EHM
model

Waveform generation in EOB models proceeds in two main steps.
First, the binary dynamics is computed using the EOB formalism.
Then, the waveform is constructed from analytical expressions evaluated on the dynamics and, for coalescing binaries, it is smoothly stitched to a phenomenological merger-ringdown ansatz.
Here, we summarize how these steps are done within the
SEOBNRv6EHM
model.

II.1
EOB dynamics

The EOB formalism
Buonanno and Damour 1999
;
Buonanno and Damour 2000
;
Damour et al. 2000
;
Damour 2001
;
Buonanno et al. 2006
;
Buonanno et al. 2007
is an analytical approach that maps the gravitational two-body dynamics onto the
effective dynamics
of a test mass or a test spin in a deformed Schwarzschild or Kerr background, with the deformation being parametrized by the symmetric mass ratio
\nu
and the spins of the binary
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
.
The effective Hamiltonian
H_{\text{eff}}
governing the dynamics of the effective particle is related to the two-body Hamiltonian
H_{\text{EOB}}
via the energy map

H_{\text{EOB}}=M\sqrt{1+2\nu\left(\frac{H_{\text{eff}}}{\mu}-1\right)}\,.

(10)

We use the aligned-spin effective Hamiltonian derived in Ref.
Khalil et al. 2023
, which was employed in the
SEOBNRv5HM

Pompili et al. 2023
and
SEOBNRv5EHM

Gamboa et al. 2025a
;
Gamboa et al. 2025b
waveform models.
This Hamiltonian is applicable to binaries on generic planar orbits, and reduces exactly to the Hamiltonian of a test mass in a Kerr background in the limit
\nu\to 0
.
It resums the full 4PN information and most of the 5PN nonspinning contributions, as detailed in Ref.
Khalil et al. 2023
.
Moreover, its accuracy is improved by including two parameters,
a_{6}
and
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}
, at high PN orders, which are calibrated against QC NR simulations (see Sec.
VI
).

The back-reaction of GW emission on the binary dynamics is modeled by incorporating a RR force into Hamilton’s equations of motion.
This RR force is derived from
balance laws
that relate the GW fluxes of energy and angular momentum to the corresponding losses of the system.
To improve its accuracy, the RR force in EOB models is
resummed
using various physically and numerically motivated techniques.

In
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we use a new expression of the RR force, whose azimuthal
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}
and radial
\mathcal{F}_{r}
components are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{modes}}\,\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}},

(11a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{r}}{p_{\phi}}\ \mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{modes}}\,\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}},

(11b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{modes}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{M}{8\pi}\left(P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]^{4/3}+\frac{3M}{4\hskip 0.5ptr}\dot{r}^{2}\right)\sum_{\ell=2}^{8}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}m^{2}\frac{\big|\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}\big|^{2}}{(M\Omega)^{1/3}}\!,

(11c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{modes}}

\displaystyle=\frac{M}{8\pi}\left(\frac{2}{3}\,P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]^{4/3}+\frac{M}{2\hskip 0.5ptr}\dot{r}^{2}\right)\sum_{\ell=2}^{8}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}m^{2}\frac{\big|\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}\big|^{2}}{(M\Omega)^{1/3}}\!,

(11d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+\exp\left[\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}\left(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\right)\right]},

(11e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+\exp\left[\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}\left(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\right)\right]}.

(11f)

In the following, we summarize and motivate each element that enters into these equations.

The RR force (
11
) employs the EOB dynamical variables
(r,\phi,p_{r_{*}},p_{\phi})
, along with their time derivatives
\dot{r}
,
\dot{\phi}\equiv\Omega
, and
\dot{p}_{r_{*}}
, where
p_{r_{*}}
is the radial momentum conjugate to the tortoise coordinate
r_{*}
defined by
\mathrm{d}r/\mathrm{d}r_{*}\equiv\xi(r)
, where
\xi(r)
is given by Eq. (44) of Ref.
Khalil et al. 2023
, and it is related to the canonical radial momentum
p_{r}
through the relation
p_{r_{*}}\!=p_{r}\hskip 0.5pt\hskip 0.5pt\xi(r)
.
The tortoise radial momentum
p_{r_{*}}
is employed since it improves the stability of the dynamics near the event horizon
Damour and Nagar 2007
;
Pan et al. 2010
.
The conservative part of
\dot{p}_{r_{*}}
is also used, and it is given by

\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\equiv-\xi(r)\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial r}(r,p_{r_{*}},p_{\phi}).

(12)

The RR force (
11
) is constructed to mimic the structure of the expressions employed in QC EOB models, while keeping the correct eccentricity contributions up to a given PN order.
This motivates the use of
\dot{r}
and
\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}
in the parametrization of the eccentricity contributions, since they satisfy
\dot{r}\to 0
and
\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\to 0
in the circular-orbit limit (using the conservative part of
\dot{p}_{r_{*}}
is convenient since it is directly given by the Hamiltonian).
Additionally, we introduce the orbital frequency for circular orbits,
\Omega_{\text{c}}
, whose PN expansion in terms of the relative separation
r
is given by [see Eq. (
128
)],

\Omega_{\text{c}}=\frac{M^{1/2}}{r^{3/2}}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\frac{M^{3/2}}{r^{5/2}}\frac{\nu}{2}+\mathcal{O}\hskip 0.5pt\hskip 0.5pt\bigg(\frac{1}{c^{3}}\bigg),

(13)

to which we apply a
(0,5)
Padé resummation,
P^{0}_{5}[\Omega_{\text{c}}]
, to improve the agreement with the true orbital frequency
\Omega=\dot{\phi}
when applied to QC binaries.
With this parametrization, the terms inside the parentheses in Eqs. (
11c
) and (
11d
) lead to the correct leading-order contribution to the RR force for generic planar orbits, in a specific gauge which we set based on the numerical behavior of the RR force in the high-velocity regime.

The eccentricity corrections
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}
ensure the correct PN expansion of the RR force for generic planar orbits beyond the leading order, and are resummed with sigmoids in Eqs. (
11e
) and (
11f
) to limit their growth during strong-field, high-velocity configurations, such as the plunge of coalescing binaries.
In
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we employ the 1PN part of
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}
, which can be read from Eqs. (
125
).

The remaining element in the RR force (
11
) is the dimensionless luminosity distance
\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}\equiv d_{\mathcal{L}}/M
multiplying the factorized waveform modes for QC orbits,
h_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}
, given by

h_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}=h_{\ell m}^{\text{N,\,qc}}\hskip 0.5pt\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}\,T_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}\,f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}\,\mathrm{e}^{i\delta_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}},

(14)

where each of these factors is defined in Sec.
V.1
, and they depend on the binary dynamics through the quantities
(\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\Omega,H_{\text{EOB}},p_{\phi})
.
This is a resummation of the modes widely employed in EOB models.
In
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we introduce additional parameters in the
f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
factors.
Some of these are calibrated against a nonspinning, equal-mass QC NR simulation, while others approximate the GSF contributions incorporated in the
SEOBNRv5HM
model
van de Meent et al. 2023
;
Leather et al. 2025
(see Sec.
VI
).
We note that the modes in Eq. (
14
) are
only
used in the computation of the RR force.
The modes returned by
SEOBNRv6EHM
are explained in the next subsection.

The Hamiltonian (
10
) and RR force (
11
) determine the binary’s evolution through Hamilton’s equations of motion

\displaystyle\dot{r}

\displaystyle=\xi(r)\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial p_{r_{*}}}(r,p_{r_{*}},p_{\phi}),

(15a)

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial p_{\phi}}(r,p_{r_{*}},p_{\phi}),

(15b)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{r_{*}}

\displaystyle=-\xi(r)\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial r}(r,p_{r_{*}},p_{\phi})+\xi(r)\,\mathcal{F}_{r},

(15c)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\phi},

(15d)

These equations are integrated numerically from a set of initial conditions
(r_{0},\phi_{0},p_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt0},p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt0})
which is determined from the binary’s intrinsic parameters (see Sec.
IV
).
These are the masses
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
, the dimensionless spin components
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
in the
z
-direction, as well as three quantities uniquely characterizing the orbit, summarized in Table
1
.

For bound binaries, we parametrize the orbit with the dimensionless orbit-averaged orbital angular frequency
\langle M\Omega\rangle
, and two eccentric parameters,
e
and
\zeta
, defined within a
Keplerian parametrization
of the orbit
Darwin 1959
,
1
1

1

In the small-mass-ratio literature, the parametrization (
16
) is known as the
quasi-Keplerian
parametrization (e.g., see Ref.
Lynch and Burke 2025
).
However, in the PN literature, “quasi-Keplerian” is a name associated with another parametrization for eccentric orbits, in which different definitions of eccentricity appear (for example, the
time eccentricity
,
e_{t}
)
Damour and Deruelle 1985
;
Damour and Schaefer 1988
;
Schäfer and Wex 1993
;
Wex 1995
;
Memmesheimer et al. 2004
;
Cho et al. 2022
.

Darwin variables
is another name for the parameters entering Eq. (
16
).

\frac{r}{M}=\frac{p}{1+e\cos\zeta},

(16)

where
e
is the
Keplerian eccentricity
,
\zeta
is a radial phase parameter (known as the
relativistic anomaly
, which reduces to the
true anomaly
in the Newtonian limit), and
p
is the semi-latus rectum which can be obtained from
(\langle M\Omega\rangle,e,\zeta)

Gamboa et al. 2025a
.

For generic planar orbits (including scattering encounters and dynamical captures), we instead use the initial separation
r
, energy
E/M
, and total angular momentum
J/M^{2}
, given by

\displaystyle E/M

\displaystyle=H_{\text{EOB}}/M,

(17)

\displaystyle J/M^{2}

Input parameters of the
SEOBNRv6EHM

II.2
EOB waveforms

The plus and cross polarizations of GWs,
h_{+}
and
h_{\times}
, are modeled through the
waveform modes

h_{\ell m}
, which are the expansion coefficients of a decomposition in terms of
-2
spin-weighted spherical harmonics,
{}_{-2}Y_{\ell,\hskip 0.5ptm}
.
Specifically,

h_{+}-i\hskip 0.5pth_{\times}=\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=2}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\!{}_{-2}Y_{\ell,\hskip 0.5ptm}(\iota,\hskip 0.5pt\varphi)\,h_{\ell m},

(19)

where
\iota
and
\varphi
are the inclination and azimuthal angles, respectively, of the line of sight measured in the source frame.

In
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we decompose the waveform modes as

\displaystyle h_{\ell m}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h_{\ell m}^{\text{F}},&\text{unbound orbits},\\
h_{\ell m}^{\text{insp-plunge}}=N_{\ell m}\,h_{\ell m}^{\text{F}},&\text{bound orbits, }t\leq t_{\text{match}},\\
h_{\ell m}^{\text{merger-RD}}\hskip-23.5pt,&\text{bound orbits, }t\geq t_{\text{match}},\end{array}\right.

(20)

\displaystyle(\ell,|m|)=\{(2,2),(3,3),(2,1),(4,4),(3,2),(4,3)\},

where
h_{\ell m}^{\text{F}}
are the
factorized
modes resumming analytical PN results,
N_{\ell m}
are
nonquasicircular corrections
which enhance the late-inspiral waveform of binary coalescences using
input values
calibrated against QC NR waveforms,
h_{\ell m}^{\text{merger-RD }}
is the merger-ringdown part of the waveform, modeled with a phenomenological ansatz for QC binaries which employs the least-damped quasinormal mode of the remnant BH, and
t_{\text{match }}
is the peak of the
(2,2)
mode for eccentric binaries, which inherits a calibration against QC NR simulations.
All the elements in Eq. (
20
) are described in Sec.
V
.
Here, we highlight the new factorization employed in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.

Specifically, we factorize the EOB modes as

h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{F}}=h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{F,\,hyb}}(\Omega,r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}})\,h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{ecc}}\left(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\right),

(21)

where
h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{F,\,hyb}}
is defined analogously to Eq. (
14
), but it employs a new leading-order (Newtonian) prefactor valid for generic planar orbits, and
h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{ecc}}
are eccentricity corrections that ensure the PN expansion of the eccentric modes is accurate beyond leading order.
These corrections are used in PN-expanded form up to relative-1PN order, and are given in Eq. (
126
).

The novel Newtonian prefactor is written as

h_{\ell m}^{\rm N}=\frac{\nu M}{d_{\mathcal{L}}}n_{\ell m}\,c_{\ell+\epsilon_{\ell m}}(\nu)\,\hat{h}_{\ell m}\hskip 0.5ptY_{\ell-\epsilon_{\ell m},-m}\left(\frac{\pi}{2},\phi\right),

(22)

where the definition of these factors is given in Sec.
V.1
, and we note that the
\hat{h}_{\ell m}
functions generalize the corresponding expressions employed in QC EOB models.

The factorization (
21
) allows for an accurate recovery of the QC expressions employed in
SEOBNRv5
models, while keeping the correct eccentricity contributions beyond the leading order.
As with the RR force (
11
), this is achieved thanks to the use of the parameters
\big(P^{0}_{5}[\Omega_{\text{c}}],\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\big)
.

To compute the waveforms, the factorized modes (
21
) are evaluated on the binary dynamics obtained from the numerical integration of the equations of motion (
15
).
For coalescing binaries, these modes are supplemented with the nonquasicircular corrections
N_{\ell m}
, and are smoothly stitched to a QC merger-ringdown, as indicated in Eq. (
20
).
This procedure yields highly accurate waveform modes for the entire inspiral-merger-ringdown evolution of bound binaries, or for the scattering process of unbound binaries.

III
Radiation-reaction force for binaries on generic planar orbits

The RR force plays a fundamental role in producing accurate waveform templates, as it determines the binary’s phase to which GW detectors are highly sensitive.
In this section, we motivate and derive a new resummation of the EOB RR force applicable for binaries moving on generic planar orbits.

III.1
The PN-expanded RR force

The RR force can be derived from first-principle calculations or through a set of
flux-balance
relations.
The former approach relies on direct integration of the retarded field generated by the binary
Damour and Deruelle 1981a
;
Damour and Deruelle 1981b
;
Damour 1982
;
Schaefer 1985
;
Kopeikin 1985
;
Blanchet et al. 1998
, or on matching procedures between the near-zone and wave-zone fields
Blanchet 1997
;
Jaranowski and Schaefer 1997
;
Pati and Will 2002
;
Konigsdorffer et al. 2003
;
Nissanke and Blanchet 2005
;
Itoh 2009
;
Blanchet et al. 2025
;
Blanchet et al. 2026
.
In contrast, the flux-balance method
Iyer and Will 1993
;
Iyer and Will 1995
;
Gopakumar et al. 1997
requires a consistency between the (gauge-dependent) local energy and angular momentum losses by the system,
\dot{E}_{\text{sys}}
and
\dot{J}_{\text{sys}}
, and the corresponding (gauge-invariant) GW fluxes of energy and angular momentum at infinity,
\Phi_{E}
and
\Phi_{J}
.
These quantities can be related by introducing a pair of
Schott terms
,
E_{\text{Schott}}
and
J_{\text{Schott}}
, which represent additional gauge-dependent contributions arising due to the interaction of the system with the radiation field, as in the theory of electromagnetism
Schott 1915
.

To determine the RR force, we start by writing the flux-balance equations for generic planar orbits as
Bini and Damour 2012

\displaystyle\dot{E}_{\text{sys}}+\dot{E}_{\text{Schott}}+\Phi_{E}

\displaystyle=0,

(23a)

\displaystyle\dot{J}_{\text{sys}}+\dot{J}_{\text{Schott}}+\Phi_{J}

\displaystyle=0.

(23b)

Then, from the equations of motion (
15
) we get the relations
\dot{E}_{\text{sys}}=\nolinebreak\dot{r}\,\mathcal{F}_{r}+\nolinebreak\Omega\,\mathcal{F}_{\phi}
and
\dot{J}_{\text{sys}}=\mathcal{F}_{\phi}
, which lead to

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\dot{J}_{\text{Schott}}-\Phi_{J},

(24a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\Phi_{E}-\dot{E}_{\text{Schott}}+\Omega\,(\Phi_{J}+\dot{J}_{\text{Schott}})}{\dot{r}}.

(24b)

Afterward, one substitutes expressions for the fluxes (e.g., the 3PN EOB fluxes derived in Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
), and proposes ansätze for the Schott terms whose time derivatives are obtained by substituting back the equations of motion (
15
).
The Schott terms are parametrized by arbitrary
gauge constants

\{\alpha_{i}\}
and
\{\beta_{i}\}
, such that
J_{\text{Schott}}(r,p_{r},p_{\phi},\{\alpha_{i}\})
and
E_{\text{Schott}}(r,p_{r},p_{\phi},\{\beta_{i}\})
.
In this way, an expansion in small velocities leads to a set of linear equations for
\{\alpha_{i}\}
and
\{\beta_{i}\}
, whose solution can be expressed in terms of a reduced set of independent gauge constants.
This ultimately yields generic, gauge-dependent PN expressions for the RR force components in terms of EOB coordinates.

At leading order (2.5PN order), there are two independent gauge constants, commonly denoted by
\alpha
and
\beta
, which allow the RR force components to be written as
Gamboa et al. 2025a
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\text{LO}}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{8M^{3}\nu^{2}}{5\,r^{3}}\frac{p_{\phi}}{\mu}\left[(2\alpha+1)\hskip 0.5pt\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-(\alpha+2)\hskip 0.5pt\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{\mu^{2}r^{2}}+(\alpha-2)\hskip 0.5pt\frac{M}{r}\right],

(25a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\text{LO}}_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\nu^{2}M^{3}}{15\,r^{3}}\frac{p_{r}}{\mu}\Bigg[6\hskip 0.5pt(\alpha-\beta+2)\,\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-3\hskip 0.5pt(\alpha-3\beta-1)\hskip 0.5pt\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{\mu^{2}r^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 55.0pt+(9\alpha-9\beta+17)\frac{M}{r}\Bigg].

(25b)

For later convenience, we rewrite Eqs. (
25
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\text{LO}}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{32\nu^{2}M^{2}\Omega}{5}\left[\frac{M^{2}}{r^{2}}-\frac{2\alpha+1}{4}\hskip 0.5pt\frac{M\dot{r}^{2}}{r}+\frac{\alpha+2}{4}\hskip 0.5pt\frac{M\dot{p}_{r}}{\mu}\right],

(26a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\text{LO}}_{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{32\nu^{2}M^{2}\Omega}{5}\frac{p_{r}}{p_{\phi}}\!\left[\frac{-3\alpha-10}{6}\frac{M^{2}}{r^{2}}-\frac{\alpha-\beta+2}{2}\hskip 0.5pt\frac{M\dot{r}^{2}}{r}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 73.0pt\left.+\frac{\alpha-3\beta-1}{4}\hskip 0.5pt\frac{M\dot{p}_{r}}{\mu}\right],

(26b)

where we have used the Newtonian relations,
\Omega=p_{\phi}/(\hskip 0.5pt\mu r^{2})
,
\dot{r}=\nolinebreak p_{r}/\mu
, and
p_{\phi}/(\hskip 0.5pt\mu M)=\sqrt{r/M+\dot{p}_{r}\hskip 0.5ptr^{3}/(\hskip 0.5pt\mu M^{2})}
.
The circular-orbit limit is easily obtained by taking
M^{2}/r^{2}=\nolinebreak(M\Omega)^{4/3}
,
\dot{r}=\nolinebreak 0
, and
\dot{p}_{r}=0
, leading to the well-known expression:

\mathcal{F}^{\text{LO, qc}}_{\phi}=-\frac{32\nu^{2}M(M\Omega)^{7/3}}{5}.

(27)

At higher PN orders, the RR force increases in complexity, with a growing number of independent constants.
2
2

2

In Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
, the calculation was extended up to the relative 3PN order.
At 2.5PN order one needs to take into account the back reaction of the leading order RR effects, which are associated to the constants
\alpha
and
\beta
.

However, a suitable gauge condition can be used to determine the values of these constants.
Here, we propose the condition

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{qc}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Phi_{E}^{\text{qc}}}{\Omega},

(28a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{qc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-3\alpha-10}{6}\,\frac{p_{r}}{p_{\phi}}\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{qc}},

(28b)

where “qc” denotes the QC-orbit limit of the corresponding expressions.
This condition keeps
\alpha
and
\beta
arbitrary, is consistent with the leading-order expressions (
26
), and generalizes the gauges employed in QC EOB models.
In particular, the gauge employed in
SEOBNRv5
models
Pompili et al. 2023
;
Gamboa et al. 2025b
can be recovered by setting
\alpha=-16/3
.

By using the gauge condition (
28
), the same ansätze for the Schott terms given in Eqs. (64) and (65) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
, and the energy and angular momentum fluxes derived therein,
3
3

3

These fluxes are composed of instantaneous and tail contributions.
The instantaneous terms are valid for generic planar orbits, while the tail contributions are derived in an expansion in
p_{r}
.
The original tail contributions to the fluxes are given in an orbit-averaged eccentricity expansion.
By employing a
p_{r}
-expanded ansatz in terms of EOB variables, the orbit-averaged tail contributions can be transformed into non-averaged expressions.
This transformation makes it possible to derive expressions for the RR force that depend only on EOB variables.

we obtain complete 3PN expressions of the RR force for binaries on generic planar orbits, as a function of the leading order gauge constants
\alpha
and
\beta
.
For reference, the 1PN expressions are given in Eq. (
119
) of Appendix
A
, while the complete 3PN expressions, as well as the Schott terms, are provided in the Supplemental Material.
Such expressions generalize the ones given in Eqs. (66) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
, which can be obtained by taking
\alpha=-16/3
and
\beta=-13/2
.

III.2
A new resummation of the RR force

PN-expanded expressions perform poorly at high velocities, making them insufficient for modeling the plunge or the periastron passages of binaries with moderate to high eccentricities.
For this reason, EOB models use resummations to improve the robustness and accuracy of PN expressions.
Here, we propose a new resummation of the RR force that enables accurate modeling of binaries on generic planar orbits.

III.2.1
Motivation

Standard resummations of the EOB RR force for QC binaries follow the work from Refs.
Damour and Nagar 2007
;
Damour and Nagar 2008
;
Damour et al. 2009
;
Pan et al. 2011
.
In particular, for
SEOBNRv5
models, the resummation reads
Pompili et al. 2023
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{F, qc}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Omega}{8\pi}\sum_{\ell=2}^{8}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}m^{2}\big|d_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell m}^{\text{F, qc}}\big|^{2}\!,

(29a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{F, qc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{r}}{p_{\phi}}\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{F, qc}}\!,

(29b)

where
h_{\ell m}^{\text{F, qc}}
are the factorized QC modes given by

h_{\ell m}^{\text{F, qc}}=h_{\ell m}^{\text{N,\,qc}}\,\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}\,T_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}\,f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}\,\mathrm{e}^{i\delta_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}},

(30)

with all factors taking their standard definitions (see Sec.
V.1
), most of which depend directly on
\Omega
.

Proposing eccentric RR force resummations that reduce to such standard EOB expressions for QC orbits has been the prevailing approach in the literature
Chiaramello and Nagar 2020
;
Khalil et al. 2021
;
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.
This strategy allows one to leverage the extensive developments achieved for QC binaries.
However, imposing this requirement introduces various challenges.
For example, QC resummations are given in a particular RR gauge and rely heavily on the orbital frequency
\Omega
.
As a result, it becomes difficult to accommodate the additional eccentric degrees of freedom and the RR force inherent gauge dependence.

To motivate our resummation proposal, let us first consider the generic planar-orbit expressions for the energy and angular momentum fluxes from the waveform modes
Ruiz et al. 2008
;
Lousto and Zlochower 2007

\displaystyle\Phi_{E}^{\text{gw}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=2}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\big|d_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{h}_{\ell m}(t)\big|^{2},

(31a)

\displaystyle\Phi_{J}^{\text{gw}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=2}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}m\,\Im\left[d_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{h}^{*}_{\ell m}(t)\,h_{\ell m}(t)\right],

(31b)

where the symbol
∗
denotes the complex conjugate and
\Im
the imaginary part.
Next, we decompose the modes as

h_{\ell m}=\nolinebreak A_{\ell m}\,e^{-im\phi},

(32)

with
\phi
the azimuthal orbital phase.
Then, one obtains

\Phi_{J}^{\text{gw}}=\frac{1}{16\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=2}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\left[m^{2}\Omega-m\hskip 0.5pt\Im\left(\frac{\dot{A}_{\ell m}}{A_{\ell m}}\right)\right]\big|d_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell m}\big|^{2}.

(33)

From this relation, it is straightforward to see that Eq. (
29a
) is an incomplete representation of the eccentric RR force.
Indeed, for QC, aligned-spin binaries, one has
h_{\ell\,-m}=\nolinebreak(-1)^{\ell}h_{\ell m}^{*}
and
A_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}=A_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}(\Omega)
, so
\Im(\dot{A}_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}/A_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}})=\nolinebreak\mathcal{O}(1/c^{10})
.
4
4

4

Imaginary terms enter the dominant amplitude
A_{22}^{\text{qc}}
at 2.5PN order;
combined with the 2.5PN leading-order contribution to
\dot{\Omega}
, they produce a 5PN correction [e.g., see Eqs. (5.3b) and (6.17) of Ref.
Blanchet et al. 2023
].

Thus,

\Phi_{J}^{\text{qc}}\approx\frac{\Omega}{8\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=2}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}m^{2}\,\big|d_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}\big|^{2},

(34)

as employed in the QC RR force (
29a
), with
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{qc}}=-\Phi_{J}^{\text{qc}}
.
In contrast, for eccentric binaries, the term
\Im(\dot{A}_{\ell m}/A_{\ell m})
has contributions at leading order, thereby making Eq. (
34
) an incomplete formula for the eccentric angular momentum flux.
Additionally, the Schott terms encoding the gauge-dependent nature of the eccentric RR force are also missing in Eq. (
29a
).

To motivate a RR force resummation that accounts for this information, we first write down an alternative expression for Eq. (
33
).
Assuming that
A_{\ell m}=\nolinebreak A_{\ell m}(r,p_{r},p_{\phi})
, we obtain

\dot{A}_{\ell m}=\frac{\partial A_{\ell m}}{\partial r}\,\dot{r}+\frac{\partial A_{\ell m}}{\partial p_{r}}\,\dot{p}_{r}+\frac{\partial A_{\ell m}}{\partial p_{\phi}}\,\dot{p}_{\phi}.

(35)

Then, substituting this relation in Eq. (
33
), we get

\displaystyle\Phi_{J}^{\text{gw}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi}\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=2}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\left\{m^{2}\Omega-\Im\left[\frac{\partial\ln(A_{\ell m}^{m})}{\partial r}\right]\dot{r}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\,\Im\left[\frac{\partial\ln(A_{\ell m}^{m})}{\partial p_{r}}\right]\dot{p}_{r}-\Im\left[\frac{\partial\ln(A_{\ell m}^{m})}{\partial p_{\phi}}\right]\dot{p}_{\phi}\right\}\big|d_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell m}\big|^{2}.

(36)

Next, we focus on the leading order piece of Eq. (
III.2.1
).
We take
\ell=2
,
m=\pm 2
, and neglect the dissipative part of
\dot{p}_{r}
and
\dot{p}_{\phi}
,

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{r}

\displaystyle\approx\dot{p}_{r,\,\text{cons}}=-\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial r},

(37a)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{\phi}

\displaystyle\approx 0.

(37b)

After some rearrangements, this leads to

\displaystyle\Phi_{J}^{\text{gw}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{M}{8\pi\hskip 0.5pt(M\Omega)^{1/3}}\left\{(M\Omega)^{4/3}-\frac{M^{4/3}\Omega^{1/3}}{4}\Im\left[\frac{\partial\ln(A_{22}^{2})}{\partial r}\right]\dot{r}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\,\frac{M^{4/3}\Omega^{1/3}}{4}\Im\left[\frac{\partial\ln(A_{22}^{2})}{\partial p_{r}}\right]\dot{p}_{r,\,\text{cons}}\right\}\,2^{2}\,\big|\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{22}\big|^{2},

(38)

where
\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}=d_{\mathcal{L}}/M
is the dimensionless luminosity distance.

Now, we make a comparison with Eq. (
26a
), which also contains terms proportional to
\dot{p}_{r}
and
\dot{r}
.
5
5

5

Note that we do not aim to establish a relation between the
A_{22}
terms in Eq. (
III.2.1
) and the terms in Eq. (
26a
).
Rather, we are motivating the structure of a factorization that encodes the leading order RR force and has a similar form as the standard QC factorization shown in Eq. (
29a
).

The main differences
in the structure
of these equations are:

1)

the presence of the
\big|\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}\,h_{22}\big|^{2}
factor in Eq. (
III.2.1
), and

2)

the absence of a
M^{2}/r^{2}
term in Eq. (
III.2.1
).

The first difference can be addressed by noting that, from the leading order contribution to
h_{22}
for QC binaries,

h_{22}^{\text{LO, qc}}=\frac{8\nu}{\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{5}}\,(M\Omega)^{2/3}\,\mathrm{e}^{-i2\phi},

(39)

we get

\frac{M\,2^{2}\,\big|\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{22}\big|^{2}}{8\pi\hskip 0.5pt(M\Omega)^{1/3}}\to\frac{32\nu^{2}M^{2}\Omega}{5},

(40)

which is the prefactor of
-\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{LO}}
from Eq. (
26a
).
This suggests using
\big|\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}\big|
in the factorization of the eccentric RR force.

As for the absence of a
M^{2}/r^{2}
term in Eq. (
III.2.1
), we need to introduce a special treatment.
For circular orbits, we would have
(M\Omega)^{4/3}=M^{2}/r^{2}
at leading order, and the connection with Eq. (
26a
) would be complete.
However, for generic planar orbits,
\Omega
and
r
are connected through the leading order relation
\Omega=p_{\phi}/(\hskip 0.5pt\mu r^{2})
, which complicates the association with Eq. (
26a
).
This motivates the introduction of a function which depends solely on
r
and whose circular-orbit limit coincides with
\Omega
.
A natural candidate is the PN-expanded expression of
\Omega
for circular orbits written as a function of
r
.
We calculate such expression up to 5PN order to include in the result the calibration parameters
a_{6}
and
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}
, which appear at 5PN and 4.5PN orders, respectively.
This results in the expression,
\Omega_{\text{c}}=\Omega_{\text{c}}(r)
, provided in Eq. (
128
) of Appendix
A
.

In this way,
\Omega_{\text{c}}
can be employed, together with the relation (
40
), to relate Eqs. (
26a
) and (
III.2.1
) at leading order via

\frac{M^{2}}{r^{2}}\to[M\Omega_{\text{c}}(r)]^{4/3}.

(41)

We emphasize that
\Omega_{\text{c}}\neq\Omega=\partial H_{\text{EOB}}/\partial p_{\phi}
for non circular orbits.
The only purpose of
\Omega_{\text{c}}
is to make a connection with the standard EOB factorizations of the RR force, which make use of the orbital frequency
\Omega
.
In practice, we employ a
(0,5)
Padé resummation,
P^{0}_{5}[\Omega_{\text{c}}]
, since it improves the agreement with the true orbital frequency
\Omega
for QC binaries and makes the model more robust.
When computing the Padé approximant, we treat
\ln r
as a constant, as done with other elements entering into the
SEOBNRv5
Hamiltonian
Khalil et al. 2023
.

III.2.2
Resummation proposal

With these results, we now write down our proposal:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{modes}}\,\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}},

(42a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{r}}{p_{\phi}}\ \mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{modes}}\,\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}},

(42b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{modes}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{M}{8\pi}\left(P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]^{4/3}-\frac{2\alpha+1}{4}\,\frac{M\dot{r}^{2}}{r}+\frac{\alpha+2}{4}\,\frac{M}{\mu}\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\right)\sum_{\ell=2}^{8}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}m^{2}\frac{\big|\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}(\Omega)\big|^{2}}{(M\Omega)^{1/3}}\!,

(42c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{modes}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{M}{8\pi}\left(\frac{-3\alpha-10}{6}\,P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]^{4/3}-\frac{\alpha-\beta+2}{2}\,\frac{M\dot{r}^{2}}{r}+\frac{\alpha-3\beta-1}{4}\,\frac{M}{\mu}\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\right)\sum_{\ell=2}^{8}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}m^{2}\frac{\big|\hat{d}_{\mathcal{L}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}(\Omega)\big|^{2}}{(M\Omega)^{1/3}}\!,

(42d)

where, as before,

\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}=-\xi(r)\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial r}(r,p_{r_{*}},p_{\phi}),

(43)

and we add the following terms to
f_{31}

(
appearing inside
h_{31}^{\text{F,\,qc}}
)

(M\Omega)^{4/3}\left[\frac{-12\nu-5}{2}\,\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}+(4\nu-5)\,\frac{\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}}{\delta}-\frac{5}{2}\,\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\right],

(44)

to have the complete QC flux at 3PN order, compared to that used in
SEOBNRv5
models
Pompili et al. 2023
[see Eq. (B3b) of Ref.
Henry et al. 2022
].

The eccentricity corrections
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}
are obtained by requiring that the PN expansion of Eqs. (
42a
) and (
42b
) matches the 3PN RR force derived in Sec.
III.1
.
The underlying QC factorization in Eqs. (
42
) ensures that these corrections satisfy
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}\to 1
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}\to 1
in the QC-orbit limit.
For later convenience, we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc, PN}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc, PN}}
the explicit PN-expanded expressions, to distinguish them from their resummed counterparts [see Eqs. (
45
)].

By construction, these expressions are consistent with the leading order RR force for generic planar orbits (
26
), since the corrections
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}
start at relative 1PN order.
In the QC orbit limit
[\Omega_{\text{c}}\to\nolinebreak\Omega+\nolinebreak\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{11}),\,\dot{r}^{2}\to\nolinebreak\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{10}),\,\dot{p}_{r}\to\nolinebreak\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{10})]
, this resummation satisfies the gauge in Eqs. (
28
), closely following the standard QC expressions (
29
).
Furthermore, using the QC modes
h_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}
without eccentricity corrections and as functions of the instantaneous frequency
\Omega
, makes the evaluation of the RR force highly efficient.

To improve the behavior of the eccentricity corrections
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}
, we find it important to parametrize them in terms of
(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}})
and to perform an additional resummation.
Without such control, previous work found significant numerical difficulties with the eccentric RR force, which led to the total (in
SEOBNRv4EHM

Ramos-Buades et al. 2022b
) or partial (in
TEOBResumS-Dalí

Nagar et al. 2021b
;
Nagar et al. 2023
, where the radial RR force component contains a QC reduction of the 2PN results from Ref.
Bini and Damour 2012
) removal of eccentricity corrections.

SEOBNRv5EHM

Gamboa et al. 2025b
keeps complete eccentricity corrections, but at the expense of introducing extra Keplerian parameters, which limit the model’s applicability to bound orbits and introduce potential issues in the equations of motion.

The parametrization
(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}})
makes the RR force (
42
) fully applicable to generic planar orbits and it enables an accurate recovery of the underlying QC factorization, since
\dot{r}
and
\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}
are very close to zero for QC inspirals.
We find that using
\Omega
or
p_{\phi}
leads to worse performance, as these variables have inherent noncircular contributions, and thus rely on PN corrections to achieve a good QC-orbit limit.
In contrast,
\dot{r}
and
\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}
are independent variables that naturally separate the eccentricity contributions from the PN orders.
We employ
\dot{r}
instead of
p_{r_{*}}
since the former remains smaller in magnitude throughout the entire evolution.
The use of
\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}
is convenient, as it is directly obtained during the integration of the equations of motion (
15
) and does not depend explicitly on the dissipative dynamics, thereby avoiding a circular (implicit) dependence on the RR force.
The 3PN-accurate transformation rules required to write all the relevant expressions in terms of
(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}})
are given in Appendix
A.1
.

The additional resummation applied to
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}
is a novel proposal that guarantees robust numerical behavior during the plunge and highly relativistic periastron passages.
As the PN order increases, the eccentric RR force becomes more intricate, involving multiple terms with different powers of three dynamical variables [e.g., see Eq. (
119
)].
This becomes problematic in high-velocity configurations, often leading to issues in the integration of the equations of motion.

Specifically, the resummation takes the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+\exp\big(\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}\big)},

(45a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+\exp\big(\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}\big)},

(45b)

where
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}
start at 1PN order (relative to the leading 2.5PN order), and are obtained from the relations given in Appendix
A.3
, which ensure that the PN expansion of Eqs. (
45
) is consistent with the expressions
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc, PN}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc, PN}}
.
The 1PN part of
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}
is shown in Eqs. (
125
) of Appendix
A
, while the full 3PN expressions are provided in the Supplemental Material.

This sigmoid resummation satisfies:

•

\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}\to 1
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}\to 1
when
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}\to 0
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}\to 0
in the Newtonian limit (low velocities),

•

\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}\to 0
or
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}\to 0
in strong periastron passages or during the plunge, if
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}\to\infty
or
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}\to\nolinebreak\infty
,

•

\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}\to 2
or
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}\to 2
in strong periastron passages or during the plunge, if
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}\to-\infty
or
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}\to\nolinebreak-\infty
,

thereby always controlling the growth of the corrections.

III.2.3
Selecting a convenient RR gauge

Figure 2:

Evolution of the functions
d_{\phi}=d_{\phi}(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}};\alpha)
(left panels) and
d_{r}=d_{r}(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}};\alpha,\beta)
(right panels), defined in Eqs. (
46
), for different values of the leading-order RR gauge constants
\alpha
and
\beta
.
Each row corresponds to a fixed value of
\alpha
, while
\beta
is varied over the interval
[-7,7]
, as indicated by the color bars.
Dashed curves indicate specific gauge choices, including the one used in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
The functions are evaluated on the dynamics of an equal-mass, nonspinning binary with initial orbit-averaged frequency
\langle M\Omega\rangle=0.0005
, eccentricity
e=0.95
, and relativistic anomaly
\zeta=\pi
, generated with the
SEOBNRv6EHM
model.
These functions
d_{\phi}
and
d_{r}
enter the denominators of the eccentricity corrections to the RR force [see Eqs. (
125
)], and are directly related to its leading-order (Newtonian) contributions [see Eqs. (
26
)].

Choosing a gauge is important for the accuracy and numerical robustness of the RR force.
In general, the gauge constants can be mapped to specific coordinate choices, and can take arbitrary values (within a certain class of coordinate systems), as explained in Refs.
Iyer and Will 1993
;
Iyer and Will 1995
;
Gopakumar et al. 1997
.
While all the gauges are physically equivalent in the PN regime (weak-field, slow-velocities), the situation is more complex during strong periastron passages or the plunge, where highly relativistic velocities are reached.
For example, Ref.
Fumagalli et al. 2025
showed that different RR gauges lead to significantly distinct features in the evolution, particularly when the eccentricity is high.
Indeed, the gauge constants determine the algebraic structure of the RR force at each PN order [e.g., see Eqs. (
119
)].
Thus, the gauge has a non-negligible impact on the evolution of the binary.

Our gauge is determined by Eqs. (
28
) together with the ansätze for the Schott terms from Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.
This leaves two free gauge constants,
\alpha
and
\beta
.
To fix them, we focus on the denominators shown in Eqs. (
125
), given by

\displaystyle d_{\phi}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2\alpha+1}{4}\,\frac{r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}}{M}+\frac{\alpha+2}{4}\,\frac{r^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu M},

(46a)

\displaystyle d_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{-3\alpha-10}{6}-\frac{\alpha-\beta+2}{2}\,\frac{r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}}{M}+\frac{\alpha-3\beta-1}{4}\,\frac{r^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu M},

(46b)

which, by construction, are associated with the leading order terms of the RR force (
26
).

In Fig.
2
, we show the evolution of
d_{\phi}
(left panels) and
d_{r}
(right panels) across various
\alpha
and
\beta
values, for a fixed highly eccentric binary dynamics.
Particularly, we focus on the gauge choice from
SEOBNRv5EHM

Gamboa et al. 2025a
(
\alpha=-16/3
,
\beta=\nolinebreak-13/2
), and a gauge that removes the factor
r^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}
from
d_{\phi}
and
d_{r}
, such that

\alpha=-2,\qquad\beta=-1.

(47)

We see that some gauge constants (including the ones from
SEOBNRv5EHM
) lead to vanishing denominators, making them unsuitable for robust evolutions.
In contrast, the values (
47
) produce a regular behavior of the denominators.
This can be inferred from Eqs. (
46
), since for these values of
\alpha
and
\beta
,
d_{\phi}
and
d_{r}
remain strictly positive and negative, respectively, for all values of
(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}})
, thereby guaranteeing nonproblematic denominators.
Furthermore, in calculating the eccentricity corrections to the RR force [e.g., Eq. (
125
)], several terms acquire a dependence on the factors
\alpha+2
and
\alpha-3\beta-1
, which vanish in the gauge (
47
).
This choice, therefore, leads to more compact expressions that are faster to evaluate.

For these reasons, we use the gauge (
47
) in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
We emphasize that this choice is based on properties of our proposed RR force resummation (
42
).
Other resummations may benefit from similar arguments to select an appropriate RR gauge.
As discussed later in Sec.
VII.2.2
, the gauge has important effects on the accuracy of the model, and thus is worth studying with more detail in future work.

III.2.4
The
SEOBNRv6EHM
RR force

The RR force in
SEOBNRv6EHM
is obtained from Eqs. (
42
) with the gauge condition (
47
), and 1PN contributions to
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}\!
, as given in Eq. (
125
).
Additionally, some of the factorized modes
h_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}
are augmented with calibration parameters, as discussed in Sec.
VI.3
.
This results in the expression (
11
) presented before.

We restrict the eccentricity corrections to 1PN order, as higher-order contributions degrade agreement with NR waveforms.
A similar issue was observed during the early development of the
SEOBNRv4EHM
model
Ramos-Buades et al. 2022b
.
We conjecture that this effect originates from the current paradigm for resumming the eccentric RR force, namely, enforcing the standard QC factorization
\propto\Omega\sum m^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\big|d_{\mathcal{L}}h_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}\big|^{2}
and supplementing it with eccentricity corrections.
Under such construction, the corrections inherit a dependence on different powers of
\Omega
, leading to increasingly intricate expressions at higher PN orders [e.g., the multiple terms proportional to
p_{\phi,\,\text{N}}^{n/3}
in Eq. (
125
)].
As a result, we noted a reduction in waveform accuracy, particularly at low eccentricities.
In contrast, we observed that the predictions for the scattering angle (presented in Sec.
VII.2.3
for the
SEOBNRv6EHM
RR force) improve as the PN order of the corrections
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}
is increased.
However, we consider a more accurate waveform model for bound binaries to have higher priority.
Thus, we restrict the eccentricity corrections to 1PN order, and leave for future work a careful study of the effects of higher PN orders on model accuracy.
Here, we emphasize that the QC part of the RR force (
42
) includes high-PN-order information that partially captures eccentric effects via its dependence on the instantaneous frequency
\Omega
.
Hence, it is not straightforward to quantify the impact of using 1PN corrections on the phasing of eccentric inspirals.

This reflects the complexity of modeling eccentric binaries and underscores the need for better resummations.
Relaxing the requirement to recover standard QC factorizations may prove advantageous in future work.
This would allow the RR force to naturally adapt to eccentric variables, eliminating the need for ad hoc elements, such as the
\Omega_{\text{c}}
function (
13
).
The sigmoid resummation (
45
) is promising for incorporating higher PN orders without compromising the robustness of the expressions.
Test-mass-limit studies could help assess different RR force prescriptions, as in Refs.
Chiaramello and Nagar 2020
;
Albanesi et al. 2021
;
Albanesi et al. 2022a
;
Albanesi et al. 2022b
;
Albanesi et al. 2023
;
Faggioli et al. 2025a
.

IV
Initial conditions for binaries on generic planar orbits

An aligned-spin binary has four degrees of freedom, as encoded in the equations of motion (
15
).
Thus, four initial values
(r_{0},\phi_{0},p_{r_{*}0},p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0})
are required to uniquely determine the binary’s evolution.
Here, we discuss how these initial conditions are computed from the input parameters given in Table
1
.
Specifically, we detail the procedures for determining initial conditions 1) for
bound orbits
, given values of the dimensionless orbit-averaged orbital frequency
\langle M\Omega\rangle
, eccentricity
e
, and relativistic anomaly
\zeta
(or mean anomaly
l\hskip 0.5pt\hskip 0.5pt
),
6
6

6

Other models use a
reference
frequency besides the
starting
frequency (e.g., see Refs.
Planas et al. 2025c
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2026
).
In
SEOBNRv6EHM
, the reference and starting frequencies are the same, but we provide the option to integrate backward the full equations of motion (
15
).
In the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
, we show the usefulness of this in parameter-estimation analyses.

and 2) for
generic planar orbits
, given values of the energy
E/M
, total angular momentum
J/M^{2}
, and separation
r
.

IV.1
Bound orbits

We determine initial conditions for bound orbits from starting values of the orbit-averaged orbital frequency
\langle M\Omega\rangle
, eccentricity
e
, and relativistic anomaly
\zeta
, following a post-adiabatic approach.
Under this approximation the conservative (adiabatic) values of the dynamical variables,
(r_{0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}},\,p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}},\,p_{r_{*}0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}})
, are computed first, followed by dissipative (post-adiabatic) corrections,
(r_{0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{diss}},\,p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{diss}},\,p_{r_{*}0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{diss}})
, due to the effect of the RR force starting at 2.5PN order.
This results in the splitting of the initial values as

\displaystyle\phi_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

(48a)

\displaystyle r_{0}

\displaystyle=r_{0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}+r_{0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{diss}},

(48b)

\displaystyle p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0}

\displaystyle=p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}+p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{diss}},

(48c)

\displaystyle p_{r_{*}0}

\displaystyle=p_{r_{*}0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}+p_{r_{*}0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{diss}},

(48d)

where we set
\phi_{0}
to zero by convention.

In
SEOBNRv6EHM
, the conservative part of the initial values is determined with PN formulas and a root solving procedure.
Specifically, the radial momentum is calculated with a 3PN-accurate relation given in Eq. (B11) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
, namely

\displaystyle p_{r_{*}0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}(\langle M\Omega\rangle,e,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle M\Omega\rangle^{1/3}\hskip 0.5pte\hskip 0.5pt\sin\zeta}{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}+\Order(1/c^{2}).

(49)

Next, the values of
r_{0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}
and
p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}
are determined by numerically finding the roots of the equations

\displaystyle\Omega(\langle M\Omega\rangle,e,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}[r,p_{r_{*}0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}},p_{\phi}]}{\partial p_{\phi}},

(50a)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{r_{*}}(\langle M\Omega\rangle,e,\zeta)

\displaystyle=-\xi(r)\,\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}[r,p_{r_{*}0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}},p_{\phi}]}{\partial r},

(50b)

where
\Omega(\langle M\Omega\rangle,e,\zeta)
and
\dot{p}_{r_{*}}(\langle M\Omega\rangle,e,\zeta)
are calculated with conservative 3PN-accurate formulas, given in Appendix B of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.
The initial guesses of the root solver are calculated with Eqs. (34b) and (34c) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.

The dissipative contributions
r_{0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{diss}}
,
p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{diss}}
, and
p_{r_{*}0,\hskip 0.5pt\text{diss}}
, are directly calculated with the 2.5PN terms given in Eqs. (40b-d) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.
These terms have a dependence on the leading-order RR gauge constants
\alpha
and
\beta
, which we set to
-2
and
-1
, respectively, as discussed in Sec.
III.2.3
.

This prescription for the initial conditions closely follows the one employed in the
SEOBNRv5EHM
model, but changes the way in which
p_{r_{*}0}
is calculated.
In
SEOBNRv5EHM
, the procedure to determine
p_{r_{*}0}
involved another root-finding procedure, and it was designed to accurately recover the method employed in the QC model
SEOBNRv5HM
.
This was important to avoid spoiling the calibration to QC NR simulations inherited from
SEOBNRv5HM
.
However, in
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we do not have such constraint, since the model has been recalibrated.
Thus, we decided to use the (simpler) prescription for the radial momentum
p_{r_{*}0}
described above, since it is less prone to errors, compared to the method used in
SEOBNRv5EHM
, which can be problematic when applied to highly eccentric binaries.

To ensure the generation of physically meaningful waveforms, we impose constraints on the allowed initial conditions.
Specifically, we prevent waveform generation if any of the following conditions are met:

1)

The initial angular momentum differs more than
1.5\%
from its PN estimate given by Eq. (34b) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.

2)

The initial separation differs more than
1\%
from its PN estimate given by Eq. (34c) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.

3)

The initial separation is less than
4M
.

These conditions were determined empirically, and they appear for very challenging configurations, e.g., when the binary is very close to merger, or when the eccentricity is very large at high frequencies (for example, see Fig.
19
in Sec.
VII.4
).
The first two conditions are also applied in
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
Additionally, such model performs a backward integration of a set of secular evolution equations
\{\dot{x},\hskip 0.5pt\dot{e},\hskip 0.5pt\dot{\zeta}\}
whenever the starting separation of the binary is less than
10M
.
This allows the determination of initial conditions at earlier (less challenging) stages, and it was necessary to avoid problems with the equations of motion
Gamboa et al. 2025b
.
However, as discussed in Sec.
VII.4
,
SEOBNRv6EHM
does not perform such secular backward integration, and instead it imposes a cutoff in waveform generation whenever the starting separation is less than
4M
, a regime in which the approximations used to construct the initial conditions are expected to be much less accurate.

Beyond the constraints on initial conditions,
SEOBNRv6EHM
imposes additional requirements to ensure high-quality waveforms.
In particular, waveform generation is aborted if the dynamics duration is less than
200M
and the final azimuthal angle is less than
\pi
(meaning that the binary has completed less than half an orbit), if the dynamics duration exceeds a threshold determined by a background QC evolution (see Sec.
V.2
), or if the separation during the evolution significantly exceeds (by a factor of
10
) a Newtonian estimate.
The first condition prevents unphysical features arising from an inaccurate merger-ringdown attachment, while the latter two discard configurations that are inconsistent with the intended physical system represented by the input values of
e
,
\langle M\Omega\rangle
, and
\zeta
.

Overall, these empirical conditions build up the robustness of
SEOBNRv6EHM
, which we discuss in Sec.
VII.4
.
We expect these conditions to change as better initial conditions and more accurate evolution equations are available.

Mean anomaly
l
as an input parameter

The default radial phase parameter in
SEOBNRv6EHM
is the relativistic anomaly
\zeta
, but we also provide the option to use the
mean anomaly

l
as an input parameter.
The mean anomaly is an angle that measures (linearly in time) the fraction of the orbital period that has elapsed since the last periastron passage
Damour and Deruelle 1985
;
Schmidt et al. 2017
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2020
;
Islam et al. 2021
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2022a
;
Shaikh et al. 2023
.
Given an input value of the mean anomaly, we employ Newtonian transformations to obtain the corresponding relativistic anomaly, which is used internally to compute EOB initial conditions as described above.
We note that this is the approach followed by the
TEOBResumS-Dalí
model
Nagar et al. 2024
.

To determine the relativistic anomaly
\zeta
from the mean anomaly
l
, first we compute the
eccentric anomaly

u
by numerically solving Kepler’s equation,

l=u-e\sin u.

(51)

Then, the relativistic anomaly is obtained as
Broucke and Cefola 1973

\zeta=u+2\arctan\left(\frac{\beta\sin u}{1-\beta\cos u}\right),

(52)

where

\beta=\frac{e}{1+\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}.

(53)

For completeness, we also provide the inverse transformation from relativistic anomaly to mean anomaly.
First, the eccentric anomaly is calculated through

u=\zeta-2\arctan\left(\frac{\beta\sin\zeta}{1+\beta\cos\zeta}\right).

(54)

Afterwards, the mean anomaly is directly obtained from Kepler’s equation (
51
).

To illustrate the differences between uniform distributions in relativistic anomaly and mean anomaly, Fig.
3
shows the corresponding distributions obtained by randomly drawing
10^{7}
samples of a given anomaly from the interval
[0,\hskip 0.5pt2\pi]
and mapping them to the other anomaly at an eccentricity of
0.3
.
Specifically, the top panel shows a uniform distribution in mean anomaly and the corresponding relativistic anomaly distribution computed using Eqs. (
51
) and (
52
), while the bottom panel shows a uniform distribution in relativistic anomaly and the corresponding mean anomaly distribution computed using Eqs. (
51
) and (
54
).
We observe that a uniform distribution in mean anomaly (cf. a uniform prior in mean anomaly, in the context of parameter estimation), leads to a concentration of the relativistic anomaly values around
\zeta=\pi
(apastron).
Conversely, a uniform distribution in relativistic anomaly (cf. a uniform prior in relativistic anomaly), leads to a concentration of mean anomaly values around
l=0
(periastron).
The effects of relativistic and mean anomalies in the waveforms are illustrated in Fig.
20
of Sec.
VII.4
, where we see that the mean anomaly parametrization leads to a
visually
more evenly distributed waveform amplitude (see also Ref.
Nee et al. 2025a
).

Figure 3:

Top panel:

Uniform distribution in mean anomaly and its mapping to relativistic anomaly through Eqs. (
51
) and (
52
).

Bottom panel:

Uniform distribution in relativistic anomaly and its mapping to mean anomaly through Eqs. (
51
) and (
54
).
Both uniform distributions are composed of
10^{7}
random samples over the interval
[0,\hskip 0.5pt2\pi]
.
The mappings are performed at an eccentricity of
0.3
.

IV.2
Generic planar orbits

We determine initial conditions for generic planar orbits from starting values of the energy
E/M
, total angular momentum
J/M^{2}
, and relative separation
r_{0}
, following the prescription employed in the
TEOBResumS-Dalí
model (e.g., see Refs.
Damour et al. 2014
;
Hopper et al. 2023
;
Albanesi et al. 2025a
).
Specifically, we set
\phi_{0}=0
, we determine the initial orbital angular momentum through

\frac{p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0}}{\mu M}=\frac{1}{\nu}\left(\frac{J}{M^{2}}-\chi_{1}\hskip 0.5ptX_{1}^{2}-\chi_{2}\hskip 0.5ptX_{2}^{2}\right),

(55)

and we compute the initial radial momentum
p_{r_{*}0}
by numerically finding the root of the equation

E=H_{\text{EOB}}(r_{0},p_{r_{*}0},p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0}),

(56)

where, as an initial guess, we use the relation

p_{r_{*}0}\approx\pm\sqrt{H_{\text{eff}}^{2}-A_{\text{noS}}(r_{0})\left(\mu^{2}+\frac{p_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt0}^{2}}{r_{0}^{2}}\right)}\,.

(57)

The sign in Eq. (
57
) is chosen depending on the initial binary’s direction, with
+
(
-
) used for an outgoing (ingoing) trajectory.

V
Multipolar waveforms for binaries on generic planar orbits

We model the GWs emitted by binaries on generic planar orbits in terms of their
waveform modes

h_{\ell m}
.
These are the expansion coefficients of the
h_{+}
and
h_{\times}
polarizations in a
-2
spin-weighted spherical harmonic basis decomposition (
19
).
We first numerically integrate the equations of motion (
15
), and then compute the modes from analytical formulas evaluated on the dynamics.
For coalescing binaries, we employ a phenomenological ansatz for the merger-ringdown phase.

More specifically, we decompose the modes according to
