h_{\ell m}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h_{\ell m}^{\text{F}},&\text{unbound orbits},\\
h_{\ell m}^{\text{insp-plunge }}=N_{\ell m}\,h_{\ell m}^{\text{F}},&\text{bound orbits, }t\leq t_{\text{match }},\\
h_{\ell m}^{\text{merger-RD }}\hskip-25.5pt,&\text{bound orbits, }t\geq t_{\text{match }},\end{array}\right.

(58)

where the functions
h_{\ell m}^{\text{F}}
are the
factorized
EOB modes which resum analytical PN results,
N_{\ell m}
are
nonquasicircular
(NQC)
corrections
which incorporate information from NR waveforms to improve the late-inspiral waveform of binary coalescences, and
h_{\ell m}^{\text{merger-RD }}
is the merger-ringdown portion of the waveform starting at the matching time
t_{\text{match }}
related to the peak of the
(2,2)
mode.
We present all these elements with more detail in the following subsections.

The multipoles supported by the
SEOBNRv6EHM
model are:

(\ell,|m|)=\{(2,2),\,(3,3),\,(2,1),\,(4,4),\,(3,2),\,(4,3)\},\!

(59)

since for these we have information for the merger-ringdown from QC NR simulations from Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
.
These are the same multipoles modeled in the
SEOBNRv5EHM
model
Gamboa et al. 2025b
.

The waveforms are aligned (
t=\nolinebreak 0
) at the
last
peak of the frame-invariant amplitude
A_{\text{inv}}
, given a threshold value of
10\%
with respect to the
largest
peak of
A_{\text{inv}}
, where

A_{\text{inv}}=\sqrt{\sum_{\ell,\,|m|\leq\ell}|h_{\ell m}|^{2}}\,.

(60)

This convention allows the possibility that the last prominent peak of
A_{\text{inv}}
(associated with the start of the merger-ringdown) is not the largest one, which can occur for some high-eccentricity binaries, as noted in Refs.
Gold and Brügmann 2013
;
Carullo et al. 2024
;
Albanesi et al. 2025a
.
The threshold of
10\%
is chosen to avoid selecting amplitude peaks due to mode-mixing during the ringdown phase.

In the following, we describe the construction of multipolar waveforms for binaries on generic planar orbits.
We introduce a new mode factorization, detail improvements to the inspiral-plunge stage via NR-informed NQC corrections, and outline the procedure for attaching the merger-ringdown phase.

V.1
Factorized EOB modes

Expressions for the eccentric modes have been derived within the PN framework
Mishra et al. 2015
;
Boetzel et al. 2019
;
Ebersold et al. 2019
;
Henry and Khalil 2023
;
Paul et al. 2024
.
However, their accuracy and numerical robustness deteriorate at high velocities, e.g., during the plunge or in strong periastron passages.
To improve this, EOB models resum the modes in terms of physically and numerically motivated factors
Damour and Nagar 2007
;
Damour and Nagar 2008
;
Damour et al. 2009
;
Pan et al. 2011
.

In
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we factorize the waveform modes as

h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{F}}=h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{F,\,hyb}}(\Omega,r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}})\,h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{ecc}}\left(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\right),

(61)

where
h_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,hyb}}
is a factor similar to the one employed in typical EOB QC models, and
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc}}
contains eccentricity contributions required for Eq. (
61
) to have the correct PN expansion.

The term
h_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,hyb}}
hybridizes elements employed in previous
SEOBNRv5
models
Pompili et al. 2023
;
Gamboa et al. 2025b
with a novel Newtonian prefactor.
Specifically, it takes the form

h_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,hyb}}=h_{\ell m}^{\text{N}}\,\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}\,T_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}\,f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}\,\mathrm{e}^{i\delta_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}}.

(62)

Starting with the second factor in Eq. (
62
),
\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}
, this is the dimensionless effective source given by

\hat{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}=\begin{cases}H_{\mathrm{eff}}/\mu,&\ell+m\text{ even},\\[5.0pt]
(M\Omega)^{1/3}\,p_{\phi}/(M\mu),&\ell+m\text{ odd}.\end{cases}

(63)

whose structure is motivated by solutions to the Regge-Wheeler-Zerilli equation for QC orbits.
A possible extension to generic planar orbits is discussed in Appendix
A.4
.

The third factor,
T_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
, resums an infinite contribution of tail terms for QC orbits
Blanchet 1998
and is given by
Damour and Nagar 2007
;
Damour and Nagar 2008
7
7

7

Recently, the function
T_{\ell m}
was generalized in Ref.
Ivanov et al. 2025
to resum both the leading (infrared) logarithms (terms proportional to
\Omega^{n}\log^{n}\Omega
) and the universal ultraviolet tails (terms proportional to
\Omega^{n+k}\log^{n}\Omega
, with
k>\nolinebreak 0
corresponding to dissipative tails).

T_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}=\frac{\Gamma\left(\ell+1-2i\hat{k}\right)}{\Gamma(\ell+1)}e^{\pi\hat{k}}e^{2i\hat{k}\ln(2m\hskip 0.5ptM\Omega\hskip 0.5ptr_{0})},

(64)

where
\Gamma
is the Euler gamma function,
\hat{k}\equiv m\hskip 0.5pt\Omega\hskip 0.5ptH_{\text{EOB}}
, and the value of
r_{0}
is set to
2/\!\sqrt{\text{e}}
to improve the agreement with waveforms in the test-body limit
Pan et al. 2011
.

The amplitude
f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
and phase
\delta_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
factors in Eq. (
62
) give consistency with the PN expanded modes for QC orbits.
We do not include calibration parameters in these functions, as done in other EOB models (particularly, in
SEOBNRv5EHM

Gamboa et al. 2025b
);
we only add them in the RR force (
11
), as discussed in Sec.
VI
.
The amplitude factor is further resummed in terms of its
\ell
-th root,
f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}=(\hskip 0.5pt\hskip 0.5pt\rho_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}})^{\ell}
, according to
Damour et al. 2009
;
Pan et al. 2011
;
Taracchini et al. 2012
;
Taracchini et al. 2014a

\displaystyle f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\hskip 0.5pt\hskip 0.5pt\rho_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}})^{\ell},&\quad m\text{ even},\\
(\hskip 0.5pt\hskip 0.5pt\rho_{\ell m}^{\text{qc,\,NS}})^{\ell}+f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc,\,S}},&\quad m\text{ odd},\end{array}\right.

where
\rho_{\ell m}^{\text{qc,\,NS}}
denotes the nonspinning part of
\rho_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
, and
f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc,\,S}}
denotes the spinning part of
f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
.
The full expressions are given explicitly in Appendix B of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
[for the RR force, we add more terms to
f_{31}
as shown in Eq. (
44
)].
We remark that the
f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
and
\delta_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
factors depend on the instantaneous orbital frequency
\Omega
and on the Hamiltonian
H_{\text{EOB}}
.

The factor
h_{\ell m}^{\text{N}}
in Eq. (
62
) contains the leading-order (Newtonian) term for generic planar orbits, and is expressed as

h_{\ell m}^{\rm N}=\frac{\nu M}{d_{\mathcal{L}}}n_{\ell m}\,c_{\ell+\epsilon_{\ell m}}(\nu)\,\hat{h}_{\ell m}\hskip 0.5ptY_{\ell-\epsilon_{\ell m},-m}\left(\frac{\pi}{2},\phi\right),

(67)

where
\epsilon_{\ell m}
is the parity of the mode (such that
\epsilon_{\ell m}=0
if
\ell+m
is even, and
\epsilon_{\ell m}=1
if
\ell+m
is odd),
Y_{\ell,m}
is the scalar spherical harmonic, the functions
n_{\ell m}
and
c_{k}(\nu)
are defined by

\displaystyle c_{k}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2}\right)^{k-1}\!\!+(-1)^{k}\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2}\right)^{k-1}\!\!\!\!,

(68)

\displaystyle n_{\ell m}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{8\pi(im)^{\ell}}{(2\ell+1)!!}\sqrt{\tfrac{(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}{\ell(\ell-1)}},&\text{$\ell+m$ even,}\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{-16i\pi(im)^{\ell}}{(2\ell+1)!!}\sqrt{\tfrac{(2\ell+1)(\ell+2)(\ell^{2}-m^{2})}{(2\ell-1)(\ell+1)\ell(\ell-1)}},&\text{$\ell+m$ odd,}\end{cases}

(69)

and the factor
\hat{h}_{\ell m}
is given, for each
SEOBNRv6EHM
mode, as

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{22}

\displaystyle=P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]^{2/3}+\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.5pt\frac{r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}+i\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}\hskip 0.5ptr\hskip 0.5pt\Omega,

(70a)

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{21}

\displaystyle=M\Omega,

(70b)

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{33}

\displaystyle=M\Omega\left(1+\frac{2}{9}\frac{r^{2}\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu M}-\frac{2}{3}\frac{r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}}{M}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+i\,\dot{r}\left(\frac{10}{9}\frac{M}{r}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{r\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu}-\frac{2}{9}\dot{r}^{2}\right),

(70c)

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{32}

\displaystyle=P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]^{4/3}+\frac{M\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu}+\frac{i}{4}\dot{r}\hskip 0.5ptM\Omega,

(70d)

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{44}

\displaystyle=P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]^{4/3}+\frac{63}{64}\frac{M\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu}+\frac{3}{32}\frac{r^{2}\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-\dot{r}^{2}\left(\frac{27}{32}\frac{M}{r}+\frac{9}{16}\frac{r\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{3}{32}\dot{r}^{4}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3i}{8}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}\hskip 0.5ptM\Omega\left(\frac{13}{4}-\frac{r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}}{M}+\frac{r^{2}\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu M}\right),

(70e)

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{43}

\displaystyle=M\Omega\,P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]^{2/3}+\frac{1}{27}\hskip 0.5ptM\Omega\left(23\,\frac{r\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu}-2\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{10i}{27}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}\left(\frac{M\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}{\mu}+\frac{M^{2}}{r^{2}}\right),

(70f)

where
\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}=-\xi\,\partial H_{\text{EOB}}/\partial r
and
P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]
is the
(0,5)
Padé resummation of the function
\Omega_{\text{c}}=\Omega_{\text{c}}(r)
given in Eqs. (
128
).

This parametrization yields a robust behavior during the plunge and a seamless transition to the QC-orbit limit.
In such limit, the Newtonian prefactor
h_{\ell m}^{\text{N,\,qc}}
is defined as in Eq. (
67
), but with
\hat{h}_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}=(M\Omega)^{(\ell+\epsilon_{\ell m})/3}

Kidder 2008
.
This definition is used in the RR force [see Eqs. (
11
) and (
14
)] without any resummation to
\hat{h}_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
, as done, e.g., in
SEOBNRv5HM

Pompili et al. 2023
.

The expressions (
70
) for
\hat{h}_{\ell m}
are motivated by our RR force prescription (
42
), and they are obtained from the leading-order part of the modes given in Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.
For example, by using the Newtonian relations,
\Omega=p_{\phi}/(\hskip 0.5pt\mu r^{2})
,
\dot{r}=\nolinebreak p_{r}/\mu
, and
p_{\phi}/(\hskip 0.5pt\mu M)=\nolinebreak\sqrt{r/M+\dot{p}_{r}\hskip 0.5ptr^{3}/(\hskip 0.5pt\mu M^{2})}
, the
(2,2)
-mode function

\hat{h}_{22}=\frac{M}{2\hskip 0.5ptr}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2\hskip 0.5pt\mu^{2}r^{2}}-\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2\mu^{2}}+i\,\frac{p_{\phi}p_{r}}{\mu^{2}r},

(71)

can be rewritten as

\hat{h}_{22}=\frac{M}{r}+\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.5pt\frac{r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r}}{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}+i\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}\hskip 0.5ptr\hskip 0.5pt\Omega.

(72)

Then, to accurately recover the known circular-orbit limit
[
\hat{h}_{22}^{\text{qc}}=(M\Omega)^{2/3}
]
, we replace the
M/r
term in Eq. (
72
) by its expression in terms of
P^{0}_{5}[M\Omega_{\text{c}}]
and we use
\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}
, in analogy with the procedure done in Sec.
III.2.1
.
This process results in Eq. (
70a
).
We note, however, that a degree of arbitrariness remains in this construction.
For example, the
p_{\phi}
variable in the complex term of Eq. (
71
) has been replaced by
\mu r^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\Omega
instead of
\mu M\sqrt{r/M+r^{3}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}/(\mu M^{2})}
;
both options are equally valid, provided that the eccentricity corrections
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc}}
(discussed below) are consistent with the chosen leading-order term.
An analogous procedure (with arbitrary choices) is done for the higher-order modes (
70b
)–(
70f
).
We defer a systematic assessment of the impact of these choices on mode accuracy to future work.

The second term in Eq. (
61
),
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc}}
, represents the eccentricity corrections to the modes, provided in Eqs. (
126
).
These corrections are derived by requiring a consistency between the PN expansion of Eq. (
61
) and the EOB modes from Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.
The parametrization
\left(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\right)
has the same advantages discussed in Sec.
III.2.2
, namely, applicability to generic planar orbits and accurate recovery of the QC-orbit limit, in which
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc}}\to 1
.
Furthermore, the corrections
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc}}
are always well behaved, eliminating the need for ad hoc treatments, such as the sigmoid functions employed in the
SEOBNRv4EHM

Ramos-Buades et al. 2022b
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
models
Nagar et al. 2021b
.

The factors
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc}}
are employed in a PN-expanded form up to 1PN order (relative to the leading-order of the
(\ell,m)
mode).
During the development of
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we tested higher PN corrections.
However, we found that their effect negatively overlaps with that of the NQC corrections
N_{\ell m}
, since the amplitudes of
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc}}
grow considerably during the plunge.
Such issue is not observed in
SEOBNRv5EHM

Gamboa et al. 2025b
, since it employs a different prescription for
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc}}
;
this enables the incorporation of higher PN orders, but restricts the model applicability to bound orbits and introduces nonphysical features in highly eccentric waveforms (see Fig.
13
).
This motivates adopting the new approach at 1PN order.
We emphasize, however, that the factor
h_{\ell m}^{\text{F,\,qc}}(\Omega)
contains high-PN-order QC information, which induces additional eccentric effects through the instantaneous frequency
\Omega
.
This may provide a sufficiently accurate approximation to the full expressions for generic planar orbits.
Future work could explore alternative resummations or parametrizations that enable the incorporation of higher PN corrections without interfering with the NQC corrections.

For scattering orbits, Eq. (
61
) determines the full multipolar emission modeled in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
No phenomenological corrections are included, due both to the lack of a suitable framework for their implementation and to the limited availability of NR scattering waveforms.
Future work could address this as more NR data becomes available.

For coalescing binaries, we improve the late-time waveform and attach a merger-ringdown, as described next.

V.2
NQC corrections and background QC dynamics

The inspiral-plunge part of the modes for coalescing binaries is computed as

h_{\ell m}^{\text{insp-plunge}}=N_{\ell m}\,h_{\ell m}^{\text{F}},

(73)

where
N_{\ell m}
are phenomenological NQC corrections which ensure that the amplitude and frequency of the modes agree with input values coming from fits of QC NR simulations.

SEOBNRv6EHM
adopts the same approach as
SEOBNRv5EHM
for the computation of the NQC corrections using a
background QC dynamics
.
Specifically, it employs the same functional form for the NQC corrections as in Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
, but evaluates them on the dynamics of a QC binary with the same values of masses, spins, and starting orbit-averaged frequency as the eccentric system.
To reduce computational cost, this QC dynamics is obtained from a post-adiabatic evolution of the equations of motion (
15
)
Nagar and Rettegno 2019
;
Rettegno et al. 2020
;
Mihaylov et al. 2021
;
Akcay et al. 2019
;
Nagar et al. 2019
.
For this evolution, we employ Eqs. (19) and (20) from Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
, modifying only the last term in their Eq. (20) to be used with a generic radial RR force component,
\mathcal{F}_{r}
, to match our prescription (
11b
).

Thus, the
SEOBNRv6EHM
NQC corrections take the form

\displaystyle N_{\ell m}

\displaystyle=\left[1+\frac{p_{r_{*},\,\text{qc}}^{2}}{(\mu r_{\text{qc}}\hskip 0.5pt\Omega_{\text{qc}})^{2}}\left(a_{1}^{h_{\ell m}}+\frac{Ma_{2}^{h_{\ell m}}}{r_{\text{qc}}}+\frac{M^{3/2}a_{3}^{h_{\ell m}}}{r^{3/2}_{\text{qc}}}\right)\right]

(74)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left[i\left(b_{1}^{h_{\ell m}}\frac{p_{r_{*},\,\text{qc}}}{\mu r_{\text{qc}}\hskip 0.5pt\Omega_{\text{qc}}}+b_{2}^{h_{\ell m}}\frac{p_{r_{*},\,\text{qc}}^{3}}{\mu^{3}r_{\text{qc}}\hskip 0.5pt\Omega_{\text{qc}}}\right)\right],

where
(r_{\text{qc}},\Omega_{\text{qc}},p_{r_{*},\,\text{qc}})
come from the background QC evolution, and
(a_{1}^{h_{\ell m}}\!
,
a_{2}^{h_{\ell m}}\!
,
a_{3}^{h_{\ell m}}\!
,
b_{1}^{h_{\ell m}}\!
,
b_{2}^{h_{\ell m}})
are coefficients determined by requiring that the amplitude, its first and second derivatives, as well as the mode frequency and its first derivative, agree for all the
(\ell,m)
modes (
59
) with fits of NR input values at the matching time
t_{\text{match}}
(discussed below)
Taracchini et al. 2014a
;
Bohé et al. 2017
;
Cotesta et al. 2018
.
The fits are given in Appendix C of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
.

The NQC corrections (
74
) become relevant only during the late inspiral and plunge.
In particular, they satisfy
N_{\ell m}\to 1
in the early inspiral since
|p_{r_{*},\,\text{qc}}/\mu|\ll\nolinebreak r_{\text{qc}}\hskip 0.5pt\Omega_{\text{qc}}
during a QC evolution.
This would not be the case if Eq. (
74
) were evaluated on an eccentric dynamics, since the quantity
p_{r_{*}}/(\mu r\hskip 0.5pt\Omega)
oscillates throughout the inspiral.
This problem was addressed in the
SEOBNRv4EHM
model by using orbit-averaged dynamical variables
(\bar{r},\,\bar{p}_{r_{*}},\,\bar{\Omega})

Ramos-Buades et al. 2022b
, and in the
TEOBResumS-Dalí
model by using a sigmoid function that tapers the effects of the NQC corrections in the early inspiral
Nagar et al. 2021b
.

We find the use of a background QC dynamics more convenient since it is independent of the eccentric dynamics (e.g., the time to merger, or the eccentricity in the late inspiral), and it helps in the determination of the merger-ringdown matching time, as discussed in Sec.
V.3
.
Furthermore, the time to merger of the QC dynamics,
t_{\text{merger}}^{\text{qc}}
, can be used as a threshold to prevent the generation of non-physical waveforms.
At fixed initial orbit-averaged frequency, eccentric systems are expected to have a shorter time to merger,
t_{\text{merger}}^{\text{ecc}}
, due to the enhanced loss of energy and angular momentum through GW emission.
Therefore, an eccentric system with
t_{\text{merger}}^{\text{ecc}}>t_{\text{merger}}^{\text{qc}}
would indicate inaccuracies in the initial conditions of the dynamics or in its subsequent evolution.
Thus, in
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we restrict waveform generation to the systems that satisfy
t_{\text{merger}}^{\text{ecc}}\leq 1.05\,t_{\text{merger}}^{\text{qc}}
, where we added an empirically determined factor to account for different stopping conditions between the eccentric and QC dynamics.

As in
SEOBNRv5EHM
, the eccentric and QC dynamics are aligned at a common value of the binary’s relative separation,
r_{\text{ref}}
.
The final dynamics in both systems is very similar thanks to the binary circularization
Peters and Mathews 1963
;
Peters 1964
;
Hinder et al. 2008
;
Sperhake et al. 2008
.
Therefore, we assume that there is a time
t_{*}
such that, for all subsequent times
t\geq t_{*}
, we have
r\approx\nolinebreak r_{\text{qc}}
, where
r
is the separation of the eccentric binary and
r_{\text{qc}}
is the separation of the QC binary.
Thus, we align both dynamics at the times
t_{\text{ref,\,ecc}}
and
t_{\text{ref,\,qc}}
at which

\displaystyle r(t_{\text{ref,\,ecc}})

\displaystyle=r_{\text{qc}}(t_{\text{ref,\,qc}})=r_{\text{ref}},

(75a)

\displaystyle r_{\text{ref}}

\displaystyle\equiv\max\left(r_{\text{final}},\,r_{\text{final,\,qc}}\right),

(75b)

where
r_{\text{final}}
and
r_{\text{final,\,qc}}
are the final values of the separation in the eccentric and QC dynamics, respectively, both of which are assumed to occur after
t_{*}
.
These approximations hold even for large eccentricities, as demonstrated by the comparisons against eccentric NR waveforms shown in Sec.
VII.2
.
However, the assumptions may break down for extreme configurations.
Future work could focus on developing other methods to determine the NQC corrections without resorting to orbit averages, sigmoid functions, or additional binary evolutions.

V.3
Matching time

For coalescing binaries, the time
t_{\text{match}}
at which the NQC coefficients
\big\{a_{i}^{h_{\ell m}}\!,b_{j}^{h_{\ell m}}\big\}
are calculated, and at which the inspiral-plunge modes
h_{\ell m}^{\text{insp-plunge }}
are matched to the corresponding merger-ringdown
h_{\ell m}^{\text{merger-RD }}\!\!
, is given by

\displaystyle t_{\text{match}}

\displaystyle=t_{\text{ref,\,ecc}}+\Delta t_{\text{match,\,qc}},

(76a)

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\text{match,\,qc}}

\displaystyle=t^{22,\,\text{peak}}_{\text{qc}}-t_{\text{ref,\,qc}},

(76b)

where the reference times
t_{\text{ref,\,qc}}
and
t_{\text{ref,\,ecc}}
are determined from Eqs. (
75
), and
t^{22,\,\text{peak}}_{\text{qc}}
is the time at which the
(2,2)
mode peaks in QC binaries, and is calculated from
Pompili et al. 2023

t^{22,\,\text{peak}}_{\text{qc}}=t_{\text{ISCO}}+\Delta t_{\text{ISCO}}^{22},

(77)

with
\Delta t_{\text{ISCO}}^{22}
a parameter calibrated with QC NR waveforms, given in Eq. (
131
), and
t_{\text{ISCO}}
being the time at which the separation of the background QC dynamics is equal to the Kerr geodesic innermost stable circular orbit (ISCO) radius
Bardeen et al. 1972
,

r_{\text{qc}}(t_{\text{ISCO}})=r_{\text{ISCO}}.

(78)

The value of
r_{\text{ISCO}}
is calculated using the final mass and spin of the remnant BH, which are determined with fits obtained from QC NR simulations
Jiménez-Forteza et al. 2017
;
Hofmann et al. 2016
.
We remark that the value of
t_{\text{ISCO}}
calculated from Eq. (
78
) is independent of features in the late dynamics, and is uniquely defined since the separation decreases monotonically in a QC binary.

This method for determining the matching time
t_{\text{match}}
is the same as in
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
For eccentric binaries, one cannot employ directly Eq. (
77
) to determine
t_{\text{match}}
since 1)
\Delta t_{\text{ISCO}}^{22}
is calibrated to QC NR waveforms, and 2) eccentricity oscillations could result in multiple roots of the equation
r(t)=r_{\text{ISCO}}
, which would make the model unstable under perturbations of the input parameters.
Therefore, given the absence of a calibration to eccentric NR waveforms, the prescription in Eqs. (
76
) for the matching time is a good strategy that leverages the background QC dynamics (used in the computation of the NQC corrections) to inherit the calibration done with QC NR waveforms.
Future investigations could focus on finding alternative methods to compute the matching time, potentially introducing a calibration to eccentric NR simulations, or by having a reference point in the late dynamics of generic coalescing systems to which one could add a calibration parameter, as in the
TEOBResumS-Dalí
model (e.g., see Ref.
Nagar et al. 2025
).

V.4
Merger-ringdown waveform modes

We employ a QC prescription for the merger-ringdown modes of coalescing binaries.
We use the same phenomenological ansatz for the merger-ringdown as in
SEOBNRv5HM
, informed by QC NR simulations and QC test-mass limit waveforms
Pompili et al. 2023
.
The ansatz physically represents a Kerr BH that vibrates
down to a state of equilibrium, emitting damped oscillations known as
quasinormal modes
(QNMs).
Specifically, it takes the form
Damour and Nagar 2014
;
Bohé et al. 2017
;
Cotesta et al. 2018
;
Pompili et al. 2023

h_{\ell m}^{\text{merger-RD }}(\bm{\Pi};t)=\nu\,\tilde{A}_{\ell m}(t)\,\mathrm{e}^{i\tilde{\phi}_{\ell m}(t)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-i\sigma_{\ell m0}\left(t-t_{\text{match}}\right)},

(79)

where
\bm{\Pi}=\nolinebreak\{q,\hskip 0.5pt\chi_{1},\hskip 0.5pt\chi_{2}\}
and
\sigma_{\ell m0}
is the complex frequency of the least-damped QNM of the remnant BH (obtained for each (
\ell
,
m
) mode as a function of the BH’s final mass and spin, using the
qnm
Python package of Ref.
Stein 2019
), and where the functions
\tilde{A}_{\ell m}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{\ell m}
are given by
Bohé et al. 2017
;
Cotesta et al. 2018
;
Pompili et al. 2023

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{\ell m}(t)

\displaystyle=c_{1,c}^{\ell m}\tanh\left[c_{1,f}^{\ell m}\left(t-t_{\text{match}}\right)+c_{2,f}^{\ell m}\right]+c_{2,c}^{\ell m},

(80a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}_{\ell m}(t)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\text{match }}^{\ell m}-d_{1,c}^{\ell m}\log\left[\frac{1+d_{2,f}^{\ell m}\mathrm{e}^{-d_{1,f}^{\ell m}\left(t-t_{\text{match}}\right)}}{1+d_{2,f}^{\ell m}}\right],

(80b)

with
\phi_{\text{match }}^{\ell m}
being the phase of the inspiral-plunge
(\ell,m)
mode at
t_{\text{match}}
, the coefficients
c_{i,c}^{\ell m}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) and
d_{1,c}^{\ell m}
ensure that the modes are continuously differentiable at
t_{\text{match}}
, and the values of
c_{i,f}^{\ell m}
and
d_{i,f}^{\ell m}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) are obtained from fits in
\nu
and
\chi
(the BH’s final spin) informed by NR and test-mass limit waveforms.
The fits are given in Appendix D of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
.

Following
SEOBNRv5HM
, another effect modeled in the merger-ringdown of
SEOBNRv6EHM
is the
mode mixing
between the
(3,2)
and
(4,3)
modes.
Due to a mismatch between the
spherical
harmonic basis and the
spheroidal
harmonics basis employed in the computation of QNMs in BH perturbation theory
Buonanno et al. 2007
;
Kelly and Baker 2013
, the
(3,2)
and
(4,3)
modes get mixed with other modes, and this leads to postmerger oscillations.
In contrast, the modes
(\ell,m)=\{(2,2),\hskip 0.5pt(3,3),\hskip 0.5pt(2,1),\hskip 0.5pt(4,4)\}
of comparable-mass binaries show a predominant monotonic amplitude and frequency evolution.

More investigation is needed to understand the biases introduced by applying a QC prescription to the merger-ringdown of eccentric binaries.
Even at relatively high eccentricities, deviations from a QC merger-ringdown remain at most at the percent level
Nee et al. 2025a
.
However, one naturally expects this to be a source of error for highly eccentric binaries
Rao and Carullo 2026
, which motivates future research directions.
Additionally, it is known that QNMs can be excited by scattering encounters or highly eccentric periastron passages (e.g., see Refs.
Thornburg et al. 2020
;
Bae et al. 2024
).
We leave the inclusion of these effects for future work.

VI
Calibration to quasi-circular waveforms

Model accuracy can be improved through calibration to waveforms generated by NR or BH perturbation theory.
This process extends the validity of models beyond the range of their underlying approximations (e.g., low-velocity or small-mass-ratio assumptions).
For EOB models, it is possible to calibrate both the binary dynamics and the corresponding waveform.
Identifying gauge-invariant quantities for the calibration process is straightforward in the QC-orbit limit, and has been done, in particular, in previous
SEOBNR
models.
In the following subsections, we describe the calibrations to QC NR waveforms performed in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.

VI.1
Calibration summary

We calibrate
SEOBNRv6EHM
to 425 QC
SXS
NR waveforms following a nested-sampling approach.
Namely, we determine posterior distributions for specific calibration parameters, which are then fitted with multidimensional functions.
We employ
ten
calibration parameters:
8
8

8

We count the calibration parameters based on the specific place in which they enter, and not on the number of coefficients employed in their fits.

seven in certain
\rho_{\ell m}
functions entering the RR force, two in the Hamiltonian, and one controlling the matching time of the merger-ringdown.
Specifically, these parameters are

•

\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}(\nu)
,

representing linear-in-
\nu
polynomials entering the
\rho_{\ell m}
amplitudes in Eq. (
V.1
) for the seven modes
(\ell,m)\in\nolinebreak\{(2,2),\hskip 0.5pt(2,1),(3,3),\hskip 0.5pt(3,2),\hskip 0.5pt(4,4),\hskip 0.5pt(4,3),\hskip 0.5pt(5,5)\}
.
They enter the RR force [see Eqs. (
11
) and (
14
)], but are not included in the output
SEOBNRv6EHM
modes.
These parameters are approximate fits of numerical second-order GSF (2GSF) flux data, except for
\Delta\rho_{22}^{(1)}
, which is obtained by calibrating to an equal-mass, nonspinning QC NR waveform.
The fits are given in Eqs. (
90
).

•

a_{6}(\nu)
,

a 5PN parameter entering the nonspinning part of the effective Hamiltonian (it enters the
A_{\text{noS}}
potential shown in Eq. (9) of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
), calibrated to QC NR waveforms.
The fit is given in Eq. (
129
).

•

\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}(\nu,\hskip 0.5pta_{+},\hskip 0.5pta_{-})
,

a 4.5PN parameter entering the odd-in-spin part of the effective Hamiltonian [see Eq. (
93
)], calibrated to QC NR waveforms.
The fit is given in Eq. (
130
).

•

\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO}}=\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}(\nu)+\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}(\nu,\hskip 0.5pta_{+},\hskip 0.5pta_{-})
,

a parameter that quantifies the time difference between the peak of the
(2,2)
-mode amplitude and the Kerr ISCO prediction for QC systems [see Eq. (
77
)], and that determines the merger-ringdown attachment through Eqs. (
76
).
The parameter
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO}}
is split into a nonspinning part
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}
and a spinning contribution
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
which vanishes in the nonspinning limit.
The corresponding fits are given in Eqs. (
131
).

The main differences with respect to the
SEOBNRv5
models are:
1) we employ a different calibration parameter
\big(\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}\big)
for the spinning part of the effective Hamiltonian, since it has a better behavior for
SEOBNRv6EHM
across the parameter space;
2) we use quartic polynomial fits for
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}
and for
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
, instead of cubic polynomial fits, since this improves the overall performance of the model;
and 3) we adopt a hierarchical fitting strategy for spinning systems, first constructing fits for equal-mass configurations and then extending them to unequal masses, resulting in improved accuracy.

VI.2
Calibration framework

We employ the nested-sampling methods presented in Refs.
Pompili et al. 2023
;
Bohé et al. 2017
.
Here, we specify the metrics, settings, and the data set employed for the calibration.

VI.2.1
Metrics

To quantify the similarity between two waveforms
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
, we use the
overlap
, defined as a noise-weighted inner product within the frequency band
[f_{\text{min}},f_{\text{max}}]
as
Sathyaprakash and Dhurandhar 1991
;
Finn and Chernoff 1993

(h_{1}\mid h_{2})\equiv 4\,\Re\int^{f_{\text{max}}}_{f_{\text{min}}}\frac{\tilde{h}_{1}(f)\,\tilde{h}^{*}_{2}(f)}{S_{\text{n}}(f)}\,\text{d}f,

(81)

where the tilde
\,\tilde{}\,
denotes the Fourier transform, the star
∗
denotes the complex conjugate, and
S_{\text{n}}
is the one-sided power-spectral density (PSD) of the detector’s noise.
Additionally, we define the normalized waveform
\hat{h}
as

\hat{h}\equiv\frac{h}{\sqrt{(h\mid h)}}.

(82)

In this work, we use the
A+
PSD of LIGO detectors
Evans et al. 2023
(expected design sensitivity for the fifth observing run O5), we set a sampling rate such that the waveforms have a time spacing of
0.1\hskip 0.5ptM
for the lowest total mass considered, we taper the time-domain waveforms using a Planck window
McKechan et al. 2010
before transforming them into the frequency domain, and we set
f_{\text{min}}=10\,
Hz and
f_{\text{max}}=2048\,
Hz.
For short QC NR waveforms whose frequency content is above
10\,
Hz, we instead set
f_{\text{min}}=1.35\,f_{\text{start}}
, where
f_{\text{start}}
is defined as the peak of the NR waveform in the frequency domain, and the factor of
1.35
mitigates Fourier-transform artifacts that would otherwise contaminate the overlap.

Given a set of intrinsic parameters for a QC binary
\bm{\Pi}^{\text{qc}}_{0}=\nolinebreak\{q,\hskip 0.5pt\chi_{1},\hskip 0.5pt\chi_{2}\}
defined at a time
t_{0}
, and a set of calibration parameters
\bm{\theta}
, we define the
calibration mismatch
of an EOB waveform
h_{\mathrm{EOB}}
to a NR waveform
h_{\mathrm{NR}}
as

\mathcal{M}(M,\bm{\theta})=1-\max_{t_{\text{c}},\,\varphi}\left[\left.\Big\langle\hat{h}_{\text{NR}}\bigm|\hat{h}_{\text{EOB}}(t_{\text{c}},\varphi;\bm{\theta})\Big\rangle\,\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-8.5359pt\\
\bm{\Pi}_{\text{NR},0}^{\text{qc}}=\bm{\Pi}_{\text{EOB},0}^{\text{qc}}\end{subarray}}\right],

(83)

where the total mass
M
dependence appears since it determines the physical frequency of the waveform.

Additionally, we define the
difference in merger time
as

\delta t_{\text{merger}}=\left|\,t^{22,\,\text{peak}}_{\text{NR}}-t^{22,\,\text{peak}}_{\text{EOB}}\,\right|,

(84)

where
t^{22,\,\text{peak}}_{\text{NR}}
and
t^{22,\,\text{peak}}_{\text{EOB}}
denote the times of the
(2,2)
-mode amplitude peaks of the NR and EOB waveforms, respectively, after a low-frequency phase alignment.

Our calibration procedures make use only of the
(2,2)
mode of QC waveforms and change depending on the intrinsic parameters
\bm{\Pi}^{\text{qc}}_{0}
and the calibration parameters
\bm{\theta}
.
However, all our calibration procedures use the metrics (
83
) and (
84
) to compare against NR waveforms.
9
9

9

We note that the calibration is done using a specific PSD (in particular, we use the
A+
PSD of LIGO detectors).
Future work could benefit from using a flat PSD to remove the dependence of the calibration on specific detectors.
In this direction, one could also use
time-domain
metrics to avoid the introduction of artifacts from the Fourier transforms.
This will be of particular relevance when calibrating to eccentric waveforms.

VI.2.2
Nested-sampling analysis

Following
SEOBNRv4

Bohé et al. 2017
and
SEOBNRv5

Pompili et al. 2023
, we adopt a calibration strategy based on stochastic sampling to obtain a posterior distribution for the calibration parameters for each NR simulation.
This approach provides an efficient and flexible calibration procedure, well suited to the dimensionality of the problem and the large number of NR simulations available.
Stochastic sampling methods allow for an exploration of high-dimensional parameter spaces, and have the advantage of providing information regarding the correlations between calibration parameters.
As in
SEOBNRv5
, we obtain the best computational performance by employing nested sampling
Skilling 2006
,
using the sampler
nessai

Williams et al. 2021
through
Bilby

Ashton et al. 2019
.

We employ a likelihood function designed to minimize the mismatch between EOB and NR waveforms while simultaneously improving the prediction of the merger time (the mismatch alone is not particularly sensitive to this quantity).
Thus, we define the likelihood function as:

\mathcal{L}(h_{\text{NR}}\mid\bm{\theta})\propto\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}_{\max_{M}}(\bm{\theta})}{\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\delta t_{\text{merger}}(\bm{\theta})}{\sigma_{t}}\right)^{2}\right],

(85)

where
{\mathcal{M}}_{\max_{M}}(\bm{\theta})
is the maximum mismatch between EOB and NR waveforms over the range
10\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}\leq\nolinebreak M\leq\nolinebreak 300\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
, and
\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\sigma_{t}
are tolerance parameters that control the effective width of the likelihood.
For this work, we set a mismatch goal of
\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}=10^{-4}
for nonspinning configurations,
\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}=5\times 10^{-4}
for spinning configurations, and
\sigma_{t}=5M
for all cases.
In
SEOBNRv5HM
,
\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
was set to
10^{-3}
for all cases
Pompili et al. 2023
;
here, we used smaller values of
\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
to facilitate the identification of the correct modes in multimodal posteriors (see next paragraph).
Achieving a mismatch goal of
10^{-4}
for
all
binary configurations would require revisiting the calibration procedure and modifying the analytic structure of the model, since not even the maximum-likelihood points for all calibration data lie below this threshold.
Our objective here is to get a model with accuracy comparable to that of
SEOBNRv5HM
.

For each NR simulation in the calibration data set, we obtain a
posterior distribution
, which is employed to construct fits for the calibration parameters as functions of the binary parameters,
\bm{\theta}(\bm{\Pi})
.
The mean and variance of these posteriors, as well as the correlations among the calibration parameters, are related to the requirements on
\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\sigma_{t}
.
In some cases, these correlations lead to secondary modes in the posteriors (see Fig. 3 of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
);
for such cases, we employ continuity considerations to select only one mode to get a more regular fit across the parameter space.
For all posteriors, we discard samples that do not satisfy the calibration requirements on
\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\sigma_{t}
.
If this would discard more than
50\%
of the points, we instead keep half of the original samples of the selected mode with the best likelihood values.

VI.2.3
Calibration data set

The calibration of
SEOBNRv6EHM
employs 425 out of the 592 QC, aligned-spin NR waveforms from the third release of the
SXS
catalog

SXS
;
Boyle et al. 2019
;
Chu et al. 2016
;
Blackman et al. 2017
;
Hemberger et al. 2013
;
Scheel et al. 2015
;
Lovelace et al. 2015
;
Abbott et al. 2016b
;
Blackman et al. 2015
;
Lovelace et al. 2016
;
Varma et al. 2019a
;
Abbott et al. 2016c
;
Varma et al. 2019b
;
Kumar et al. 2015
;
Mroue et al. 2013
;
Yoo et al. 2022
;
Scheel et al. 2025
, all produced with the pseudo-Spectral Einstein code (
SpEC
)
SpE
.
The calibration dataset comprises 21 nonspinning and 404 spinning cases, of which 78 are equal-mass spinning configurations and 326 are unequal-mass spinning configurations.
Only two out of these 425 simulations were not employed in the calibration of
SEOBNRv5HM
.
Namely,
SXS:BBH:4121
with
q=4
,
\chi_{1}=0.9
,
\chi_{2}=0.5
, and
SXS:BBH:4123
with
q=4
,
\chi_{1}=0.9
,
\chi_{2}=-0.5
.
These lie at parameter-space corners, enabling better extrapolation of the calibration-parameter fits.

The parameter-space distribution of the 592 QC
SXS
NR waveforms employed throughout this work is shown in Fig.
4
, as a function of the spin variables
a_{+}
and
a_{-}
, and the mass ratio
q
.
This includes the 425 waveforms used for the calibration of
SEOBNRv6EHM
(round markers), and the remaining 167 waveforms (star markers).

SXS:BBH:4121
and
SXS:BBH:4123
(top right corner).

benefits from the merger-ringdown and input-value fits of

.

SXS

Einstein

Toolkit
waveform
Haas et al. 2022
;

, and 13 test-mass-limit waveforms computed using the Teukolsky equation
Barausse et al. 2012
;
Taracchini et al. 2014b
.
These add up to the 442 NR + 13 test-mass-limit waveforms quoted in Ref.

.

only counts the simulations actually employed in the new calibration of the parameters
\{a_{6},\hat{d}_{\text{SO}},\Delta t_{\text{ISCO}}^{22}\}
.
