VI.3
RR force calibration

The first part in our calibration procedure consists of determining suitable coefficients at high PN orders for the
\rho_{\ell m}
functions entering the RR force (
11
) via Eqs. (
14
) and (
V.1
).

Following Ref.
van de Meent et al. 2023
, we split the
\rho_{\ell m}
functions based on their dependence on the symmetric mass ratio
\nu
, as

\displaystyle\rho_{\ell m}

\displaystyle=\rho_{\ell m}^{(0)}+\nu\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}+\mathcal{O}\left(\nu^{2}\right),

\displaystyle=\rho_{\ell m}^{(0)}+\nu\left(\rho_{\ell m}^{(1),\,\text{EOB}}+\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\nu^{2}\right).

(86)

The functions
\rho_{\ell m}^{(0)}
and
\rho_{\ell m}^{(1),\,\text{EOB}}
only contain terms derived from PN calculations up to a certain PN order (these terms can be read from Eq. (46) and Appendix B of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
), and the functions
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
are employed as calibration parameters with a dependence on the instantaneous velocity

v_{\Omega}=(M\Omega)^{1/3}.

(87)

In
SEOBNRv5HM

Pompili et al. 2023
, the functions
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
were given as polynomials in
v^{2}_{\Omega}

(
starting at the lowest order in
v^{2}_{\Omega}
not already included in
\rho_{\ell m}^{(1),\,\text{EOB}}
)
whose coefficients were determined by fitting to the numerical, linear in
\nu
, contributions to the
\rho_{\ell m}
functions from 2GSF flux data
van de Meent et al. 2023
;
Warburton et al. 2021
.
10
10

10

After the publication of the
SEOBNRv5
models, the authors of Ref.
Warburton et al. 2021
found an error in their numerical data, hence affecting the physical correctness of the
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
calibration done in Ref.
van de Meent et al. 2023
for the
SEOBNRv5HM
model.
This was recently corrected in Ref.
Leather et al. 2025
, where it was pointed out that, apart from the error in Ref.
Warburton et al. 2021
, there was an inconsistency in the way the GSF calibration was propagated to the waveform modes in Ref.
van de Meent et al. 2023
.
Namely, the correct GSF calibration should yield
different

\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
contributions for the EOB RR force and for the waveform modes.

In
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we employ the same
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
functions as derived in Ref.
Leather et al. 2025
, except for
\Delta\rho_{22}^{(1)}
, which we determine by fitting to an equal-mass, nonspinning, QC NR waveform.
Formally, the new RR force (
11
) requires a different calibration to GSF data because the
\Omega_{\text{c}}
function introduces a linear-in-
\nu
correction to the RR force [as seen in Eqs. (
128
)].
In particular, the functional form of Eq. (53) for
\rho_{\ell m}^{(1),\hskip 0.5pt\text{GSF}}
in Ref.
van de Meent et al. 2023
would need to change.
For simplicity, we do not follow this GSF calibration route.
Instead, we calibrate the (dominant) contribution
\Delta\rho_{22}^{(1)}
to an equal-mass, nonspinning, QC NR waveform, and we use the results for the rest of the functions
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
given in Ref.
Leather et al. 2025
as
approximations
to the physically correct calibration.
Another option was to remove all the
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
functions;
however, we observed that having these functions (in particular,
\Delta\rho_{22}^{(1)}
) is highly important for the calibration to spinning, QC NR waveforms, as it gives a more regular structure to the calibration data, and improves the overall accuracy.

To determine the
\Delta\rho_{22}^{(1)}
contribution, we decompose it as

\Delta\rho_{22}^{(1)}=\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{8}}\,v^{8}_{\Omega}+\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{10}}\,v^{10}_{\Omega},

(88)

and perform a nested-sampling analysis over the parameters
\bm{\theta}=\{\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{8}},\,\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{10}},\,a_{6},\,\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO}}\}
, with uniform priors
\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{8}}\in[-20,40]
,
\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{10}}\in[-200,50]
,
a_{6}\in[-100,150]
, and
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO}}\in[-80,20]
, and with the settings specified in Sec.
VI.2
.
We include the parameters
a_{6}
and
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO}}
since we do not know them a priori, and this gives the sampler the freedom to explore the parameter space (the resulting posteriors of
a_{6}
and
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO}}
are broadly consistent with the actual values determined in Sec.
VI.4
).
We employ the equal-mass, nonspinning QC NR simulation
SXS:BBH:2325
.

Afterwards, we simply select the maximum-likelihood values, which correspond to:

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{8}\!,\,\text{max}\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=40.3,

(89a)

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{10}\!,\,\text{max}\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=-473.

(89b)

These values are similar (in order of magnitude and sign) to the ones employed in
SEOBNRv5HM
[namely,
\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{8}}=21.2
and
\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{10}}=-411
; see Eq. (45a) of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
], and to the ones determined in Ref.
Leather et al. 2025
[namely,
\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{8}}=20.6
and
\Delta\rho_{22,\,v^{10}}=-410
; see Eq. (56a)].

We observed that coefficients that significantly depart from those in Eqs. (
89
), lead to a fit for the nonspinning part of
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO}}
which is problematic for the spinning calibration (in particular, the medians of the calibration posteriors are not well fitted for small spins, as they lie significantly away from the predicted nonspinning value
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}
).
Thus, having coefficients similar to Eqs. (
89
) leads to overall better spinning fits.
This explains why the nonspinning GSF calibration in
SEOBNRv5HM

van de Meent et al. 2023
led to an improved accuracy for spinning systems compared to an uncalibrated model (
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}=0
), despite using incorrect GSF data (see footnote
10
).

Collecting these results with the ones from Eqs. (56b–g) of Ref.
Leather et al. 2025
, gives the values of
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
, as used in
SEOBNRv6EHM
:

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{22}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{40.3}\,v^{8}_{\Omega}-\mathbf{473}\,v^{10}_{\Omega},

(90a)

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{21}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=1.65\,v^{6}_{\Omega}+\mathbf{24.3}\,v^{8}_{\Omega}+80\,v^{10}_{\Omega},

(90b)

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{33}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=12\,v^{8}_{\Omega}-\mathbf{222}\,v^{10}_{\Omega},

(90c)

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{32}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{-0.25}\,v^{6}_{\Omega}-6.5\,v^{8}_{\Omega}+98\,v^{10}_{\Omega},

(90d)

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{44}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=-3.56\,v^{6}_{\Omega}+\mathbf{15.3}\,v^{8}_{\Omega}-216\,v^{10}_{\Omega},

(90e)

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{43}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=-0.654\,v^{4}_{\Omega}-\mathbf{3.73}\,v^{6}_{\Omega}+\mathbf{18}\,v^{8}_{\Omega},

(90f)

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{55}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{-2.608}\,v^{4}_{\Omega}+\mathbf{1.13}\,v^{6}_{\Omega}-\mathbf{35.1}\,v^{8}_{\Omega},

(90g)

where we highlight terms differing from
SEOBNRv5HM
.

We emphasize that these parameters enter only the RR force and not the output
SEOBNRv6EHM
modes.
We find that including them in the waveform modes leads to unphysical features in scattering waveforms, such as a small indentation near the peak of the
(2,2)
-mode amplitude.
Since a proper GSF calibration would in any case yield different coefficients (see footnote
10
), we omit them from the modes for simplicity.

VI.4
Calibration of non-spinning sector

With the polynomials
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
in Eqs. (
90
) fixed, we turn to the calibration of the
a_{6}
parameter entering the nonspinning sector of the Hamiltonian, and the
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}
parameter which determines the merger-ringdown attachment for nonspinning binaries.
We follow the procedure done in
SEOBNRv5HM

Pompili et al. 2023
.

We perform a nested-sampling analysis over the parameters
\bm{\theta}=\{a_{6},\,\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}\}
, with uniform priors

a_{6}\in[-500,500]
and
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}\in[-100,40]
.
We employ the calibration settings specified in Sec.
VI.2
, for 21 nonspinning, QC
SXS
NR simulations
Scheel et al. 2025
, with mass ratios ranging from
q=1
to
q=20
.
This results in a set of calibration posteriors for
a_{6}
and
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}
, which we parametrize in terms of the symmetric mass ratio
\nu
, as in
SEOBNRv5HM
.

We complement the nonspinning NR data set with the following test-mass-limit information:

\displaystyle a_{6}|_{\nu=0.001}

\displaystyle=39.1161,

(91a)

\displaystyle\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}|_{\nu=0.001}

\displaystyle=-225.4.

(91b)

The value of
a_{6}|_{\nu=0.001}
is inferred by requiring that the ISCO shift predicted by the EOB Hamiltonian agrees with the 1GSF ISCO shift, and the value of
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}|_{\nu=0.001}
is estimated from a Teukolsky waveform
Taracchini et al. 2014b
(the details for these calculations can be found in Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
).

The simple structure of the nonspinning calibration data enables us to directly fit the maximum-likelihood values of the posteriors via least-squares regression (we do not use more information from the posteriors in constructing the nonspinning fits).
For
a_{6}
we use a quartic polynomial ansatz in
\nu
, while for
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}
we use an ansatz of the form
Pompili et al. 2023

\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}=\left(\Delta_{0}+\Delta_{1}\hskip 0.5pt\nu+\Delta_{2}\hskip 0.5pt\nu^{2}+\Delta_{3}\hskip 0.5pt\nu^{3}\right)\,\nu^{-1/5+\Delta_{4}\hskip 0.5pt\nu},

(92)

where
\Delta_{i}
(
i\in[0,4]
) represents fitting coefficients, and the
\nu^{-1/5}
factor ensures the expected test-mass scaling for
\big(t^{22,\,\text{peak}}_{\text{qc}}-t_{\text{ISCO}}\big)

Buonanno and Damour 2000
, and provides a better extrapolation of the fit in the
\nu\rightarrow 0
limit.
The maximum-likelihood values of the posteriors are fitted with the
optimize.curve_fit
function from the
scipy
package
Virtanen et al. 2020
;
we assign the same uncertainty to all cases, except for the test-mass-limit data of
a_{6}
, for which we set
\texttt{sigma}=0.3
to give it greater weight.

The data for
a_{6}
and
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}
, and the resulting fits are given in Eqs. (
129
) and (
131b
), and shown in Fig.
22
of Appendix
B
.
These results are used in the calibration and fitting procedure for the spinning cases discussed below.

VI.5
Calibration of spinning sector

Having determined the fits for the nonspinning parameters
\big\{\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)},\hskip 0.5pta_{6},\hskip 0.5pt\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}\big\}
, we now proceed with the calibration of
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
which determines the merger-ringdown attachment for spinning binaries, and of
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}
which enters the spinning sector of the Hamiltonian, via [see Eq. (12) of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
]

H_{\text{eff}}\,\,\propto\,\,\text{SO}_{\text{calib}}=\nu\frac{M^{5}}{r^{3}}p_{\phi}\hskip 0.5pt\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}.

(93)

In
SEOBNRv5HM
, one has
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}=d_{\text{SO}}\hskip 0.5pta_{+}/M
, but here we directly calibrate
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}
as it yields more regular calibration posteriors.

We adopt a different procedure from that used in
SEOBNRv5HM
.
In particular, we employ a
hierarchical spinning approach
, where the equal-mass cases are fitted first, followed by the unequal-mass cases (e.g., see Ref.
Nagar et al. 2024
).
This approach is particularly useful for
SEOBNRv6EHM
, as it provides greater flexibility in fitting the data observed for systems with close-to-equal masses (
q\approx 1
), as discussed below.

We perform a nested sampling analysis over the parameters
\bm{\theta}_{\text{S}}=\big\{\hat{d}_{\text{SO}},\,\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}\big\}
, with uniform priors

\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}\in[-500,500]
and
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}\in[-100,100]
.
We employ the calibration settings specified in Sec.
VI.2
, for 404 spinning, QC
SXS
NR simulations
Scheel et al. 2025
, with mass ratios ranging from
q=1
to
q=15
, and dimensionless spins
\chi_{1,\hskip 0.5pt2}\in(-1,1)
.
This results in a set of calibration posteriors for
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}
and
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
for different mass ratios and spin values, which we parametrize in terms of
(\nu,\hskip 0.5pta_{+},\hskip 0.5pta_{-})
, as in
SEOBNRv5HM
.
As discussed in Sec.
VI.2
, these posteriors are processed by removing secondary modes and excluding samples that do not satisfy the calibration requirements.
For all processed posteriors, we extract the medians
\langle\bm{\theta}_{\text{S}}\rangle_{(n)}
and covariance matrices
{C_{\text{S}}}_{(n)}
, with
n
labeling each of the employed NR waveforms.
We complement this data set with estimates for
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
in the test-mass-limit from Table III of Ref.
Barausse et al. 2012
(see also Sec. IV C of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
).
The estimated values,
\Delta t^{22,\,\text{TML}}_{\text{ISCO,\,}\chi_{i}}
, are employed in Eq. (
100
) below.

Out of the 404 spinning, QC NR simulations, 78 correspond to equal-mass binaries, for which:

a_{+}\,|_{\,q=1}\!=\frac{\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad a_{-}\,|_{\,q=1}\!=\frac{\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}}{2}.

(94)

This suggests a further classification of the 78 equal-mass simulations into:
equal-spin
(20 cases with
a_{-}=0
),
opposite-spin
(7 cases with
a_{+}=0
), and
different-spin
(51 cases with
a_{+}\neq 0
and
a_{-}\neq 0
) configurations.
The calibration data for
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO}}=\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,noS}}+\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
and
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}
, corresponding to each of these spinning cases, is plotted in Fig.
23
of Appendix
B
.
Notably, the data corresponding to the equal-spin and different-spin configurations display a behavior that is not possible to fit with simple, low-order polynomials, due to the structure seen for
a_{+}\in[0,0.5]
.

This behavior motivates the use of a more flexible fitting strategy than that used in
SEOBNRv5HM
, leveraging the different spinning configurations of equal-mass binaries.
This hierarchical spinning approach proceeds as follows:
first, we fit the equal-mass, equal-spin cases as a function of
a_{+}
only, and the equal-mass, opposite-spin cases as a function of
a_{-}
only.
Next, we fit the equal-mass, different-spin cases using the residuals from the previous fits.
Finally, the unequal-mass cases are fitted using the results of all previous fits.

Specifically, these hierarchical spinning fits take the form:

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}

\displaystyle=\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,q=1}+\!\sqrt{1-4\nu}\,\,\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,q\neq 1}\!,

(95a)

\displaystyle\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}

\displaystyle=\Delta t^{22,\,q=1}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}+\!\sqrt{1-4\nu}\,\,\Delta t^{22,\,q\neq 1}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}\hskip 0.5pt,

(95b)

where

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,q=1}

\displaystyle=\,\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,a_{+}}+\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,a_{-}}+\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,a_{+}a_{-}},

(96a)

\displaystyle\Delta t^{22,\,q=1}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}

\displaystyle=\,\Delta t_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}^{22,\,a_{+}}+\Delta t_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}^{22,\,a_{-}}+\Delta t_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}^{22,\,a_{+}a_{-}}.

(96b)

In these equations, the terms with superscript “
q=1
” are associated with equal-mass cases, while those with superscript “
q\neq\nolinebreak 1
” are associated with unequal-mass cases (the factor
\sqrt{1-4\nu}
allows the equal-mass cases to be fitted first).
Additionally, the terms with superscript “
a_{+}\!
” are fitted to equal-spin cases, those with “
a_{-}\!
” to opposite-spin cases, and those with “
a_{+}a_{-}\!
” to different-spin cases.

An advantage of this splitting is that it allows
SEOBNRv6EHM
to satisfy the expected
spin-exchange symmetry
for equal-mass binaries:
the dynamics and waveform of equal-mass systems must remain invariant under the interchange
\chi_{1}\leftrightarrow\chi_{2}
.
The
SEOBNRv5
models fail to reproduce this property, because the fits for the parameters
d_{\text{SO}}
and
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
lack the required symmetry;
instead, these fits rely on the approximate behavior inherited from the calibration data.
In contrast,
SEOBNRv6EHM
respects this symmetry by enforcing it in the fitting procedure.

VI.5.1
Equal-mass spinning fits

To fit the structures observed in the upper panels of each plot in Fig.
23
, we employ the following ansätze:

•

For the equal-spin contributions,
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,a_{+}}
and
\Delta t_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}^{22,\,a_{+}}
, we use a quartic polynomial in
a_{+}
augmented with a Gaussian term,

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,a_{+}}

\displaystyle=4\hskip 0.5pt\nu\,\bigg[d_{1}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}+d_{2}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}^{2}+d_{3}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}^{3}+d_{4}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad+d_{5}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}^{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{\,d_{6}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,(a_{+}-d_{7}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}})^{2}}\bigg],

(97a)

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}^{22,\,a_{+}}

\displaystyle=4\hskip 0.5pt\nu\,\bigg[\Delta_{1}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}+\Delta_{2}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}^{2}+\Delta_{3}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}^{3}+\Delta_{4}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Delta_{5}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,a_{+}^{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{\Delta_{6}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}\,(a_{+}-\Delta_{7}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}})^{2}}\bigg]\,\nu^{-1/5},

(97b)

where
d_{i}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}
and
\Delta_{j}^{\hskip 0.5pta_{+}}
(
i,j\in[1,7]
) represent fitting coefficients, and we have introduced normalization factors of
4\hskip 0.5pt\nu
(equal to one for equal-mass cases), apart from the test-mass-limit scaling of
\nu^{-1/5}
for
\Delta t_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}^{22}
, since this improved the fits when considering unequal-mass systems.

•

For the opposite-spin contributions,
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,a_{-}}
and
\Delta t_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}^{22,\,a_{-}}
, shown in the middle panels of the plots in Fig.
23
, we use a quartic polynomial in
a_{-}
, but we keep only even powers to enforce the spin-exchange (
a_{-}\to-a_{-}
) symmetry.

•

For the different-spin contributions,
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,a_{+}a_{-}}
and
\Delta t_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}^{22,\,a_{+}a_{-}}
, shown in the lower panels of the plots in Fig.
23
, we use a quartic polynomial in both
a_{+}
and
a_{-}
, excluding the common terms already used in the equal-spin and opposite-spin ansätze, and also keeping only even powers in
a_{-}
.

In this way, the full expressions for
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,q=1}
and
\Delta t^{22,\,q=1}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
are given by quartic polynomials in
a_{+}
and
a_{-}
, where odd powers of
a_{-}
are excluded, plus Gaussian terms.
We fit the median values of the calibration posteriors with the
optimize.curve_fit
function from
scipy

Virtanen et al. 2020
.
To facilitate the fitting process of
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,a_{+}}
and
\Delta t_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}^{22,\,a_{+}}
, we first obtain the coefficients of the polynomial part of Eqs. (
97
) by fitting to equal-spin cases with
a_{+}\leq 0
or
a_{+}\geq 0.55
, and then, these results are employed to get the coefficients of the exponential part by fitting to all equal-spin cases.
In
optimize.curve_fit
, we assign the same uncertainty to all cases, except for two equal-spin cases with
a_{+}\sim 0.25
, for which we set
\texttt{sigma}=0.3
to give them greater weight.
The resulting fits for
\Delta t^{22,\,q=1}_{\text{ISCO}}
and
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,q=1}
are presented in Eqs. (
130b
) and (
131c
), and are plotted in Fig.
23
.

VI.5.2
Unequal-mass spinning fits

The fitting process for unequal-mass cases follows the methods employed in
SEOBNRv5HM

Pompili et al. 2023
;
Bohé et al. 2017
, which we summarize here.
First, we propose ansätze for

\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,q\neq 1}
and
\Delta t^{22,\,q\neq 1}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
, in the form of the most generic quartic polynomials in
(\nu,\hskip 0.5pta_{+},\hskip 0.5pta_{-})
, such that they vanish in the nonspinning limit, when
a_{+}=a_{-}=0
.
We note that
SEOBNRv5HM
uses cubic polynomials for the spinning fits, with an extra
a_{+}^{4}
feature for
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
.
However, we find that quartic polynomials help reduce outlier mismatches and improve the overall accuracy.

The coefficients of the polynomial fits are determined by minimizing the function,

\chi_{\text{s}}^{2}\,=\sum_{n\,\in\,\mathcal{S}_{\text{s}}}\frac{w}{2}\left(\bm{\theta}_{\text{S}}-\left\langle\bm{\theta}_{\text{S}}\right\rangle_{(n)}\right)\left(C_{\text{S}}^{-1}\right)_{(n)}\left(\bm{\theta}_{\text{S}}-\left\langle\bm{\theta}_{\text{S}}\right\rangle_{(n)}\right)^{\text{T}}+\chi_{\text{TML}}^{2},

(98)

where
\bm{\theta}_{\text{S}}=\Big\{\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,q\neq 1}\!,\Delta t^{22,\,q\neq 1}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}\Big\}
,
C_{\text{S}}
are the covariance matrices of the calibration posteriors, and
w
is a weighting factor given by

w=\chi_{1}^{2}+\chi_{2}^{2}+\frac{|\,\chi_{S}+\chi_{A}\delta/(1-2\nu)|}{2\nu}\,,

(99)

which accounts for the inhomogeneous distribution of NR waveforms in the binary BH parameter space, and
\chi_{\text{TML}}^{2}
is a term that penalizes deviations from the test-mass limit of
\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
and takes the form

\chi_{\text{TML}}^{2}=\sum_{\chi_{i}\,\neq\,0}\frac{\Big(\Delta t^{22}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}-\Delta t^{22,\,\text{TML}}_{\text{ISCO,\,}\chi_{i}}\Big)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{\text{TML}}},

(100)

with
\sigma_{\text{TML}}=5M
, as in
SEOBNRv5HM
.

The minimization of the function (
98
) determines the fit coefficients for the unequal-mass, spinning contributions
\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}^{\,q\neq 1}
and
\Delta t^{22,\,q\neq 1}_{\text{ISCO,\,S}}
.
This process is done with a Sequential Least Squares Programming minimization algorithm
Bohé et al. 2017
.

We performed extensive tests of the model and its fits to ensure physical robustness across broad regions of parameter space, including eccentricity.
These tests are discussed in Sec.
VII.4
.
To guard against overfitting, we also performed comparisons with additional QC and eccentric NR simulations not used in the calibration, as well as with QC NR surrogate models, as detailed in Sec.
VII.2
.
Moreover, the reliability of the calibration pipeline for the multidimensional spinning fits was validated in Sec. E of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
.
Overall, these results indicate that the fits in
SEOBNRv6EHM
achieve the same level of robustness as the established
SEOBNRv5HM
model.

Our results highlight the complex interplay between the calibration process, fitting strategies, and model components (Hamiltonian, RR force, and waveform modes).
For example, the calibration parameters
\Delta\rho_{\ell m}^{(1)}
discussed in Sec.
VI.3
improve the model accuracy because they facilitate the overall calibration-fitting procedure, both in
SEOBNRv5HM
and
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
Future work may explore alternative calibration and fitting strategies, assess the role of individual model components in the calibration process, and extend the calibration to NR or small-mass-ratio eccentric waveforms.

VII
Model validation

In this section, we assess the accuracy, speed, and robustness of
SEOBNRv6EHM
across different parameter space regions.
Accuracy is quantified through waveform and scattering-angle comparisons with NR results, efficiency through waveform-generation benchmarks, and robustness through an analysis of the regions where
SEOBNRv6EHM
yields physically meaningful and accurate waveforms.
In the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
, we perform further tests within the context of Bayesian parameter estimation, confirming the high accuracy and computational efficiency of
SEOBNRv6EHM
.

VII.1
Waveform metrics

The
strain
measured in a GW detector can be expressed as

\displaystyle h(t)\,=

\displaystyle\;\,F_{+}(\Theta,\Phi,\Psi)\,h_{+}(\iota,\varphi,d_{\mathcal{L}},\bm{\Pi},t_{\text{c}};t)

\displaystyle+F_{\times}(\Theta,\Phi,\Psi)\,h_{\times}(\iota,\varphi,d_{\mathcal{L}},\bm{\Pi},t_{\text{c}};t),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\,\mathcal{A}(\Theta,\Phi)\left(h_{+}\cos\kappa+h_{+}\sin\kappa\right),

(101)

where
F_{+}
and
F_{\times}
are the detector’s antenna-patterns
Sathyaprakash and Dhurandhar 1991
;
Finn and Chernoff 1993
,
\,h_{+}
and
h_{\times}
are the GW polarizations defined in Eq. (
19
),
\mathcal{A}(\Theta,\Phi)
is given in Refs.
Cotesta et al. 2018
;
Ossokine et al. 2020
, and
\kappa=\kappa(\Theta,\Phi,\Psi)
is an effective polarization angle.
The strain depends both on
extrinsic
and
intrinsic
parameters of the source.
The extrinsic parameters determine the location of the source with respect to a GW detector:
the angular position of the line of sight measured in the source frame (
\iota
,
\varphi
), the sky location of the source in the detector frame
(\Theta,\Phi)
, the polarization angle
\Psi
, the luminosity distance of the source
d_{\mathcal{L}}
, and the coalescence time
t_{\text{c}}
.
The intrinsic parameters
\bm{\Pi}
determine a unique physical configuration:
the binary masses
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
(or, equivalently, mass ratio
q
and total mass
M
), the dimensionless spin components along the
z
direction
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
, as well as an orbital eccentricity
e
and a radial phase
\zeta
specified at a certain stage of the binary’s evolution (e.g., at an orbit-averaged frequency
\langle M\Omega\rangle
), or a set of reference values for the energy
E/M
, angular momentum
J/M^{2}
, and relative separation
r
of the binary.

To quantify the agreement between a template
h_{\text{t}}
(e.g., an EOB waveform) and a signal
h_{\text{s}}
(e.g., a NR waveform), we employ the
faithfulness
functions from Sec. IV A of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025b
.
In general, these functions maximize the overlap (
81
) between the template and signal,

\mathcal{F}(M,\Sigma_{\text{s}})=\max_{\Sigma_{\text{t}}}\,\langle h_{\text{s}}|\hskip 0.5pth_{\text{t}}\rangle,

(102)

by optimizing the template parameters
\Sigma_{\text{t}}
at a certain total mass
M
and for specific source parameters
\Sigma_{\text{s}}
.
For inner-product calculations, we use the settings discussed below Eq. (
82
).
For comparisons against eccentric NR waveforms, however, we interpret
f_{\text{start}}
as the initial orbit-averaged frequency of the NR
(2,2)
mode and we trim the longest waveform to ensure that both waveforms
h_{\text{NR}}
and
h_{\text{t}}
have the same time to merger.
This approach ensures that only physically meaningful frequencies are considered, thereby avoiding contamination from Fourier transform artifacts
Pompili et al. 2023
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2023b
;
Gamboa et al. 2025b
.

The template parameters optimized in Eq. (
102
) depend on the type of binary (QC or eccentric) and on the mode content [e.g.,
(2,2)
-mode only or including higher-order modes].
For QC waveforms, the coalescence time
t_{\text{c}}
, azimuthal angle
\varphi
, and effective polarization angle
\kappa
of the template are optimized
Capano et al. 2014
;
Harry et al. 2018
;
Cotesta et al. 2018
;
Ossokine et al. 2020
;
García-Quirós et al. 2020
.
For eccentric waveforms, additional optimizations are required due to the gauge dependence of orbital eccentricity in general relativity.

The standard approach for comparing eccentric waveforms is to optimize over the template’s eccentric parameters
Ramos-Buades et al. 2022b
;
Knee et al. 2022
;
Gamboa et al. 2025b
;
Planas et al. 2025c
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2026
;
Thomas et al. 2026
;
Morras et al. 2026b
(another approach, based on time shifts, is presented in Ref.
Bonino et al. 2024
).
Here, we follow the procedure described in Sec. IV A 3 of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025b
, in which the faithfulness is numerically optimized over a grid of eccentricities and initial orbit-averaged frequencies with a fixed radial phase parameter.
Particularly, the radial phase is set to apastron,
\zeta=\pi
, as it avoids waveform-generation issues at high eccentricities across all the considered models.
This brute-force optimization is performed only for the
(2,2)
mode and for the lowest total mass under consideration.

When including higher-order modes, we use the
sky-and-polarization-averaged, signal-to-noise-ratio (SNR)-weighted faithfulness

Ossokine et al. 2020
;
Cotesta et al. 2018
,

\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{SNR}}(M,\iota_{\text{s}})=\sqrt[3]{\frac{\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi_{\text{s}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\kappa_{\text{s}}\ \mathcal{F}^{3}\ \text{SNR}^{3}}{\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi_{\text{s}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\kappa_{\text{s}}\ \text{SNR}^{3}}}\,,

(103)

where
\text{SNR}=\sqrt{\langle h_{\text{s}}|h_{\text{s}}\rangle}
.
These integrals are computed as a discrete sum over a uniform grid of
8\kern-0.80002pt\times\kern-0.80002pt8
values for
\varphi_{\textrm{s}},\kappa_{\text{s}}\in\nolinebreak{[0,\hskip 0.5pt2\pi]}
with a fixed value of
\iota_{\text{s}}
.

In the following subsections, we quote all our results in terms of the
unfaithfulness
(or
mismatch
) metric

\mathcal{M}\equiv 1-\mathcal{F}.

(104)

Accordingly, we denote
\mathcal{M}_{\ell m}
for individual
(\ell,m)
-mode mismatches, and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{SNR}}
for the SNR-weighted mismatches.
In this way, lower mismatch values imply better agreement between two waveforms for a given GW detector.

VII.2
Comparison against NR simulations

Figure 5:

Relative velocity (top panel) and separation (bottom panel) for a set of NR equal-mass, nonspinning BBH configurations with varying eccentricity:
QC (
SXS:BBH:2325
, blue), moderately eccentric (
SXS:BBH:2521
, yellow), highly eccentric (
SXS:BBH:2527
, red), dynamical capture (
SXS:BBH:4000
, violet), and scattering encounter (
SXS:BBH:4292
, orange).
The dashed line in the top panel marks
30\%
of the speed of light, underscoring the highly relativistic nature of eccentric systems.
The systems are aligned such that
t=0
corresponds to the peak of GW emission.
Here,
\mathbf{r}_{i}
and
\mathbf{v}_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) denote the position and velocity vectors of the two BHs.

In general, eccentric systems present a significant modeling challenge, as their periastron passages involve small separations and high relativistic velocities.
This is illustrated in Fig.
5
, which shows the relative velocity (top panel) and separation (bottom panel) for a subset of equal-mass, nonspinning NR simulations with varying eccentricities.
State-of-the-art approximants accurately model the GW emission from QC binaries (blue curves) up to velocities of order a few tenths of the speed of light (for reference, we indicate the value of
30\%
by a dashed line), corresponding to a certain number of orbits before merger.
As the eccentricity increases, however, the binary becomes progressively more relativistic at periastron. This is evident in moderately (yellow curves) and highly eccentric (red curves) simulations, whose periastron passages reach velocities comparable to those reached by QC binaries at the very end of their inspiral.
Importantly, these highly relativistic configurations arise even during the early inspiral of long-lived systems with moderate-to-high eccentricities, since the major change in the dynamics of these binaries occurs at apastron, while the velocity and separation at periastron remain relatively constant throughout the binaries’ evolution.
Therefore, accurate modeling of eccentric waveforms requires capturing the strong-field, high-velocity binary dynamics to prevent error accumulation at periastron passages.

Here, we quantify the accuracy of the
SEOBNRv6EHM
dynamics and waveforms for different BBH systems.
Specifically, we compute waveform mismatches against sets of QC and eccentric NR simulations, we compare against NR waveforms from a dynamical capture and scattering encounters, and we compute scattering angles for different configurations for which NR data is available.
All the NR waveforms and scattering angles were computed with the
SpEC
code
SpE
from the
SXS
collaboration
SXS
;
Boyle et al. 2019
;
Chu et al. 2016
;
Blackman et al. 2017
;
Hemberger et al. 2013
;
Scheel et al. 2015
;
Lovelace et al. 2015
;
Abbott et al. 2016b
;
Blackman et al. 2015
;
Lovelace et al. 2016
;
Varma et al. 2019a
;
Abbott et al. 2016c
;
Varma et al. 2019b
;
Kumar et al. 2015
;
Mroue et al. 2013
;
Yoo et al. 2022
;
Scheel et al. 2025
.
We also include predictions from the state-of-the-art eccentric, aligned-spin models
SEOBNRv5EHM

Gamboa et al. 2025b
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí

Nagar et al. 2024
.
For waveform comparisons, we include all modes supported by each model.
Namely, for
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
SEOBNRv6EHM
we use
(\ell,|m|)\in\nolinebreak\{(2,2),\,(3,3),\,(2,1),\,(4,4),\,(3,2),\,(4,3)\}
, and for
TEOBResumS-Dalí
we employ the modes
(\ell,|m|)\in\nolinebreak\{(2,2),\,(3,3),\,(2,1),\,(4,4)\}
.
11
11

11

In this work, we employ the
TEOBResumS-Dalí
model presented in Ref.
Nagar et al. 2024
, whose waveform modes
(\ell,m)\in\nolinebreak\{(2,2),(2,1),(3,3),(4,4)\}
for aligned-spin BBHs have been reviewed by the LVK collaboration.
We have used the code on the
LVK-Dali
branch from the repository

https://git.ligo.org/waveforms/software/teobresums
,
with git hash
5868b3a965ad4227ac99a3aaf2b348206ea94f48
.

For NR waveforms, we include all modes up to
\ell=4
.
Results for
SEOBNRv5HM

Pompili et al. 2023
in QC waveform comparisons are identical to those of
SEOBNRv5EHM
, and thus are not included.

VII.2.1
QC binaries

Figure 6:

Waveform mismatches between different approximants and the 592 QC
SXS
NR simulations used in this work, computed over the total mass range
M\in[10,300]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
.
Nonspinning cases are shown in orange and spinning cases in blue.
Columns 1–3 correspond to
SEOBNRv5EHM
,
SEOBNRv6EHM
, and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
, respectively.
The top panels show the
(2,2)
-mode mismatch
\mathcal{M}_{22}
, while the bottom panels display the sky-and-polarization averaged, SNR-weighted mismatch
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{SNR}}
(including higher-order modes) for an inclination
\iota_{\text{s}}=\pi/3
.
The red dot-dashed lines mark the median of the maximum mismatches,
\text{med}[\max_{M}\mathcal{M}]
, across the total mass range for each panel.

Figure 7:

Histograms of SNR-weighted mismatches, maximized over a specific total-mass range, for the
SEOBNRv6EHM
model against 592 QC (top panel) and 319 eccentric (bottom panel) NR
SXS
waveforms with inclination
\iota_{\mathrm{s}}=\pi/3
and
\ell\leq 4
, including
m=0
modes (dark purple) and excluding them (green).
The total-mass range spans
[10,300]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
for QC waveforms, and
[20,200]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
for eccentric waveforms.
An estimate of the NR error (gray) is also shown.

Figure 8:

Distribution of maximum mode-by-mode mismatches between
SEOBNRv5EHM
(blue) and
SEOBNRv6EHM
(red) against the NR surrogates
NRHybSur3dq8
(top panel) and
NRHybSur2dq15
(bottom panel), computed over the total mass range
M\in[10,300]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
across different, randomly-distributed binary QC configurations.
For
NRHybSur3dq8
, we take
5000
points with
q\leq 8
,
|\chi_{i}|\leq 0.9
(
i\in\nolinebreak\{1,2\}
), and
M\Omega=0.015
, while for
NRHybSur2dq15
, we take
5000
points with
q\leq 15
,
|\chi_{1}|\leq 0.6
,
\chi_{2}=0
, and
M\Omega=0.015
.

We compute waveform mismatches against a set of 592 BBH QC
SXS
NR waveforms
Scheel et al. 2025
.
The parameter space distribution of these waveforms is shown in Fig.
4
.
A summary of each NR waveform is provided in an ancillary file.
12
12

12

In the ancillary file, we list the mass ratio
q
, dimensionless spin components
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
, and the initial dimensionless orbital frequency
\langle M\Omega\rangle
.
Additionally, we provide the maximum
(2,2)
-mode mismatch
\max_{M}\mathcal{M}_{22}
(calculated over the range
M\in[10,300]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
) for each waveform model used in this work.

The
(2,2)
-mode mismatches are computed with Eq. (33) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025b
over a range of total masses
M\in\nolinebreak{[10,300]}\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
, and the results are shown in the top panels of Fig.
6
.
We observe an overall consistency between the accuracies of both
SEOBNR
models, with a slightly better median mismatch, a better control of mismatch outliers, and a slightly improved accuracy for nonspinning waveforms with
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
Additionally, we observe an overall accuracy improvement of one order of magnitude for both
SEOBNR
models with respect to
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
The most challenging cases correspond to systems with high mass ratios and/or high spins.
However, the mismatches of
SEOBNRv6EHM
for all these systems remain below
3\times 10^{-3}\!
, thanks to its analytical content, the higher-order fits of the calibration parameters in
SEOBNRv6EHM
, and the augmented calibration set with respect to
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
13
13

13

The accuracy of
SEOBNRv6EHM
degrades only slightly when using analogous fits and the same calibration set as in
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
The final fits and the selected NR simulations for the calibration of
SEOBNRv6EHM
were chosen to get the best performance across different binary configurations, as discussed in Sec.
VI
.

The accuracy of GW polarizations (with higher-order modes) is quantified with the sky-and-polarization-averaged, SNR-weighted faithfulness (
103
) [see also Eq. (32) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025b
], calculated for an inclination
\iota_{\text{s}}=\pi/3
, over the same range of
M\in[10,300]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
.
The corresponding mismatches are shown in the bottom panels of Fig.
6
.
For multipolar waveforms,
SEOBNRv6EHM
also has a better overall accuracy compared to
SEOBNRv5EHM
, and shows a significant improvement with respect to
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
In particular, the latter model underperforms in many cases, even resulting in mismatches of
\sim 40\%
.
By contrast, the mismatches of
SEOBNRv6EHM
remain always below
1\%
.
We note that all models exhibit reduced accuracy for systems with high mass ratios and/or high spins, as already pointed out, e.g., in Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
).
Furthermore, the models also show an increase in mismatch with respect to the total mass, indicating that the number of modeled higher-order modes and their accuracy (particularly in the late inspiral) need to be improved
Foo et al. 2026
.

We also observe a spread in the SNR-weighted mismatches of nonspinning waveforms (orange curves) for
SEOBNRv6EHM
relative to
SEOBNRv5EHM
(compare bottom-left and bottom-middle panels of Fig.
6
).
Thus, some multipolar nonspinning cases in
SEOBNRv6EHM
are underperforming.
In contrast, for the
(2,2)
mode, there is an overall improvement for nonspinning cases in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
This behavior is likely related to the arbitrary choices made when defining the Newtonian prefactors in Eqs. (
70
).
Since these mismatches stay below
\sim 10^{-3}
and are still comparable to those of
SEOBNRv5EHM
, we defer the investigation of improved Newtonian prefactors for higher-order modes to future work.

As a complementary study, we provide in the top panel of Fig.
7
histograms of maximum
SEOBNRv6EHM
SNR-weighted mismatches for all the QC NR waveforms with (dark purple bins) and without (green bins) the
m=0
multipoles, for
\ell\leq 4
.
We also include an estimate of the NR errors computed as the SNR-weighted mismatches between the highest and second-highest resolutions (always including the
m=0
modes), when available.
For the highest-mismatch cases, other sources of error (e.g., large spins) dominate over the absence of
m=0
modes in
SEOBNRv6EHM
(the same is true for
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
).
However, we observe that the lowest maximum mismatches are pushed toward values above
\sim 2\times 10^{-4}
, and we also see an increase in the number of cases with higher mismatches.
This explains why the mismatches shown in the bottom panels of Fig.
6
are higher than those presented in Refs.
Pompili et al. 2023
;
Gamboa et al. 2025b
, which did not include
m=0
modes in their comparison.
Overall, these results point to the importance of including
m=0
modes to achieve mismatches below
\sim 10^{-4}
.
This direction has only recently started to be explored within EOB modeling in Refs.
Albanesi 2025
;
Estelles et al. 2026
.

We provide a complementary accuracy study by comparing against QC NR surrogate models.
Both
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
SEOBNRv6EHM
have been calibrated against a subset of the NR waveforms used in Fig.
6
.
Thus, computing mismatches against waveforms outside the calibration set is an important step to consolidate the models’ accuracy and to discard overfitting.
For this purpose, we compare
SEOBNRv6EHM
against the
NRHybSur3dq8

Varma et al. 2019a
and
NRHybSur2dq15

Yoo et al. 2022
QC NR surrogate models, which are hybridized using the
SEOBNRv4

Bohé et al. 2017
and
SEOBNRv4HM

Cotesta et al. 2018
models, respectively.

NRHybSur3dq8
is a model for binaries with mass-ratios
q\in\nolinebreak{[1,8]}
and spin magnitudes
|\chi_{i}|\leq 0.8
(
i\in\{1,2\}
), while
NRHybSur2dq15
is a model for binaries with mass-ratios
q\in\nolinebreak{[1,15]}
, primary spin
|\chi_{1}|\leq 0.5
, and secondary spin
\chi_{2}=0
.
These surrogate models provide QC waveforms with errors comparable to the NR accuracy in the region of parameter space where the models were trained.

In Fig.
8
, we show the mode-by-mode comparisons of
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
SEOBNRv6EHM
against the NR surrogates
NRHybSur3dq8
(top panel) and
NRHybSur2dq15
(bottom panel), for randomly distributed points in a region of parameter space slightly larger than the training region of each surrogate model, to allow for some extrapolation.
Specifically, for the
NRHybSur3dq8
comparisons, we take
5000
points in the region determined by
q\leq 8
,
|\chi_{i}|\leq 0.9
(
i\in\{1,2\}
), and starting dimensionless orbital frequency of
M\Omega=0.015
(we choose this value to reduce the contributions from the hybridization of the NR surrogate in the early inspiral), while for the
NRHybSur2dq15
comparisons we take
5000
points with
q\leq 15
,
|\chi_{1}|\leq 0.6
,
\chi_{2}=0
, and
M\Omega=0.015
.
For each of these configurations, we compute the maximum mismatch of each
(\ell,m)
mode across an interval of total masses
M\in[10,300]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
, denoted as
\max_{M}\mathcal{M}
.

Overall, Fig.
8
shows that the accuracies of
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
SEOBNRv6EHM
are very similar across the considered regions of parameter space, with the most challenging configurations being binaries with high
q
and large spins, as before.
For certain cases,
SEOBNRv5EHM
performs better than
SEOBNRv6EHM
, but the opposite is also true.
Notably, both models have the same tails of large mismatches, particularly for the
(2,1)
,
(3,3)
, and
(4,3)
modes, for systems with large spins.
These high mismatches originate from the small amplitudes these modes can reach near merger, which conflicts with the methods for constructing and attaching the merger-ringdown part of the waveform, as already identified in Refs.
Cotesta et al. 2018
;
Pompili et al. 2023
.
Ongoing work is addressing these problems, in preparation for the accuracy requirements of next-generation GW detectors
Foo et al. 2026
.

These results show that the accuracy of
SEOBNRv6EHM
in the zero-eccentricity limit is at the same order as other state-of-the-art
dedicated
QC models.
14
14

14

The NR mismatches of other QC waveform models have been shown, e.g., in Fig. 7 of Ref.
Pompili et al. 2023
.
Such results were produced for a representative subset of the NR waveforms employed in this work, using Advanced LIGO’s PSD
Barsotti et al. 2018
with all NR modes up to
\ell=5
.

We remark that developing an eccentric model with an accurate QC limit is highly important to avoid biases in parameter estimation
Bonino et al. 2023
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2023a
.

e_{\text{gw}}
of each NR waveform measured by
gw_eccentricity
.
Nonspinning cases are shown with solid curves, and spinning cases with dashed curves.
Columns 1–3 correspond to
SEOBNRv5EHM
,
SEOBNRv6EHM
, and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
, respectively.
The top panels display the
(2,2)
-mode mismatch
\mathcal{M}_{22}
, while the bottom panels show the sky-and-polarization averaged, SNR-weighted mismatch
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{SNR}}
(including higher-order modes) for an inclination
\iota_{\text{s}}=\pi/3
.
The magenta dot-dashed lines mark the median of the maximum mismatches,
\text{med}[\max_{M}\mathcal{M}]
, across the total mass range for each panel.

), and the best-fit parameters for each model are provided in the ancillary file.

).

We validate the accuracy of
SEOBNRv6EHM
by computing waveform mismatches against a catalog of 319 BBH eccentric
SXS
NR waveforms.
Of these, 184 (composed of 159 nonspinning and 25 aligned-spin configurations) are publicly available in the latest
SXS
catalog
Scheel et al. 2025
, and 135 (composed of 107 nonspinning and 28 aligned-spin configurations) are private waveforms from Refs.
Ramos-Buades et al. 2022a
;
Nee et al. 2025a
.
15
15

15

Following the
SXS
catalog, we classify a NR simulation as
eccentric
if its reference eccentricity exceeds
0.001
, and as
nonspinning
if the dimensionless
z
-components of the spins satisfy
|\chi_{i}|<\nolinebreak 0.001
, with
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Thus, in total, we compare against 266 nonspinning and 53 aligned-spin eccentric NR waveforms, all of which are represented in Fig.
9
, characterized by their mass ratio
q
, dimensionless effective spin parameter
\chi_{\text{eff}}
, and their earliest GW eccentricity
e_{\text{gw}}
as defined in Refs.
Ramos-Buades et al. 2022a
;
Shaikh et al. 2023
.
16
16

16

We use version
1.0.2
of the
gw_eccentricity
package from the repository
https://github.com/vijayvarma392/gw_eccentricity
.
We note that the
earliest

e_{\text{gw}}
value is not the
initial
GW eccentricity of the waveform.
This is because
e_{\text{gw}}
is calculated only at the times where periastron and apoastron frequency interpolants are defined, thus avoiding extrapolation.
Throughout this work, we always report the earliest
e_{\text{gw}}
value as returned by
gw_eccentricity
.

We provide a summary of each of these NR waveforms in an ancillary file.
17
17

17

In the ancillary file, we list the mass ratio
q
, dimensionless spin components
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
, the GW eccentricity
e_{\text{gw}}
at the earliest
(2,2)
-mode orbit-averaged dimensionless frequency
\langle M\omega_{22}\rangle
calculated with
gw_eccentricity
, number of periastron passages
N_{p}
, and time to merger
t_{\text{merger}}/M
.
Additionally, we provide the optimum values of initial eccentricity
e_{0}
, initial dimensionless orbit-averaged orbital frequency
\langle M\Omega\rangle_{0}
, and maximum
(2,2)
-mode mismatch
\max_{M}\mathcal{M}_{22}
(calculated over the range
M\in[20,200]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
), corresponding to the best-fitting template for each eccentric waveform model employed in this work.

An additional characterization of the eccentric NR waveforms employed in this work is given in Fig.
10
.
For each NR waveform, we show the evolution of its mass-scaled (geometric) orbit-averaged
(2,2)
-mode frequency
\langle Mf_{22}\rangle
as a function of its GW eccentricity
e_{\text{gw}}
, and colored according to the value of the maximum
(2,2)
-mode mismatch corresponding to the best-fitting
SEOBNRv6EHM
waveform (to be discussed below).
This figure allows one to estimate the eccentricities of the NR waveforms at a given physical frequency for a specific total mass.
For example, the dashed horizontal line indicates the value
\langle Mf_{22}\rangle=0.01
which is the one employed in the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
for quoting the eccentricities in an injection-recovery study (see Fig. 3 therein).

We calculate mismatches against NR waveforms following the methods summarized in Sec.
VII.1
.
The three models,
SEOBNRv5EHM
,
SEOBNRv6EHM
, and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
, are treated equally to ensure a fair comparison.
The best-fitting parameters for each model and each eccentric NR simulation are provided in the ancillary file to this work (see footnote
17
).

The results are presented in Fig.
11
for the three models in separate columns.
Each panel shows the mismatch as a function of the total mass (
M\in[20,\hskip 0.5pt200]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
) corresponding to the best-fitting waveforms for each NR simulation.
The color of the curves corresponds to the initial GW eccentricity of the NR waveform, and we use dashed curves to indicate spinning configurations.
The top row shows the
(2,2)
-mode mismatch [calculated with Eq. (34) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025b
], while the bottom row displays the sky-and-polarization-averaged, SNR-weighted mismatch (which includes higher-order modes) at a source inclination of
\iota_{\text{s}}=\pi/3
[calculated with Eqs. (36) and (37) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025b
].
In each panel, we overlay a horizontal, dot-dashed, blue line indicating the corresponding median of all the maximum mismatches over the given total mass interval.
For the
(2,2)
-mode mismatches, the median is
0.029\%
for
SEOBNRv5EHM
,
0.023\%
for
SEOBNRv6EHM
, and
0.37\%
for
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
For the SNR-weighted mismatches, the median is
0.081\%
for both
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
SEOBNRv6EHM
, and
1.48\%
for
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.

As a complement, we provide in Fig.
12
plots that summarize the statistical information about the mismatches for each model.
The top panels show the maximum, median, and minimum mismatches calculated for each NR waveform over the employed range of total masses, with the left panel showing the
(2,2)
-mode mismatches, and the right panel displaying the SNR-weighted mismatches.
The bottom panels provide histograms for the maximum
(2,2)
-mode (left) and maximum SNR-weighted (right) mismatches over the same range of total masses for all the NR waveforms, including an estimate of their numerical error computed as the mismatch between the maximum and second-maximum resolutions, when available.

The results shown in Figs.
11
and
12
indicate that
SEOBNRv6EHM
is, overall, the most accurate model across the considered configurations.
In the low eccentricity limit, the mismatches of
SEOBNRv6EHM
are comparable to those of
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
Since many of the employed NR waveforms have low eccentricities, the minimum, medium, and maximum mismatches across the considered range of total masses remain very similar between these models, for both the
(2,2)
-mode and SNR-weighted mismatches (see Fig.
12
).
However, as eccentricity increases,
SEOBNRv5EHM
progressively loses accuracy, with mismatches rising to
\sim\nolinebreak 20\%
at the highest eccentricities.
In contrast,
SEOBNRv6EHM
keeps the maximum mismatches below
\sim 2\%
for
all
eccentric NR waveforms in the
SXS
catalog.
The difference in accuracy becomes even more pronounced when compared to
TEOBResumS-Dalí
, for which
SEOBNRv6EHM
achieves mismatches that are an order of magnitude smaller across all configurations.

We exemplify in Fig.
13
the accuracy of
SEOBNRv6EHM
when tested against the most eccentric simulation in the catalog,
SXS:BBH:2527
.
This corresponds to an equal-mass, nonspinning binary with a GW eccentricity of
e_{\text{gw}}\approx 0.88
at a dimensionless orbit-averaged
(2,2)
-mode frequency of
\langle M\omega_{22}\rangle=0.0069
, corresponding to a time near the second apoastron passage, approximately
t/M\sim-10500
before merger.
We display the real part and amplitude of the nonvanishing multipoles
\{(2,2),(3,2),(4,4)\}
for the best-fitting waveforms of the three models under consideration (the corresponding input parameters are given in the ancillary file; see footnote
17
).
For a total mass of
20\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
,
SEOBNRv6EHM
yields a mismatch of
\mathcal{M}_{22}=2.1\%
, while
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
give significantly larger values of
\mathcal{M}_{22}=19.5\%
and
\mathcal{M}_{22}=30.6\%
, respectively.

SEOBNRv6EHM
is therefore able to accurately capture the bursts of radiation at each periastron passage, where the relative velocity reaches up to
40\%
of the speed of light (see Fig.
5
).
In contrast, both
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
predict a less faithful evolution, characterized by wrong intervals of time between each periastron passage.
Moreover, these two models exhibit unphysical features in the higher-order modes:

SEOBNRv5EHM
shows an incorrect amplitude behavior during the early inspiral, while
TEOBResumS-Dalí
develops zero-crossings in the
(4,4)
mode near merger.
In comparison, the higher-order modes of
SEOBNRv6EHM
display a well-behaved evolution, even qualitatively outperforming the NR modes, which are affected by numerical noise due to their small amplitudes.

The accurate modeling of highly eccentric binaries is enabled thanks to the new analytical ingredients employed in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
Particularly, the novel resummation of the RR force, the choice of the RR gauge, and the use of a suitable parametrization, played a key role in achieving small mismatches.

SEOBNRv6EHM
employs 1PN eccentricity corrections to the RR force and waveform modes, compared to the 3PN expressions used in
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
Despite this,
SEOBNRv6EHM
yields a clear improvement for high eccentricities.
As discussed in Sec.
III.2.4
, better resummations and parametrizations are needed to include higher PN orders in a way which benefits the model accuracy.

Another aspect to note in Fig.
11
is that some spinning configurations are better modeled in
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
This behavior originates from the choice of the RR gauge (discussed in Sec.
III.2.3
).
During the development of
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we observed that using the
SEOBNRv5EHM
RR gauge (i.e.,
\alpha=-16/3
and
\beta=-13/2
) leads to the same improved mismatch behavior for these spinning configurations.
However, the mismatch for the highly eccentric configurations increases significantly (for example, the mismatch for the highly eccentric simulation
SXS:BBH:2527
goes from
\sim\nolinebreak 2\%
to
\sim\nolinebreak 15\%
, compared to the
\sim\nolinebreak 20\%
of
SEOBNRv5EHM
).
Furthermore, as commented in Sec.
III.2.3
, the
SEOBNRv5EHM
gauge could lead to numerical problems with our resummation of the RR force (
42
) (see Fig.
2
).
For these reasons, and considering that the mismatches of
SEOBNRv6EHM
for the most demanding spinning configurations are still within the
\sim\nolinebreak 2\%
level, we decided to keep a different RR gauge (namely,
\alpha=-2
and
\beta=-1
).
We additionally note that this behavior is not related to the QC spinning calibration.
For instance, one of the spinning simulations with high mismatch in
SEOBNRv6EHM

has a mass ratio
q=2
, dimensionless spins
\chi_{1}=0.8
and
\chi_{2}=0.8
, and initial GW eccentricity
e_{\text{GW}}=0.48
.
The QC counterpart
SXS:BBH:0618
has the same intrinsic parameters, but the maximum mismatch for
SEOBNRv6EHM
is
\sim 0.073\%
, while
SEOBNRv5EHM
has
\sim 0.084\%
(i.e.,
SEOBNRv6EHM
models slightly better this QC configuration).
Overall, these results point to the need for further investigation into the impact of the RR gauge on the accuracy of EOB models.

Apart from the RR force, the modeling of higher-order modes also requires improvement in future developments.
When transitioning from the
(2,2)
-mode metric to the SNR-weighted metric, the median of maximum mismatches increases from
0.030\%
to
0.081\%
(a factor of
\sim 2.7
) for
SEOBNRv5EHM
, from
0.023\%
to
0.084\%
(a factor of
\sim 3.6
) for
SEOBNRv6EHM
, and from
0.37\%
to
1.48\%
(a factor of
\sim 4
) for
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
Since the degradation of mismatches when including higher-order modes is larger in
SEOBNRv6EHM
than in
SEOBNRv5EHM
, this suggests that the prescription for higher-order modes in
SEOBNRv6EHM
may not be the most adequate.
As discussed in Secs.
V.1
and
VII.2.1
, there is a significant freedom in how the higher-order modes are modeled;
future work could focus on studying more accurate prescriptions.
However, we emphasize that the improvement for large eccentricities is also observed when higher-order modes are included.
Thus,
SEOBNRv6EHM
provides an overall better approximation for the full GW signal of eccentric binaries.

Figure 14:

Mismatch between NR waveforms with only the
(2,2)
mode and those including all modes up to
\ell=8
, for the 319 eccentric
SXS
waveforms used in this work, shown as a function of the mass ratio
q
and earliest GW eccentricity
e_{\text{gw}}
measured by
gw_eccentricity
.
The color indicates the sky-and-polarization-averaged SNR-weighted mismatch maximized over the total mass range
M\in[20,200]\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
for an inclination
\iota_{\text{s}}=\pi/3
.

The contribution of higher-order modes to the full waveform is, however, independent of eccentricity.
Figure
14
, shows the sky-and-polarization-averaged SNR-weighted mismatch between NR waveforms including only the
(2,2)
mode and those including all modes up to
\ell=8
, for an inclination
\iota_{\text{s}}=\pi/3
, as a function of the mass ratio and initial GW eccentricity (only NR waveforms are used to disentangle the results from inaccuracies in waveform models).
It is well known that large mass ratios and non-negligible inclinations enhance the contributions of higher-order modes in QC binaries (e.g.,
Cotesta et al. 2018
).
This is also the case for eccentric binaries, as shown in Fig.
14
, where we observe a baseline mismatch of
\sim 1\%
, increasing with mass ratio up to
\sim 20\%
.
However, the mismatch shows no dependence on eccentricity.
18
18

18

See also Fig. 11 of Ref.
Islam et al. 2026
for analogous results restricted to certain modes up to
\ell=5
and mass ratios up to
q=4
, obtained with a waveform approximant constructed from a NR surrogate model.
Additionally, the impact of neglecting higher-order modes in eccentric parameter-estimation analyses has recently been explored in Ref.
Tang et al. 2026
, where high mass ratios and inclinations lead to larger biases.
The role of eccentricity, however, is less clear and requires further investigation.

Indeed, orbital eccentricity does not introduce asymmetries on the sphere
(\iota,\varphi)
in which
h_{+}
and
h_{\times}
are expanded in spherical-harmonic modes
h_{\ell m}
.
Rather, eccentric binaries introduce an additional timescale characterized by the radial oscillations.
19
19

19

The higher-order modes of waveform decompositions based on
radial frequencies
do become more important as eccentricity increases.
This is the case for the typical decompositions employed in extreme-mass-ratio eccentric waveform models (e.g., see Fig. 4 in Ref.
Chapman-Bird et al. 2025
).

Therefore, the increase in mismatch with eccentricity observed in the bottom panels of Fig.
11
is entirely driven by inaccuracies in the binary phasing, rather than by the impact of higher-order modes or the absence of additional multipoles.
These results further suggest that improvements in the modeling of higher-order modes for QC binaries will be beneficial regardless of the eccentricity.

To complement this discussion about higher-order modes, we provide in the bottom panel of Fig.
7
histograms of maximum
SEOBNRv6EHM
SNR-weighted mismatches for all the eccentric NR waveforms with (dark purple bins) and without (green bins) the
m=0
multipoles, for
\ell\leq 4
.
We also display the estimated NR errors computed as the SNR-weighted mismatches between the highest and second-highest resolutions (always including the
m=0
modes), when available.
Similar to the case of QC binaries discussed in Sec.
VII.2.1
, the inclusion of NR
m=0
modes degrades the comparisons against NR, but the highest-mismatch cases are dominated by other sources of error (e.g., large spins, large eccentricities), rather than the absence of
m=0
modes in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
Although other sources of error (e.g., inaccuracies in the RR force) currently play a more significant role in limiting the accuracy for moderate-to-high eccentricities, low-eccentricity systems would immediately benefit from the modeling of
m=0
modes.
We leave this for future work.

We conclude by summarizing our results as follows:

•

SEOBNRv6EHM
matches the accuracy of
SEOBNRv5EHM
at low eccentricities, while at high eccentricities it can be up to an order of magnitude more accurate.

•

SEOBNRv6EHM
is overall an order of magnitude more accurate than
TEOBResumS-Dalí
for all eccentricities.

•

The improvements of
SEOBNRv6EHM
at high eccentricities come solely from new analytical results, and not from calibrations to eccentric NR simulations.
20
20

20

While no explicit numerical calibration is performed, it is important to note that different analytical choices were made based on comparisons against eccentric NR waveforms.

VII.2.3
Generic planar binaries

Modeling the GW emission from dynamically captured binaries or from scattering encounters poses a great challenge, since they probe higher velocities than QC or moderately-eccentric systems, as observed in the dynamics from NR simulations shown Fig.
5
.
The relative velocity of the considered dynamically captured binary (violet) reaches
\sim 55\%
the speed of light during the first and second periastron passages, at which the binary’s separation is
\sim 5M
.
Similarly, the relative velocity of the shown scattered binary (orange) reaches
\sim 65\%
the speed of light during the encounter at a separation of
\sim 5M
.
By contrast, the QC and eccentric (bound) systems reach
\sim 60\%
the speed of light
only
at the end of the plunge.

To quantify the accuracy of
SEOBNRv6EHM
for generic planar-orbit configurations, we compare with NR simulations.
Specifically, we compare with the NR waveforms from a dynamical capture (
SXS:BBH:4000
), a
90^{\circ}
scattering encounter (
SXS:BBH:3999
), and a
180^{\circ}
scattering encounter (
SXS:BBH:4292
), all publicly-available, equal-mass, nonspinning simulations from the
SXS
catalog
Scheel et al. 2025
.
Additionally, we employ data (predicted scattering angles) from Ref.
Long et al. 2025
, comprising 61
SXS
NR scattering encounters spanning a wide range of binary configurations, with varying energy, angular momentum, spins, and mass ratios.
In all the comparisons, we also include the predictions from the
TEOBResumS-Dalí
model
Nagar et al. 2024
.

The waveforms comparisons are provided in Fig.
15
.
The first set of panels (left column of the page) is dedicated to the dynamical capture, while the second and third sets of panels (right column of the page) focus on the
\sim 90^{\circ}
(top) and
\sim 180^{\circ}
(bottom) scattering encounters.
In each set of panels, we display the real part and amplitude of the non-vanishing modes, for the corresponding NR simulation (gray), along with the
SEOBNRv6EHM
(red) and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
(yellow) predictions.
To generate the waveforms, both models are initialized with the ADM energy and total angular momentum from the NR metadata, at an initial separation of
10^{3}M
.

The results in Fig.
15
suggest a better qualitative performance of
SEOBNRv6EHM
with respect to
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
For the dynamical capture case, we find that
SEOBNRv6EHM
yields better predictions for the time between periastron passages and the merger.
This results in an improved overall agreement with the NR waveforms, including the higher-order modes.
We note, however, that the NR higher-order modes exhibit unphysical features in the amplitude at early times, associated with the waveform extrapolation procedure used in the simulation.
In contrast, the higher-order modes of
SEOBNRv6EHM
show a smooth decay at early times and remain qualitatively consistent with the NR results close to merger, even reproducing the mode-mixing during the merger-ringdown phase of the
(3,2)
mode.
This is not the case for
TEOBResumS-Dalí
, where we see that the
(4,4)
mode displays nonsmooth behavior around
t=-1500M
, as well as amplitude zero crossings near merger.
For scattering waveforms,
TEOBResumS-Dalí
also develops unphysical features in the higher-order modes around
t\approx\pm 40M
, whereas
SEOBNRv6EHM
reproduces more closely the NR waveforms.

The predicted scattering angles for a wide range of binary configurations are shown in Fig.
16
.
Each panel shows the measured scattering angles from NR simulations (black dots with error bars), along with the predictions of
SEOBNRv6EHM
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
Additionally, we include the predictions by the
SEOBNRv5HM
model
Pompili et al. 2023
when given generic planar-orbit initial conditions
Long et al. 2025
.
The models are initialized with the ADM energy and total angular momentum from the NR metadata at an initial separation of
10^{6}M
, to ensure smaller errors in the EOB angles than in the NR data.
The scattering angle is computed as
\theta=\phi_{\text{f}}-\phi_{0}-\pi
, where
\phi_{\text{f}}
and
\phi_{0}
are the final and initial azimuthal orbital angles, respectively, returned by the models.

Figure 15:

Real part and amplitude of the waveform modes for an equal-mass, nonspinning BBH in different configurations:
a dynamical capture with
E/M=1.001
and
L/(\mu M)=4.02
(left panels), a scattering encounter of
\sim 90^{\circ}
with
E/M=1.02264
and
L/(\mu M)=6.4
(top-right panels), and a scattering encounter of
\sim\!180^{\circ}
with
E/M=1.02264
and
L/(\mu M)=4.8
(bottom-right panels), all shown in geometric units.
For each case, we show a
SXS
NR waveform (gray) alongside the corresponding
SEOBNRv6EHM
(red) and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
(yellow) predictions.
Only the nonvanishing
(\ell,m)
modes supported by the models are shown (the
(3,2)
mode of
TEOBResumS-Dalí
is not reviewed, see footnote
11
).
Both models are initialized with the same energy and angular momentum as the corresponding NR simulation, at an initial separation of
r=10^{3}M
.

for six sets of binary configurations as functions of mass ratio, spins, energy

, reduced orbital angular momentum

, effective energy

, and impact parameter

.
Data are extracted from NR simulations
Long et al. 2025
(black dots with error bars) and compared with the predictions of

(blue),

(red), and

(yellow);
vertical dotted lines mark the first known dynamical capture for each model.
In each panel, the upper plot shows

, while the lower plot displays the relative difference with respect to NR;
zoomed insets are included where useful for clarity.

L/(\mu M)
at

;

;

\chi_{\text{S}}
at

and

;

\chi_{\text{A}}
at
E/M=1.055
and
L/(\mu M)=5.18
;
nonspinning binaries varying mass ratio at

and

;
and nonspinning binaries varying mass ratio at
\gamma=1.0916
and
