.

The results shown in Fig.
16
indicate that
SEOBNRv6EHM
provides slightly more accurate predictions of the scattering angle than
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
In most cases, the scattering angles and predicted captures (dotted vertical lines) from
SEOBNRv6EHM
are closer to the NR data.
However, this improvement is less pronounced than in QC and eccentric (bound) configurations.
Remarkably, the best performance is achieved by
SEOBNRv5HM
, as already reported in Ref.
Long et al. 2025
.

SEOBNRv5HM
shares the same Hamiltonian structure as
SEOBNRv6EHM
, but has different calibration parameters and employs a QC RR force.
One might naively expect that employing an eccentric RR force would improve the modeling of scattering encounters, but this is not the case (at least not with our chosen resummation).
During the development of
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we observed an improvement in the predicted scattering angles when higher PN orders were included in the eccentricity corrections to the RR force (beyond the 1PN order used in
SEOBNRv6EHM
).
However, as discussed in Sec.
III.2.4
, increasing the PN order of these corrections results in a degradation of accuracy for bound systems.

Taken together, our findings for the generic planar-orbit waveforms and scattering angles underscore the need for further investigation into alternative resummations of the waveform modes and the RR force that accurately apply to both bound and unbound binaries.
This, together with a more systematic investigation of model accuracy for unbound orbits, is left for future work.
For now, conclusions drawn from generic planar-orbit results obtained with
SEOBNRv6EHM
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
should be interpreted with caution.

We conclude that
SEOBNRv6EHM
demonstrates a strong performance across all configurations, marking an important step toward accurate models for generic-orbit binaries.

VII.3
Computational performance

Waveform generation in eccentric models is computationally more expensive than their QC counterparts due to the extra equations and functions required to model eccentricity.
Furthermore, eccentric binaries depend on two extra parameters with respect to QC binaries (e.g., eccentricity and a radial phase).
Thus, strategies and codes that reduce the overall computational cost of eccentric models need to be developed.

SEOBNRv6EHM
is implemented within the high-performance and flexible
pySEOBNR
Python package
Mihaylov et al. 2023
, which has been optimized to reduce waveform generation time.
The optimizations consisted mainly of reducing redundancy in the original code (as used in
SEOBNRv5
models) and using
Cython

Behnel et al. 2011
to speed up the evaluation of cost-intensive expressions.
Furthermore,
SEOBNRv6EHM
evolves only four equations of motion (
15
) and employs compact expressions for the RR force and modes, compared to
SEOBNRv5EHM
, which evolves six equations of motion and uses longer expressions.

Figure 17:

Walltime for waveform generation of
SEOBNRv5HM
(dashed),
SEOBNRv5PHM
(dash-dotted),
SEOBNRv5EHM
(solid-dotted), and
SEOBNRv6EHM
(solid), as function of the total mass
M\in\nolinebreak{[5,\,100]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}}
.
The systems are characterized by a starting orbit-averaged frequency
\langle f_{\text{start}}\rangle=10\,
Hz, dimensionless spin components
\chi_{1}=0.8
and
\chi_{2}=0.3
, and three different mass ratios
q\in\{1,3,10\}
.
For
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
SEOBNRv6EHM
, the systems are initialized at apastron (
\zeta_{0}=\pi
) with different starting eccentricities
e_{0}\in\{0,0.01,0.1,0.3,0.5\}
, each represented by a different color.

Figure 18:

Histograms of waveform-evaluation walltimes for different models and for randomly distributed QC (top panel) and eccentric (bottom panel) binary configurations, initialized at an orbit-averaged frequency of
\langle f_{\text{start}}\rangle=10\,
Hz with a fixed sampling rate of
2048\,
Hz.
For the QC parameter space, we sample
5000
points with
q\in[1,10]
,
M\in[5,100]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
, and
|\chi_{i}|\leq 0.9
(
i\in\{1,2\}
, aligned-spins). For the eccentric parameter space, we sample
10000
points with the same mass and spin ranges, and with
e\in[0,0.5]
and
\zeta\in[0,2\pi]
.
The vertical dashed lines indicate the median of the corresponding histogram.

Here, we quantify the speed of
SEOBNRv6EHM
and compare it with other state-of-the-art models across various binary configurations typically expected for current LVK detectors.
For all the models below, we include all their supported modes up to
\ell=4
, with the remaining settings kept as default.
The benchmarks were performed with the
Hypatia
computer cluster at the Max Planck Institute for Gravitational Physics in Potsdam, on a compute node equipped with a dual-socket 64-core AMD EPYC (Rome) 7742 CPU.

In Fig.
17
, we show the walltimes for waveform generation (time-domain GW polarizations) as a function of the binary’s total mass
M\in[5,\,100]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
for three mass ratios
q\in\{1,3,10\}
, and for four waveform approximants:
the QC aligned-spin
SEOBNRv5HM
model
Pompili et al. 2023
, the QC precessing-spin
SEOBNRv5PHM
model
Ramos-Buades et al. 2023b
, and the eccentric aligned-spin models
SEOBNRv5EHM

Gamboa et al. 2025b
and
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
The considered configurations correspond to aligned-spin binaries with dimensionless spin components
\chi_{1}=0.8
and
\chi_{2}=0.3
, starting at an orbit-averaged frequency
\langle f_{\text{start}}\rangle=10\,
Hz.
For the eccentric models, the binaries are initialized with different eccentricities
e_{0}\in\nolinebreak\{0,0.01,0.1,0.3,0.5\}
at apastron
\zeta_{0}=\pi
.
For each total mass, we choose a sampling rate that satisfies the Nyquist criterion for the
\ell=4
multipoles.
This is reflected as discontinuities in the curves shown in Fig.
17
, since smaller sampling rates lead to faster waveform generations.
For systems with
e_{0}>0
, the discontinuities are less pronounced, as the computational cost of modeling eccentricity dominates over the differences caused by the change in sampling rate.
Related to this, for both
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
SEOBNRv6EHM
, we observe in Fig.
17
an increase of walltimes as the eccentricity is augmented, until a certain point at which a further increase of eccentricity decreases the generation time.
This is counterintuitive to the fact that, at fixed orbit-averaged frequency, an increase in eccentricity leads to a decrease in waveform duration due to larger losses of energy and angular momentum at periastron passages.
This effect was documented in Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025b
and, in summary, can be explained by the balance between the numerical integrator resolving the eccentricity timescales and the waveforms becoming shorter as the eccentricity increases.

Additionally, we show in Fig.
18
histograms of the walltimes for waveform generation (time-domain GW polarizations) for randomly distributed configurations in the QC (top panel) and eccentric (bottom panel) parameter spaces, for different waveform approximants.
For the QC configurations, we take
5000
random points with
q\in[1,10]
,
M\in\nolinebreak{[5,\,100]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}}
,
|\hskip 0.5pt\hskip 0.5pt\chi_{i}|\leq 0.9
(
i\in\{1,2\}
, aligned-spins), while for the eccentric configurations, we take
10000
random points with the same bounds for masses and spins but with
e\in[0,0.5]
and
\zeta\in[0,2\pi]
.
For both cases, we start the waveforms at
\langle f_{\text{start}}\rangle=10\,
Hz with a fixed sampling rate of
2048\,
Hz.

In the zero-eccentricity limit, both figures show that the performance of
SEOBNRv6EHM
is markedly close to that of
SEOBNRv5HM
, and is significantly better than that of
SEOBNRv5PHM
and
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
These are remarkable properties, as the
SEOBNRv6EHM
code includes several additional functions for modeling generic planar-orbit waveforms.
Particularly,
SEOBNRv6EHM
evolves
two
binary dynamics (a main dynamics and a background QC evolution that helps in the construction of inspiral-merger-ringdown waveforms; see Sec.
V.2
).
In fact,
SEOBNRv5HM
is faster only because it uses a post-adiabatic (PA) evolution of the dynamics.
In contrast,
SEOBNRv6EHM
does not use any simplification for the main binary dynamics (it only uses a PA prescription for the background QC evolution).
This encourages the development of analytical methods to accelerate the EOB dynamics of eccentric binaries
Lynch et al. 2026
, since they will bring QC and eccentric models to a common computational performance.

For eccentric configurations, the computational efficiency of
SEOBNRv6EHM
outperforms that of
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
Figure
17
shows explicitly that, for
all
cases,
SEOBNRv6EHM
is significantly faster than
SEOBNRv5EHM
.
Both models have a similar definition of eccentricity (differing only in the RR gauge), yielding nearly equal-duration waveforms for the same input parameters (with the exception of high-eccentricity systems, not included in these tests).
This justifies a fair timing comparison between the two models when evaluated at the same point in parameter space.
In contrast, due to a different eccentricity treatment, waveforms generated with
TEOBResumS-Dalí
are typically shorter than those of
SEOBNRv6EHM
(and
SEOBNRv5EHM
) when given the same input parameters.
This precludes a comparison similar to that in Fig.
17
, unless the eccentric parameters of one of the models are optimized to find the most adequate waveform for each considered case.
For this reason, we find it more convenient to compare models by evaluating them at random points across a given region of parameter space, as shown in Fig.
18
.
There, it is straightforward to see the waveform-evaluation improvement of
SEOBNRv6EHM
over
SEOBNRv5EHM
(by factors of
\sim 2-4
) and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
(by factors of
\sim 2-6
) across all configurations, including binaries with high mass ratios and small total masses, which compose the tail of high walltimes in the histograms of Fig.
18
.
21
21

21

We note that
TEOBResumS-Dalí
supports only four multipoles,
\{(2,2),\,(2,1),\,(3,3),\,(4,4)\}
, whereas both
SEOBNR
models additionally include the
(3,2)
and
(4,3)
modes.
Thus, Fig.
18
shows that
SEOBNRv6EHM
is faster, even though it returns additional multipoles.

These results indicate that
SEOBNRv6EHM
is faster than all EOB eccentric models.
Our benchmarks are complemented by results from the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
.
There, it is shown that
SEOBNRv6EHM
can be up to
\sim 3
times faster than
SEOBNRv5EHM
in GW parameter estimation.
Hence, we expect
SEOBNRv6EHM
to open the possibility of cost-intensive investigations that require accurate eccentric waveforms.

VII.4
Robustness across parameter space

Figure 19:

Contour plots of the initial separation as a function of the eccentricity and frequency for an equal-mass, nonspinning BBH, as predicted by
SEOBNRv6EHM
and Eq. (34c) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
, along with a particular EOB dynamics and waveform.
The values in the top panel are computed for binaries initialized at periastron (
\zeta=0
), while those in the middle panel correspond to initialization at apastron (
\zeta=\pi
).
The red dashed curves separate regions where waveform evaluation is successful (below) from those where it fails (above), due to constraints imposed to ensure physically sensible waveforms.
For reference, we also show the corresponding total mass
M
at a GW orbit-averaged frequency of
\langle f_{\text{start}}\rangle=10\,
Hz.
The bottom panels display the trajectory (left) and
(2,2)
mode (right) for a representative boundary configuration (pink cross in the middle panel).

Studying the behavior of a waveform model across the binary parameter space is crucial for establishing its reliability.
Here, we analyze the robustness of
SEOBNRv6EHM
by identifying the parameter space regions where we expect the model to generate physically meaningful waveforms, and by studying waveform changes under variations of the input parameters.

Determining initial conditions for generic planar orbits is challenging, as one must know precise instantaneous values of the relative separation and momenta of the binary.
To ensure physically accurate initial conditions,
SEOBNRv6EHM
imposes a range of constraints, as discussed in Sec.
IV
.
The excluded initial conditions are always associated with challenging configurations, for example, very small separations or states very close to merger.
As an example, we show in Fig.
19
the predicted initial separation for an equal-mass, nonspinning binary as a function of the starting eccentricity and frequency, as predicted by
SEOBNRv6EHM
, complemented with a PN formula (see below).
The top (middle) panel shows the predicted separation when the binary is initialized at periastron (
\zeta=0
) [apastron (
\zeta=\pi
)].
For reference, besides the dimensionless orbit-averaged orbital frequency
\langle M\Omega\rangle
, we also indicate the total mass
M
of the binary when the physical orbit-averaged GW frequency is equal to
10\,
Hz.
The red dashed curves delineate the regions where
SEOBNRv6EHM
waveform generation is successful (below the curves) from those where it fails (above the curves); these curves were calculated numerically, by iterating model evaluations over the eccentricity.
The separation is calculated using Eq. (34c) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
,
22
22

22

Intuitively, one would expect the apastron distance to increase with eccentricity at fixed frequency.
However, this is not observed in the contours of the bottom panel of Fig.
19
for, e.g.,
\langle M\Omega\rangle\approx 0.04
and
e\approx 0.8
.
This unintuitive behavior arises from the 3PN terms from Eq. (34c) of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
;
removing such terms gives the contours the expected behavior.
We cannot assess whether this is a true prediction for the apastron distance or an artifact of the PN expansion applied at high eccentricities.

but we verified that plotting the true
SEOBNRv6EHM
predicted separation results in the same plots (for the regions below the red dashed curves).
Additionally, in the bottom panels, we include the EOB trajectory and the
(2,2)
mode for one representative boundary case (indicated by a pink cross in the middle panel).
For other configurations (unequal mass ratios, nonzero spins, or different frequencies), the qualitative behavior of the initial separation is analogous to the one observed in Fig.
19
.

It is crucial to note that not all input values
(\langle M\Omega\rangle,\,e,\,\zeta)
will produce a successful waveform generation.
Some combinations are not physically meaningful (e.g., initial separations below
2M
), or correspond to configurations resembling head-on collisions.
This is illustrated in the bottom panels of Fig.
19
, which show that higher eccentricities result in shorter-duration systems that fall outside the model’s assumptions.
QC models overcome this issue by exploiting the system’s circular symmetry, thereby determining initial conditions at lower frequencies (which still correspond to the
same
physical system).
An eccentric binary, however, cannot be trivially evolved backward as its dynamical state depends not only on the frequency, but on the eccentricity and radial anomaly.

This is a problem common to all eccentric models, and it is important to take it into account when drawing conclusions from their results (e.g., when observing posterior railing in parameter estimation
Gupte et al. 2026
).
The
SEOBNRv5EHM
model addresses this problem by integrating backward a set of PN secular evolution equations whenever the starting separation is smaller than
10M
.
In this way, EOB initial conditions can be determined at an earlier (less challenging) stage of the evolution.
This approach was needed in
SEOBNRv5EHM
to avoid issues with its equations of motion.
Nonetheless, a study assessing its impact (e.g., in parameter estimation) has not yet been performed.
Such method, however, turned out to be highly non-user-friendly, as it gives the model an unexpected behavior from the user perspective, and its purpose is not obvious to someone outside the eccentric waveform modeling field.
For these reasons, and thanks to the simpler analytical structure of the
SEOBNRv6EHM
equations of motion, we do not adopt the secular backward integration approach (not to be confused with the backward evolution of the
full
equations of motion, as explained in footnote
6
).
This results in a smoother model behavior across the parameter space.
As demonstrated in the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
, this choice does not negatively affect parameter estimation, as all the runs there were executed seamlessly (some even with fewer waveform generation failures than
SEOBNRv5EHM
), including high-eccentricity injection-recovery analyses with upper priors on eccentricity of
0.9
(we also used an upper prior on eccentricity of
0.99
for some additional analyses, finding no issues with the runs or their convergence).
Thus,
SEOBNRv6EHM
enables accurate high-eccentricity parameter estimation studies.

Apart from the excluded challenging initial configurations, the parameter-space coverage of
SEOBNRv6EHM
has
no
inherent restrictions.
The
SEOBNRv5EHM
model had to be constrained to a conservative region of parameter space to avoid a “desynchronization” of its equations of motion that can potentially appear for highly-eccentric, long-lived systems (see Appendix E of Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025b
).
In
SEOBNRv6EHM
, no such constriction is needed, as the equations of motion (
15
) are well-defined and self-consistent (this is one of the major improvements with respect to
SEOBNRv5EHM
).
Thus,
SEOBNRv6EHM
can be used anywhere in parameter space, with the usual caveats of models.
For example, we expect reduced accuracy for configurations that differ significantly from those used to calibrate the model (e.g., high mass ratios or large spins; see Sec.
VI
),
and reduced accuracy as eccentricity increases.

Figure 20:

Uniform variation of eccentricity (left panels), relativistic anomaly (middle panels), and mean anomaly (right panels) for the different
SEOBNRv6EHM
waveform mode amplitudes (in geometric units).
Each of these waveforms is characterized by a mass ratio

, dimensionless spin components
\chi_{1}=0.6
and

(equivalent, e.g., to a starting GW frequency

).
The systems in the left panels are initialized at apastron (

), and the eccentricity is varied uniformly from
e_{0}=0
(dark blue) to
e_{0}=0.7
(yellow).
The systems in the middle and right panels are initialized with eccentricity
e_{0}=0.4
, and the anomalies are varied uniformly from
0
(dark blue) to
2\pi
(yellow).
In the left panels, we include insets of the zoomed-in merger-ringdown portions of the waveform modes to ease visualization, while in the middle and right panels, we directly show the ending portions to better illustrate the dependence on the radial anomaly.
The transformation from mean anomaly to relativistic anomaly for the waveforms shown in the right panels is done under a Newtonian approximation using Eqs. (
51
) and (
52
), as the intrinsic (and default) radial phase parameter in
SEOBNRv6EHM
is the relativistic anomaly.

Figure 21:

Variation of the GW polarizations,
h_{+}
and
h_{\times}
(in geometric units), with respect to the energy
E/M
(top panel) and total angular momentum
J/M^{2}
(bottom panel), as predicted by
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
Each of these waveforms belongs to a BBH system with mass ratio
q=3

.
The specific energy values employed in the top panel are

J/(\mu M)\in\{4.99,4.992,4.994,4.996,4.998,5\}

.
We also include insets of zoomed-in portions of the waveforms in the top panel to ease visualization.

Building on the LVK review tests carried out for
SEOBNRv5EHM
, we successfully assessed the robustness of
SEOBNRv6EHM
across broad parameter space regions covering mass ratios
q\in[1,100]
, the full spin range, and eccentric parameters
(e,\hskip 0.5pt\zeta)
for bound orbits, as well as
(r,\hskip 0.5ptE/M,\hskip 0.5ptJ/M^{2})
for generic planar orbits.
Namely, we performed visual inspection of the waveforms, stability under perturbations of the input parameters, checks of the mode hierarchy (ensuring that the
(2,2)
mode has the largest amplitude), and verification of the monotonicity of the inspiral amplitude and frequency evolution for the QC
(2,2)
mode, as well as of the envelope defined by the maxima and minima of the eccentric
(2,2)
mode.

For example, in Fig.
20
, we show the smooth changes of the amplitude of all the
SEOBNRv6EHM
waveform modes,
(\ell,|m|)=\{(2,2),\,(3,3),\,(2,1),\,(4,4),\,(3,2),\,(4,3)\}
, under uniform variations of the eccentric parameters
e\in[0,\hskip 0.5pt0.7]
(left panels),
\zeta\in[0,\hskip 0.5pt2\pi]
(relativistic anomaly, middle panels), and
l\in[0,\hskip 0.5pt2\pi]
(mean anomaly, right panels), at a dimensionless orbit-averaged orbital frequency of
\langle M\omega\rangle\approx 0.01547
(equivalent, e.g., to an initial GW frequency
\langle f_{\text{start}}\rangle=20\,
Hz for a total mass
M=50\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
) for a system with mass ratio
q=3
, and dimensionless spin components
\chi_{1}=0.6
and
\chi_{2}=-0.3
.

In the left panels of Fig.
20
, we observe the expected decrease in the time to merger with increasing eccentricity at fixed starting frequency, while in the middle and right panels, we observe the amplitude oscillations induced by the radial anomaly.
The middle panels show that a uniform distribution on relativistic anomaly leads to a clustering of the amplitude around periastron at
\zeta\in\{0,2\pi\}
.
In contrast, the right panels show that a uniform distribution on mean anomaly leads to evenly distributed waveform amplitudes.
These observations are complemented by Fig.
3
in Sec.
IV.1
, where one can see the effects of uniformly sampling in either of the two anomalies (see also Fig. 5 of Ref.
Nee et al. 2025a
for the effect of mean anomaly in NR waveforms).
In the context of surrogate modeling of eccentric waveforms, the mean anomaly approach has been quite fruitful
Nee et al. 2025b
;
Maurya et al. 2025
.
However, the impact of using either anomaly on parameter estimation still needs to be addressed.
For the present, we chose the relativistic anomaly
\zeta
as the default radial phase parameter in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.

In Fig.
20
, all cases show analogous behavior during merger-ringdown due to the employed QC prescription.
The true behavior at merger has a dependence on eccentricity and radial anomaly, as illustrated in Fig. 5 of Ref.
Nee et al. 2025a
(see also Refs.
Radia et al. 2021
;
Wang et al. 2024
;
Wang et al. 2023a
;
Wang et al. 2025
;
Carullo et al. 2024
;
Carullo 2024
;
Rao and Carullo 2026
).
These effects are relatively small, so we neglect them and leave their inclusion for future work.
Further investigation is required to characterize the impact of eccentric merger-ringdown effects on waveform accuracy and parameter estimation.

Going beyond bound orbits, we show in Fig.
21
the changes of the waveform polarizations,
h_{+}
and
h_{\times}
, under variations of the input parameters
E/M
and
J/M^{2}
for generic planar orbits, for systems with mass ratio
q=3
, dimensionless spin components
\chi_{1}=0.3
and
\chi_{2}=-0.4
, initial separation
r=500M
, and a line-of-sight inclination of
\iota=\pi/3
.
Specifically, the top panel shows the changes with respect to a set of energy values
E/M\in\{1.0005,1.0008,1.00105,1.0016\}
at fixed total angular momentum
J/(\mu M)=5
, while the bottom panel show the changes with respect to different total angular momentum values
J/(\mu M)\in\{4.99,4.992,4.994,4.996,4.998,5\}
at fixed energy
E/M=1.000001
.
To ease visualization, we show insets of zoomed-in portions of the waveforms in the top panel.

A particularly interesting behavior observed in the top panel of Fig.
21
is the transition from dynamical capture to a scattering encounter.
Starting from the dynamically captured system with
E/M=1.0005
(dark blue curves), we observe that increasing the energy delays the subsequent periastron passages to later times, until the system becomes unbound (light green curves, with
E/M=1.0016
).
The precise threshold value between scattering and dynamical capture for this system lies between
E/M=1.00105
and
E/M=1.0016
.
We remark that the smoothness properties exemplified in Fig.
21
are important for studies analyzing dynamical-capture and scattering waveforms
East et al. 2013
;
Mukherjee et al. 2021
;
Gamba et al. 2023a
;
Morrás et al. 2022
;
Bini et al. 2024
;
Henshaw et al. 2025
;
Smith et al. 2025
;
Lange et al. 2026
.
As a proof of principle, in the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
, we have successfully performed parameter estimation over different high-mass events using generic planar-orbit initial conditions.

Overall, the behavior of
SEOBNRv6EHM
remains robust across different parameter space regions.
This robustness ensures the model can be used straightforwardly in different GW studies.
Better prescriptions for initial conditions are needed to expand the parameter space of eccentric waveform models.
However, new data analysis techniques are also needed to enable robust eccentric GW inference.
We expect
SEOBNRv6EHM
to play a role in supporting these future investigations.

VIII
Conclusions

Eccentric GW models will play a key role in discriminating compact binary formation channels and in achieving unbiased parameter estimation.
Here, we presented
SEOBNRv6EHM
: a new multipolar, time-domain, EOB model for the complete GW radiation of comparable-mass BBHs orbiting in generic planar orbits.
The model leverages new resummations of the RR force and waveform modes, and it provides the multipoles
(\ell,|m|)\in\{(2,2),\,(3,3),\,(2,1),\,(4,4),\,(3,2),\,(4,3)\}
for the full inspiral-merger-ringdown evolution of coalescing binaries, dynamical captures, and scattering encounters.
The model has been calibrated to QC
SXS
NR simulations, and it constructs the merger-ringdown under a QC approximation.

SEOBNRv6EHM
is
accurate
.
In the low-eccentricity limit, its performance is comparable to that of
SEOBNRv5EHM

Gamboa et al. 2025b
, while for the largest eccentricities it improves the accuracy by up to an order of magnitude.

SEOBNRv6EHM
achieves a median of
\sim 0.02\%
for the maximum mismatches across the total mass interval
[20,200]\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}
when comparing against 319 eccentric
SXS
NR waveforms
Scheel et al. 2025
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2022a
;
Nee et al. 2025a
.
Importantly, mismatches remain below
\sim 2\%
even for the most eccentric NR configurations.
Relative to
TEOBResumS-Dalí

Nagar et al. 2024
, we observe an overall improvement of about one order of magnitude across all eccentricities.
In the generic planar-orbit regime, we compare against NR waveforms from one dynamical capture and two scattering encounters, as well as against 61 scattering-angle results from Ref.
Long et al. 2025
, finding improved agreement relative to
TEOBResumS-Dalí
.
The high accuracy of
SEOBNRv6EHM
is further demonstrated in the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
, where its capability to accurately infer the parameters of highly eccentric binaries is established through injection-recovery studies with synthetic NR signals.

SEOBNRv6EHM
is
computationally efficient
.
We performed benchmarks to assess the computational speed of
SEOBNRv6EHM
across a broad range of binary configurations.
Thanks to code optimizations and to a simpler analytical structure,
SEOBNRv6EHM
can be
2-6
times faster than
SEOBNRv5EHM
and
TEOBResumS-Dalí
in single waveform evaluations, depending on the specific binary parameters.
The improved efficiency of
SEOBNRv6EHM
in Bayesian parameter estimation is demonstrated in the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
, where the model is shown to be approximately three times faster than its predecessor,
SEOBNRv5EHM
.

SEOBNRv6EHM
is
robust
.
The parameter space covered by
SEOBNRv6EHM
has no inherent restrictions, apart from systems with very high eccentricity near merger.
This is a large improvement over
SEOBNRv5EHM
, whose parameter space coverage had to be restricted to avoid unphysical features in the waveforms, mainly occurring for long-lived, highly eccentric systems.
Leveraging this robustness and the improved computational efficiency, the companion manuscript
Pompili et al. 2026
presents analyses of
26
GW events spanning a wide range of configurations, including high mass ratios, low total masses (including NSs), and large eccentricities. These results show that
SEOBNRv6EHM
is readily applicable to standard GW analyses.

Several subsequent studies can be made to improve eccentric EOB modeling.
Further research on the RR force will be essential, as it is one of the main accuracy bottlenecks.
The current paradigm of enforcing a QC factorization in resummations of the RR force and waveform modes may, in fact, be introducing limitations in the modeling of eccentric systems.
The sigmoid resummation introduced in this work may prove useful for developing new approaches.
Studying different RR gauges could be important for achieving better accuracy across different binary configurations.
The final portion of the waveform could be improved by incorporating recent developments in the modeling of the eccentric merger-ringdown phase (e.g.,
Carullo et al. 2024
;
Carullo 2024
;
Nee et al. 2025a
;
Faggioli et al. 2026
;
Albanesi et al. 2026
;
Rao and Carullo 2026
).
Developing a framework for calibrating EOB models to eccentric NR simulations is a challenging but promising avenue that will require careful control over the eccentric parameters.
Finally, testing the impact of post-Minkowskian results on the accuracy of inspiral-merger-ringdown waveforms remains an open problem that has been addressed only in the QC-orbit limit
Buonanno et al. 2024
;
Damour et al. 2025
.

Our next goal is to develop an accurate and efficient waveform model for generic-orbit,
precessing-spin
BBHs, using
SEOBNRv6EHM
as baseline model
Gamboa et al. 2026
.
In this context, there has been recent work in developing inspiral-only PN waveform models
Klein et al. 2018
;
Klein 2021
;
Arredondo et al. 2024
;
Morras et al. 2025b
;
Morras et al. 2026b
, as well as inspiral-merger-ringdown EOB waveform models
Liu et al. 2023b
;
Gamba et al. 2024
.
Such generic models will be essential to allow for
unbiased
BBH parameter estimation.

Appendix A
Expressions at high PN orders

In this appendix, we provide expressions for various quantities derived throughout this work.
Section
A.1
derives rules to transform EOB variables to the parametrization employed in the eccentricity corrections to the RR force and waveform modes.
Section
A.2
presents the PN-expanded RR force components derived from flux-balance laws.
Section
A.3
discusses the sigmoid resummation and its use in the eccentricity corrections of the RR force,
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}
.
Section
A.4
presents the eccentricity corrections
h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{ecc}}
to the factorized waveform modes, and discusses a possible generalization of the source term
\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}
.
Finally, Section
A.5
presents a PN-expanded expression of the orbital frequency for circular orbits
\Omega_{\text{c}}(r)
.
Throughout this section, we employ dimensionless variables to simplify notation.
To restore units, one can employ the relations in Eqs. (
9
).

We define the conservative part of
\dot{p}_{r_{*}}
as

\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\equiv-\xi(r)\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial r}(r,p_{r_{*}},p_{\phi}).

(105)

We also define

p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}\equiv\sqrt{r+r^{3}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}}\,,

(106)

to simplify some expressions below.
Note that
p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}
reduces to the orbital angular momentum in the Newtonian limit.

A.1
Transformation to
(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{cons}})

Here, we write the 3PN relations employed to transform the EOB variables
(p_{r},p_{\phi},\Omega)
that appear in the RR force and waveform modes, into
(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}=-\xi\,\partial H_{\text{EOB}}/\partial r)
.
The latter is a convenient parametrization of the eccentricity corrections to the RR force and modes, since it favors the recovery of an underlying QC structure of the model in the zero-eccentricity limit.

First, we consider the PN expanded equations of motion in terms of
(r,p_{r},p_{\phi})
:

\displaystyle\dot{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial p_{r}}(r,p_{r},p_{\phi})=p_{r}+\epsilon^{2}\bigg[\frac{p_{r}}{r}(\nu-3)-\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}\hskip 0.5ptp_{r}}{r^{2}}\frac{\nu+1}{2}-p_{r}^{3}\frac{\nu+1}{2}\bigg]+\text{3PN expansion},

(107a)

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial p_{\phi}}(r,p_{r},p_{\phi})=\frac{p_{\phi}}{r^{2}}+\epsilon^{2}\bigg[\frac{p_{\phi}}{r^{3}}(\nu-1)-\frac{p_{\phi}^{3}}{r^{4}}\frac{\nu+1}{2}-\frac{p_{\phi}\hskip 0.5ptp_{r}^{2}}{r^{2}}\frac{\nu+1}{2}\bigg]+\text{3PN expansion},

(107b)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial H_{\text{EOB}}}{\partial r}(r,p_{r},p_{\phi})+\mathcal{F}_{r}(r,p_{r},p_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{r^{3}}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}-\epsilon^{2}\bigg[\frac{\nu+1}{r^{3}}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{4}}{r^{5}}\frac{\nu+1}{2}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}\hskip 0.5ptp_{r}^{2}}{r^{3}}\frac{\nu+1}{2}-\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{r^{2}}\frac{\nu-3}{2}-\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{r^{4}}\frac{3(\nu-1)}{2}\bigg]+\text{3PN expansion},

(107c)

where
\mathcal{F}_{r}
is given by Eq. (
25b
).
These expressions can be inverted, respectively, to get:

\displaystyle p_{r}(r,\dot{r},p_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\dot{r}+\frac{\dot{r}\epsilon^{2}}{2r^{2}}\Bigg[p_{\phi}^{2}(1+\nu)+r(6-2\nu)+r^{2}\dot{r}^{2}(1+\nu)\Bigg]+\text{3PN expansion},

(108a)

\displaystyle p_{\phi}(r,p_{r},\Omega)

\displaystyle=r^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\Omega+\frac{r\epsilon^{2}\Omega}{2}\Bigg[2+rp_{r}^{2}(1+\nu)+r^{3}\Omega^{2}+\nu(-2+r^{3}\Omega^{2})\Bigg]+\text{3PN expansion},

(108b)

\displaystyle p_{\phi}(r,p_{r},\dot{p}_{r})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{r+r^{3}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4\sqrt{r+r^{3}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r}}}\Bigg[6+\dot{p}_{r}r^{2}(5-\nu)+\dot{p}_{r}^{2}r^{4}(1+\nu)+4rp_{r}^{2}+\dot{p}_{r}r^{3}p_{r}^{2}(1+\nu)\Bigg]+\text{3PN expansion}.

(108c)

Next, we substitute Eq. (
108b
) into Eq. (
108a
) and solve iteratively for
p_{r}
to get

\displaystyle p_{r}(r,\dot{r},\Omega)

\displaystyle=\dot{r}+\frac{\dot{r}\epsilon^{2}}{2r}\Bigg[6-2\nu+r\dot{r}^{2}(1+\nu)+r^{3}(1+\nu)\Omega^{2}\Bigg]+\text{3PN expansion}.

(109)

Afterwards, we substitute Eq. (
108c
) into Eq. (
107b
) to get:

\Omega(r,p_{r},\dot{p}_{r})=\frac{\sqrt{r+r^{3}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r}}}{r^{2}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4r^{2}\sqrt{r+\dot{p}_{r}r^{3}}}\Bigg[2\nu-\dot{p}_{r}r^{2}(3+\nu)-\dot{p}_{r}^{2}r^{4}(1+\nu)+2rp_{r}^{2}(1-\nu)-\dot{p}_{r}p_{r}^{2}r^{3}(1+\nu)\Bigg]+\text{3PN expansion}.

(110)

Then we substitute Eq. (
109
) into Eq. (
110
) and solve iteratively for
\Omega
, leading to

\Omega(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r})=\frac{\sqrt{r+r^{3}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r}}}{r^{2}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4r^{2}\sqrt{r+\dot{p}_{r}r^{3}}}\Bigg[2\nu+2r\dot{r}^{2}(1-\nu)-\dot{p}_{r}r^{2}(3+\nu)-\dot{p}_{r}\dot{r}^{2}r^{3}(1+\nu)-\dot{p}_{r}^{2}r^{4}(1+\nu)\Bigg]+\text{3PN expansion}.

(111)

The next step is to substitute Eq. (
111
) into Eq. (
109
) to get

p_{r}(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r})=\dot{r}+\frac{\dot{r}\epsilon^{2}}{2r}\Bigg[7-\nu+\dot{p}_{r}r^{2}(1+\nu)+r\dot{r}^{2}(1+\nu)\Bigg]+\text{3PN expansion}.

(112)

Finally, substituting Eq. (
112
) into Eq. (
108c
) gives us

\displaystyle p_{\phi}(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{r+r^{3}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4\sqrt{r+\dot{p}_{r}r^{3}}}\Bigg\{6+\dot{p}_{r}r^{2}[5-\nu+2r\dot{r}^{2}(1+\nu)]+\dot{p}_{r}^{2}r^{4}(1+\nu)+2r\dot{r}^{2}(1+\nu)

\displaystyle\qquad-p_{r}^{2}\big[2r(-1+\nu)+\dot{p}_{r}r^{3}(1+\nu)\big]\Bigg\}+\text{3PN expansion}.

(113)

Thus, Eqs. (
111
)–(
113
) transform
(p_{r},p_{\phi},\Omega)
into
(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r})
.

The final process is to find a transformation from
\dot{p}_{r}
to
\dot{p}_{r_{*}}
.
This is convenient because
\dot{p}_{r_{*}}
is directly associated with the equations of motion (
15
).
First, we consider the relation

p_{r_{*}}=p_{r}\,\xi(r),

(114)

where the tortoise function
\xi(r)
is defined in Eq. (44) of Ref.
Khalil et al. 2023
and whose 3PN expansion is given by

\xi(r)=1-\frac{2\epsilon^{2}}{r}+\frac{3\epsilon^{4}\nu}{r^{2}}+\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{r^{3}}\Bigg[-3\nu^{2}+\nu(22-8\chi_{\text{A}}^{2})+2(\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}+2\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}+\chi_{\text{S}}^{2})\Bigg].

(115)

Then, taking a time derivative of Eq. (
114
) and substituting Eq. (
112
) yields

\dot{p}_{r_{*}}=\dot{p}_{r}\hskip 0.5pt\xi+p_{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\mathrm{d}r}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}=\dot{p}_{r}+\epsilon^{2}\left(\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}}{r^{2}}-\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r}}{r}\right)+\text{3PN expansion}.

(116)

A PN inversion of this relation gives the desired formula

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{r}

\displaystyle=\dot{p}_{r_{*}}+\frac{2\epsilon^{2}}{r^{2}}\Big(\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r-\dot{r}^{2}\Big)-\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{r^{3}}\Bigg\{\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r(-4+3\nu)+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[11-7\nu+\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r^{2}(1+\nu)\Big]+\dot{r}^{4}r(1+\nu)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{4r^{4}}\Bigg\{-4\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r(8-34\nu+3\nu^{2})+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[171-487\nu+47\nu^{2}+6\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r^{2}(6+3\nu-2\nu^{2})+\dot{p}_{r_{*}}^{2}r^{4}(1+5\nu+\nu^{2})\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\Big[2r(19+9\nu-7\nu^{2})+2\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r^{3}(2+7\nu+2\nu^{2})\Big]+3\dot{r}^{6}r^{2}(1+3\nu+\nu^{2})\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2\epsilon^{6}}{r^{4}}\Big(\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r-4\dot{r}^{2}\Big)\Big[(-1+4\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}-2\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}-\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\Big],

(117)

which we substitute in Eqs. (
111
)–(
113
) to finally obtain:

\displaystyle\Omega(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*}})

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}}{r^{2}}-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4p_{\phi,\mathrm{N}}r^{4}}\Bigg[p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)+2r^{2}(1-\nu)-p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(3+\nu)+\dot{r}^{2}(1+\nu)r^{2}(p_{\phi,\mathrm{N}}^{2}+r)\Bigg]+\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{2r^{3}}\Bigg[(\nu-2)\chi_{\text{S}}-2\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{32p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}r^{6}}\Bigg\{4r^{4}(-1+\nu)^{2}+4p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{3}(5-7\nu^{2})+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8}(-1+4\nu-\nu^{2})+2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r(16+9\nu+2\nu^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{2}(-55+46\nu+9\nu^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[-4r^{5}(-1+\nu^{2})-4p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{3}(-8-4\nu+\nu^{2})+2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{2}(1+8\nu+\nu^{2})+2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{4}(13+8\nu+7\nu^{2})\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\Big[6p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{5}\nu+r^{6}(1+\nu)^{2}+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{4}(1+4\nu+\nu^{2})\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\dot{r}^{2}\epsilon^{4}}{2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}r^{2}}\Bigg[(-1+4\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}-2\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}-\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{4\epsilon_{\text{RR}}^{5}\nu}{15p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}r^{4}}\dot{r}\Bigg\{r(17+9\alpha-9\beta)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}(3-3\alpha+9\beta)+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[6r^{2}(2+\alpha-\beta)\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{32r^{5}}\Bigg\{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}\Big[\delta(48+5\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}+(48-49\nu+2\nu^{2})\chi_{\text{S}}\Big]-2r\Big[\delta(24+25\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}+(24+7\nu-26\nu^{2})\chi_{\text{S}}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+8r^{2}\dot{r}^{2}\nu\Big[2\delta\chi_{\text{A}}-(-2+\nu)\chi_{\text{S}}\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{384p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5}r^{8}}\Bigg\{3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{12}(1+\nu-3\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{10}r(25-115\nu+35\nu^{2}-\nu^{3})-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8}r^{2}(411-45\nu-97\nu^{2}-13\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{3}(575-2045\nu+9\nu^{2}-107\nu^{3})+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{4}(-462+(-6470+492\pi^{2})\nu-834\nu^{2}+534\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-12p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{5}(7-5\nu-15\nu^{2}+13\nu^{3})+24r^{6}(-1+\nu)^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[9p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{10}r^{2}(1-11\nu-15\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})-9p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8}r^{3}(23+133\nu-19\nu^{2}+3\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{4}(-343+217\nu+269\nu^{2}+21\nu^{3})-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{5}(283-115\nu-83\nu^{2}+75\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+24p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{6}(-8+\nu+3\nu^{2}+6\nu^{3})-36r^{7}(-1+\nu)^{2}(1+\nu)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\Big[-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8}r^{4}(5+93\nu+105\nu^{2}+5\nu^{3})+9p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{5}(-31-95\nu+11\nu^{2}+3\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{6}(-3+653\nu-387\nu^{2}+9\nu^{3})-9p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{7}(9+17\nu+3\nu^{2}+3\nu^{3})+18r^{8}(-1+\nu)(1+\nu)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{6}\Big[-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{6}(7+59\nu+63\nu^{2}+7\nu^{3})+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{7}(1-17\nu-21\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{8}(1+9\nu+9\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-3r^{9}(1+\nu)^{3}\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{16p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}r^{6}}\Bigg\{4p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}\Big[p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)(-1+4\nu)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(1+\nu)(-1+4\nu)+6r^{2}-28r^{2}\nu\Big]\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+8p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}\delta\Big[-p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)-p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(1+\nu)+3r^{2}(2-7\nu)\Big]\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\Big[-4p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}(1+\nu)-4p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r(1+\nu)+2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{2}(12-76\nu+25\nu^{2})\Big]\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\dot{r}^{2}r^{2}\Big[(1-4\nu)\Big(3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(7-13\nu)+2r^{2}(-1+\nu)\Big)\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+2\Big(3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)+7p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(1-7\nu)+2r^{2}(-1+\nu)\Big)\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\Big(3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(7-85\nu+18\nu^{2})+2r^{2}(-1+\nu)\Big)\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\dot{r}^{4}r^{4}(p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+r)\Big[(-1+3\nu+4\nu^{2})\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}-2\delta(1+\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}-(1+\nu)\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\Big]\Bigg\},

(118a)

\displaystyle p_{r}(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*}})

\displaystyle=\dot{r}+\frac{\dot{r}\epsilon^{2}}{2r}\Big[7-\nu+\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r^{2}(1+\nu)+r\dot{r}^{2}(1+\nu)\Big]+\frac{\dot{r}\epsilon^{4}}{8r^{2}}\Big\{83-59\nu-\nu^{2}+2\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r^{2}(14+11\nu)+\dot{p}_{r_{*}}^{2}r^{4}(1+5\nu+\nu^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[-2r(-15-11\nu+\nu^{2})+2\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r^{3}(2+7\nu+2\nu^{2})\Big]+3\dot{r}^{4}r^{2}(1+3\nu+\nu^{2})\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\dot{r}\epsilon^{4}}{r^{2}}\Big[(-1+4\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}-2\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}-\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\Big]+\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}\dot{r}\epsilon^{5}(3+\nu)}{2r^{3}}\Big(-2\delta\chi_{\text{A}}+(-2+\nu)\chi_{\text{S}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\dot{r}\epsilon^{6}}{16r^{3}}\Bigg\{467-1275\nu+132\nu^{2}-\nu^{3}+3\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r^{2}(88+39\nu-24\nu^{2})+\dot{p}_{r_{*}}^{2}r^{4}(21+97\nu+14\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})+\dot{p}_{r_{*}}^{3}r^{6}\nu(5+6\nu)

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[-r(-291+169\nu-74\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})+2\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r^{3}(44+139\nu+26\nu^{2})+3\dot{p}_{r_{*}}^{2}r^{5}(1+11\nu+12\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\Big[3r^{2}(23+61\nu+12\nu^{2}-\nu^{3})+\dot{p}_{r_{*}}r^{4}(8+57\nu+60\nu^{2}+8\nu^{3})\Big]+\dot{r}^{6}\Big[r^{3}(5+29\nu+30\nu^{2}+5\nu^{3})\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\dot{r}\epsilon^{6}}{2r^{3}}\Bigg\{2(-5+18\nu+8\nu^{2})\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}-4\delta(5+8\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}+(-10-28\nu+6\nu^{2})\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[3r(1+\nu)(-1+4\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}-6r\delta(1+\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}-3r(1+\nu)\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\Big]\Bigg\},

(118b)

\displaystyle p_{\phi}(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r_{*}})

\displaystyle=p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}r^{2}}\Bigg\{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(7-3\nu)+2r^{2}(-1+\nu)+\dot{r}^{2}r^{2}(1+\nu)(p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}-r)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{2r}\Big[-6\delta\chi_{\text{A}}+3(-2+\nu)\chi_{\text{S}}\Big]+\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{32p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}r^{4}}\Bigg\{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8}(1+8\nu+\nu^{2})+2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r(22+7\nu)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{2}(103-110\nu-9\nu^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+4p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{3}(-9+8\nu+3\nu^{2})-4r^{4}(-1+\nu)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[6p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{2}(1+4\nu+\nu^{2})-4p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{3}(-10+2\nu+3\nu^{2})+2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{4}(-21-8\nu+\nu^{2})+4r^{5}(-1+\nu^{2})\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\Big[p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{4}(5+16\nu+5\nu^{2})-2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{5}(2+7\nu+2\nu^{2})-r^{6}(1+\nu)^{2}\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}r^{2}}\Big(2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}-r^{2}\dot{r}^{2}\Big)\Big[(-1+4\nu)\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}-2\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}-\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{4\dot{r}\epsilon_{\text{RR}}^{5}\nu}{15p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}r^{2}}\Big\{r(17+9\alpha-9\beta)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}(3-3\alpha+9\beta)+6\dot{r}^{2}r^{2}(2+\alpha-\beta)\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{32r^{3}}\Bigg\{\Big[p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}\delta(-96+119\nu)-4r\delta(36+13\nu)\Big]\chi_{\text{A}}+\Big[p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}(-96+317\nu-82\nu^{2})+4r(-36+11\nu+20\nu^{2})\Big]\chi_{\text{S}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[-48r^{2}\delta\chi_{\text{A}}+24r^{2}(-2+\nu)\chi_{\text{S}}\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{384p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5}r^{6}}\Bigg\{3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{12}(1-11\nu-15\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{10}r(41+237\nu-29\nu^{2}+7\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8}r^{2}(-691+273\nu+93\nu^{2}+5\nu^{3})+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{3}(-707+2777\nu-373\nu^{2}+31\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{4}(918+(5726-492\pi^{2})\nu+762\nu^{2}-174\nu^{3})+12p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{5}(11-17\nu-3\nu^{2}+9\nu^{3})-24r^{6}(-1+\nu)^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{10}r^{2}(5+93\nu+105\nu^{2}+5\nu^{3})+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8}r^{3}(-223-597\nu+147\nu^{2}+5\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{4}(-483+1001\nu+269\nu^{2}+9\nu^{3})+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{5}(611-267\nu-251\nu^{2}+3\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-24p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{6}(-11+4\nu+6\nu^{2}+3\nu^{3})+36r^{7}(-1+\nu)^{2}(1+\nu)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\Big[-9p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8}r^{4}(7+59\nu+63\nu^{2}+7\nu^{3})+9p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{5}(-57-89\nu+77\nu^{2}+13\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{6}(-147-887\nu+417\nu^{2}+9\nu^{3})-9p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{7}(-13-21\nu+\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})-18r^{8}(-1+\nu)(1+\nu)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{6}\Big[-3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}r^{6}(15+95\nu+99\nu^{2}+15\nu^{3})+3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r^{7}(11+77\nu+81\nu^{2}+11\nu^{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+9p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{8}(1+5\nu+5\nu^{2}+\nu^{3})+3r^{9}(1+\nu)^{3}\Big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{16p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}r^{4}}\Bigg\{\Big[-8p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}(-1+3\nu+4\nu^{2})+8p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r(7-30\nu+8\nu^{2})-8p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{2}(-2+9\nu+4\nu^{2})\Big]\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+8p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}\Big[2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)+2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(7-2\nu)+r^{2}(4-19\nu)\Big]\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\Big[8p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}(1+\nu)+8p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}r(7-2\nu)+2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r^{2}(8-72\nu+25\nu^{2})\Big]\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Big[2r^{2}(-1+4\nu)\Big(5p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(-1+11\nu)-2r^{2}(-1+\nu)\Big)\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+4r^{2}\Big(-5p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(1-47\nu)+2r^{2}(-1+\nu)\Big)\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+2r^{2}\Big(-5p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}(1+\nu)+p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}r(1-83\nu+18\nu^{2})+2r^{2}(-1+\nu)\Big)\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\Big[2r^{4}(3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+r)(-1+3\nu+4\nu^{2})\chi_{\text{A}}^{2}-4r^{4}(3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+r)(1+\nu)\delta\chi_{\text{A}}\chi_{\text{S}}-2r^{4}(3p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+r)(1+\nu)\chi_{\text{S}}^{2}\Big]\Bigg\},

(118c)

These expressions are also provided in the Supplemental Material.

To obtain a dependence on
\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}=-\xi(r)\,\partial H_{\text{EOB}}/\partial r
, one just needs to remove the dissipative terms appearing at 2.5PN order in the nonspinning part of Eqs. (
118a
) and (
118c
), flagged with the parameter
\epsilon_{\text{RR}}
.
Since
\dot{p}_{r_{*}}
appears at 1PN order in Eq. (
118b
), then one can just make the replacement
\dot{p}_{r_{*}}\to\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}
at 3PN accuracy.

A.2
PN-expanded RR force

The 1PN expressions of the PN-expanded RR force components derived in Sec.
III.1
, in terms of the leading-order gauge constants
\alpha
and
\beta
, are given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{1\text{PN}}(r,p_{r},p_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{8\nu p_{\phi}}{5r^{3}}\left[(2\alpha+1)\,p_{r}^{2}-\frac{\alpha+2}{r^{2}}\hskip 0.5ptp_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{\alpha-2}{r}\right]+\frac{\epsilon^{2}\nu p_{\phi}}{r^{3}}\bigg\{p_{r}^{4}\left[\left(-\frac{8\alpha}{5}-\frac{236}{105}\right)\nu-\frac{8\alpha}{5}-\frac{548}{105}\right]

(119a)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{r^{3}}\left[\left(\frac{6\alpha}{5}+\frac{188}{105}\right)\nu+\frac{2423\alpha}{210}+\frac{1577}{63}\right]+\frac{p_{r}^{2}p_{\phi}^{2}}{r^{2}}\left[\left(\frac{548}{105}-\frac{4\alpha}{5}\right)\nu-\frac{4\alpha}{5}-\frac{88}{105}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{p_{\phi}^{4}}{r^{4}}\left[\left(\frac{4\alpha}{5}+\frac{424}{105}\right)\nu+\frac{4\alpha}{5}-\frac{22}{21}\right]+\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{r}\left[\left(\frac{724}{21}-\frac{42\alpha}{5}\right)\nu-\frac{2423\alpha}{70}-\frac{3821}{105}\right]

(119b)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left[\left(\frac{188}{35}-2\alpha\right)\nu-\frac{1583\alpha}{210}+\frac{1195}{63}\right]\bigg\},

(119c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}^{1\text{PN}}(r,p_{r},p_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{8\nu p_{r}}{15r^{3}}\left[6\hskip 0.5pt(\alpha-\beta+2)\,p_{r}^{2}-\frac{3\hskip 0.5pt(\alpha-3\beta-1)}{r^{2}}\hskip 0.5ptp_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{9\alpha-9\beta+17}{r}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{2}\nu p_{r}}{r^{3}}\Bigg\{p_{r}^{4}\left[\left(-\frac{8\alpha}{5}-\frac{848}{105}\right)\nu-\frac{8\alpha}{5}+\frac{44}{21}\right]+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{r^{3}}\left[\left(-\frac{2\alpha}{5}-\frac{3256}{105}\right)\nu+\frac{71\alpha}{210}+\frac{24\beta}{5}+\frac{89}{63}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{p_{r}^{2}p_{\phi}^{2}}{r^{2}}\left[\left(-\frac{4\alpha}{5}-\frac{148}{15}\right)\nu-\frac{4\alpha}{5}+\frac{878}{105}\right]+\frac{p_{\phi}^{4}}{r^{4}}\left[\left(\frac{4\alpha}{5}-\frac{548}{105}\right)\nu+\frac{4\alpha}{5}+\frac{88}{105}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{r}\left[\left(\frac{40}{21}-10\alpha\right)\nu-\frac{1863\alpha}{70}-\frac{48\beta}{5}-\frac{1057}{15}\right]+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left[\left(\frac{1844}{105}-6\alpha\right)\nu-\frac{911\alpha}{70}-\frac{96\beta}{5}-\frac{6577}{105}\right]\Bigg\}.

(119d)

The complete 3PN expressions and the corresponding Schott terms are provided in the Supplemental Material.

A.3
Eccentricity corrections to the RR force and the sigmoid resummation

PN expressions for the RR force tend to grow significantly during plunges or strong periastron passages, spoiling the numerical integration of the equations of motion.
Here, we introduce an
sigmoid resummation
which limits their growth.

We consider a PN expanded function
f^{\text{PN}}
which has been factorized as

\displaystyle f^{\text{F}}

\displaystyle\equiv f^{\text{fact}}\,f^{\text{corr}},

(120a)

\displaystyle f^{\text{corr}}

\displaystyle\equiv 1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{i}\Delta f_{i},

(120b)

where
f^{\text{fact}}
is another factorization containing the leading-order (Newtonian) term of
f^{\text{PN}}
(and, potentially, additional higher PN terms), and the functions
\Delta f_{i}
are defined in such a way that the PN expansion of
f^{\text{F}}
coincides with
f^{\text{PN}}
at a given
n
-th PN order.

Then, the sigmoid resummation of
f^{\text{corr}}
is defined as

\displaystyle f^{\text{exp}}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{2}{1+\exp(f^{\text{arg exp}})},

(121a)

\displaystyle f^{\text{arg exp}}

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{i}\Delta f_{i}^{\text{arg exp}},

(121b)

where the corrections
\Delta f_{i}^{\text{arg exp}}
make the PN expansion of
f^{\text{exp}}
to coincide with
f^{\text{corr}}
.
At 3PN order, a straightforward calculation (a Taylor expansion in the PN parameter
\epsilon
) yields

\displaystyle\Delta f^{\text{arg exp}}_{1}

\displaystyle=-2\Delta f_{1},

(122a)

\displaystyle\Delta f^{\text{arg exp}}_{2}

\displaystyle=-2\Delta f_{2},

(122b)

\displaystyle\Delta f^{\text{arg exp}}_{3}

\displaystyle=-2\Delta f_{3}-\frac{2}{3}\Delta f_{1}^{3},

(122c)

\displaystyle\Delta f^{\text{arg exp}}_{4}

\displaystyle=-2\Delta f_{4}-\frac{6}{3}\Delta f_{1}^{2}\Delta f_{2},

(122d)

\displaystyle\Delta f^{\text{arg exp}}_{5}

\displaystyle=-2\Delta f_{5}-\frac{2}{5}\Delta f_{1}^{5}-2\Delta f_{1}\Delta f_{2}^{2}-2\Delta f_{1}^{2}\Delta f_{3},

(122e)

\displaystyle\Delta f^{\text{arg exp}}_{6}

\displaystyle=-2\Delta f_{6}-\frac{2}{3}\Delta f_{2}^{3}-2\Delta f_{1}^{4}\Delta f_{2}-2\Delta f_{1}^{2}\Delta f_{4}-4\Delta f_{1}\Delta f_{2}\Delta f_{3}.

(122f)

Thus, given the PN coefficients
\Delta f_{i}
, these formulas give the elements entering into the argument of the exponential.
Importantly, if corrections to
f^{\text{PN}}
start at 1PN order (
n=2
), then
\Delta f_{1}=\nolinebreak 0
, and Eqs. (
122
) simplify significantly.

In this way, we can resum the PN-expanded function
f^{\text{PN}}
as

f^{\text{F, exp}}\equiv f^{\text{fact}}\;\frac{2}{1+\exp(f^{\text{arg exp}})},

(123)

from which we can observe the following properties:

•

f^{\text{F, exp}}\to f^{\text{fact}}\,
when
f^{\text{arg exp}}\to 0
, e.g., in the Newtonian, low-velocity limit.

•

f^{\text{F, exp}}\to 0\,
or
f^{\text{F, exp}}\to 2f^{\text{fact}}\,
when
f^{\text{arg exp}}\to\pm\infty
, respectively, e.g., during strong periastron passages or the plunge.

The connection with the RR force (
42
) is immediate:
after extracting a factor which contains the leading order RR force
(
\hskip 0.5ptf^{\text{fact}}\in\nolinebreak{\big\{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{modes}},\,p_{r}/p_{\phi}\ \mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{modes}}\big\}}
)
, the corresponding corrections
(
\hskip 0.5ptf^{\text{corr}}\in\big\{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}},\,\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}\big\}
)
can be resummed with the method discussed above.
This will result in corrections to the RR force which have a controlled behavior during high-velocity configurations.
Particularly, the fact that
f^{\text{F, exp}}\to\{0,\,2f^{\text{fact}}\}
is the key property that enables the RR force to stay constrained during the plunge, thus avoiding a premature end of the dynamics.

As discussed in Sec.
III.2.2
, we first calculate the PN-expanded eccentricity corrections,
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc, PN}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc, PN}}
, from the PN expression of the RR force [e.g., see (
119
)] and the proposed resummation (
42
).
Then, these are transformed to the parametrization
(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r*,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{cons}})
with the rules derived in Appendix
A.1
.
The resulting PN-expanded expressions are then resummed with the rules (
121
) and Eq. (
122
).
This process leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{ecc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+\exp\big(\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}\big)},

(124a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{ecc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+\exp\big(\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}\big)},

(124b)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\phi}^{\text{arg exp}}

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon^{2}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{r}\left(\frac{(273\alpha-506)\nu}{336}+\frac{717\alpha-7918}{4032}\right)-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{5(63\alpha+86)\nu}{336}+\frac{213\alpha+3674}{4032}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{4}}{r^{3}}\left(\frac{(21\alpha-22)\nu}{168}+\frac{55-42\alpha}{336}\right)-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{2/3}}{r^{4/3}}\frac{(\alpha-2)(2828\nu+2761)}{4032}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{8/3}}{r^{7/3}}\frac{(\alpha+2)(2828\nu+2761)}{4032}-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{10/3}}{r^{8/3}}\frac{(\alpha-2)(4\nu-1)}{144}+\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{16/3}}{r^{11/3}}\frac{(\alpha+2)(4\nu-1)}{144}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Bigg[\frac{41(21\alpha-38)\nu}{336}+\frac{1221\alpha+3946}{1344}+\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{2}}{r}\left(-\frac{(63\alpha+116)\nu}{168}+\frac{1-21\alpha}{168}\right)-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{10/3}}{r^{5/3}}\frac{(2\alpha+1)(4\nu-1)}{144}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{2/3}}{r^{1/3}}\frac{(2\alpha+1)(2828\nu+2761)}{4032}\Bigg]-r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{4}\Bigg[\frac{(21\alpha+1)\nu}{42}-\frac{29}{42}\Bigg]\Bigg\}\;\Bigg(1-\frac{2\alpha+1}{4}\,r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}+\frac{\alpha+2}{4}\,r^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\Bigg)^{-1},

(125a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\text{arg exp}}

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon^{2}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{r}\left(\frac{(2289\alpha-1512\beta-470)\nu}{1008}+\frac{-627\alpha+8064\beta+7778}{1344}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{(-315\alpha+756\beta+1600)\nu}{336}+\frac{7851\alpha-24192\beta-4586}{4032}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{4}}{r^{3}}\left(\frac{(21\alpha-126\beta+95)\nu}{168}-\frac{21\alpha+22}{168}\right)-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{2/3}}{r^{4/3}}\frac{(2828\nu+2761)(9\alpha-9\beta+17)}{12096}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{8/3}}{r^{7/3}}\frac{(2828\nu+2761)(\alpha-3\beta-1)}{4032}-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{10/3}}{r^{8/3}}\frac{(4\nu-1)(9\alpha-9\beta+17)}{432}+\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{16/3}}{r^{11/3}}\frac{(4\nu-1)(\alpha-3\beta-1)}{144}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\Bigg[\frac{(861\alpha-252\beta+712)\nu}{336}+\frac{-41\alpha+2688\beta+1274}{448}-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{10/3}}{r^{5/3}}\frac{(4\nu-1)(\alpha-\beta+2)}{72}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{2/3}}{r^{1/3}}\frac{(2828\nu+2761)(\alpha-\beta+2)}{2016}-\frac{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{2}}{r}\left(\frac{(9\alpha+5)\nu}{24}-\frac{-42\alpha+84\beta-607}{336}\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{4}\Bigg[\frac{(-42\alpha+63\beta-20)\nu}{84}+\frac{42\beta-139}{168}\Bigg]\Bigg\}\;\Bigg(\frac{-3\alpha-10}{6}-\frac{\alpha-\beta+2}{2}\,r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}^{2}+\frac{\alpha-3\beta-1}{4}\,r^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{p}_{r_{*},\hskip 0.5pt\text{cons}}\Bigg)^{-1}.

(125b)

These expressions are employed in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
The 3PN generalizations are provided in the Supplemental Material.

A.4
Eccentricity corrections to the waveform modes

As discussed in Sec.
V.1
, we calculate the eccentricity corrections,
h_{\ell m}^{\mathrm{ecc}}
, to the factorized waveform modes (
61
) using the Newtonian prefactor given in Eq. (
67
) and the 1PN (relative to the leading-order of the
(\ell,m)
mode) expressions for the modes given in Ref.
Gamboa et al. 2025a
.
Then, we transform the resulting expressions to the parametrization
(r,\dot{r},\dot{p}_{r*,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{cons}})
with the rules derived in Appendix
A.1
.
This process yields the following corrections, as used in
SEOBNRv6EHM
:

\displaystyle h_{22}^{\mathrm{ecc}}

\displaystyle=1+\epsilon^{2}\Bigg\{56(\nu-3)\,r^{2}+(86-55\nu)\,r^{5/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2/3}+(86-55\nu)\,r^{2/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8/3}+3(8\nu-5)\,p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}+(30\nu+11)\,rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}\,\bigg[-2i(55\nu-86)\,r^{5/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5/3}+6i(15\nu+2)\,rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}-2i(96\nu-25)\,r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\bigg[(55\nu-86)\,r^{8/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2/3}-21(\nu+1)\,r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+(72\nu-129)\,r^{3}\bigg]+\dot{r}^{3}\,6i(15\nu+2)\,r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\,(-45\nu-6)\,r^{4}\Bigg\}\;\Bigg[42\,r^{2}\left(p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+2irp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}-r^{2}\dot{r}^{2}+r\right)\Bigg]^{-1}\!,

(126a)

\displaystyle h_{21}^{\mathrm{ecc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2/3}}{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4/3}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{1}{84r^{4/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{10/3}}\Bigg\{4(26\nu-69)\,rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}-56(\nu-1)\,r^{2}+(177-46\nu)\,r^{2/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8/3}+(43-2\nu)p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad-\dot{r}\,6i(12\nu-83)\,rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}+\dot{r}^{2}\bigg[(-38\nu-212)\,r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+28(\nu+1)\,r^{3}\bigg]\Bigg\},

(126b)

\displaystyle h_{33}^{\mathrm{ecc}}

\displaystyle=1+\epsilon^{2}\Bigg\{18i(7-4\nu)\,r^{2/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{11/3}-63i(4\nu-7)\,r^{5/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5/3}+6i(5\nu-1)\,p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5}+9i(14\nu-5)\,rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}+12i(14\nu-43)\,r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}\,\bigg[36(4\nu-7)\,r^{8/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2/3}+54(4\nu-7)\,r^{5/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8/3}+(18-90\nu)\,rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}+270(\nu-1)\,r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+(182-100\nu)\,r^{3}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\bigg[54i(4\nu-7)\,r^{8/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5/3}-6i(19\nu+7)\,r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}+54i(6\nu-7)\,r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{3}\bigg[(126-72\nu)\,r^{11/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2/3}+(30-42\nu)\,r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+(222-66\nu)\,r^{4}\bigg]-\dot{r}^{4}\,36i(4\nu+1)\,r^{4}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{5}\,(48\nu+12)\,r^{5}\Bigg\}\;\Bigg[36ir^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}+126ir^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}+\dot{r}\left(-108r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}-72r^{4}\right)-\dot{r}^{2}\,108ir^{4}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}+\dot{r}^{3}\,36r^{5}\Bigg]^{-1}\!,

(126c)

\displaystyle h_{32}^{\mathrm{ecc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2/3}}{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4/3}}+\epsilon^{2}\Bigg\{-4\left(320\nu^{2}-1115\nu+328\right)r^{2/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{11/3}-6\left(25\nu^{2}+205\nu-53\right)p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5}-120\left(3\nu^{2}+2\nu-1\right)r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\left(1790\nu^{2}-2990\nu+874\right)rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}\;\bigg[-i\left(320\nu^{2}-1115\nu+328\right)r^{5/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8/3}-15i\left(72\nu^{2}-325\nu+95\right)rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}-10i\left(35\nu^{2}-215\nu+64\right)r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-60i\left(3\nu^{2}-4\nu+1\right)r^{3}\bigg]+\dot{r}^{2}\bigg[-12\left(90\nu^{2}+250\nu-89\right)r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}-60\left(3\nu^{2}+2\nu-1\right)r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{3}\bigg[30i\left(3\nu^{2}+2\nu-1\right)r^{4}-15i\left(4\nu^{2}+43\nu-14\right)r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}\bigg]\Bigg\}\;\Bigg[90(3\nu-1)(4p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}+i\,r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r})\,r^{4/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{10/3}\Bigg]^{-1}\!,

(126d)

\displaystyle h_{44}^{\mathrm{ecc}}

\displaystyle=1+\epsilon^{2}\Bigg\{-14\left(2625\nu^{2}-5870\nu+1614\right)r^{8/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2/3}-102\left(2625\nu^{2}-5870\nu+1614\right)r^{5/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8/3}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+40\left(1344\nu^{2}-4699\nu+1437\right)r^{3}-12\left(2625\nu^{2}-5870\nu+1614\right)r^{2/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{14/3}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+180\left(39\nu^{2}-50\nu+10\right)p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}+9\left(9420\nu^{2}-15580\nu+3127\right)rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}+9\left(21160\nu^{2}-46020\nu+13241\right)r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}\;\bigg[720i\left(72\nu^{2}-28\nu-1\right)rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5}-108i\left(2625\nu^{2}-5870\nu+1614\right)r^{8/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5/3}-24i\left(345\nu^{2}+5320\nu-2076\right)r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-36i\left(3765\nu^{2}-4700\nu+1237\right)r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}-48i\left(2625\nu^{2}-5870\nu+1614\right)r^{5/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{11/3}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\bigg[36\left(2625\nu^{2}-5870\nu+1614\right)r^{11/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2/3}+72\left(2625\nu^{2}-5870\nu+1614\right)r^{8/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8/3}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+180\left(-393\nu^{2}+118\nu+16\right)r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}+18\left(10740\nu^{2}-30320\nu+8561\right)r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}-120\left(819\nu^{2}+61\nu-87\right)r^{4}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{3}\bigg[48i\left(2625\nu^{2}-5870\nu+1614\right)r^{11/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5/3}-7920i\left(3\nu^{2}+2\nu-1\right)r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}+36i\left(5415\nu^{2}-10090\nu+2568\right)r^{4}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\bigg[-12\left(2625\nu^{2}-5870\nu+1614\right)r^{14/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2/3}-540\left(109\nu^{2}-54\nu+2\right)r^{4}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}-540\left(43\nu^{2}-230\nu+68\right)r^{5}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad-\dot{r}^{5}\,2160i\left(35\nu^{2}-2\nu-4\right)r^{5}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}+\dot{r}^{6}\,540\left(35\nu^{2}-2\nu-4\right)r^{6}

\displaystyle\qquad\Bigg\}\;\Bigg\{660(3\nu-1)\,r^{2}\bigg[6p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}+51rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+7r^{2}+\dot{r}\,irp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}\left(24p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+54r\right)-\dot{r}^{2}18r^{2}\left(2p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+r\right)-\dot{r}^{3}\,24ir^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}+\dot{r}^{4}\,6r^{4}\bigg]\Bigg\}^{-1}\!,

(126e)

\displaystyle h_{43}^{\mathrm{ecc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2/3}}{p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4/3}}+\epsilon^{2}\Bigg\{-352\left(2\nu^{2}-3\nu+1\right)r^{3}-12\left(160\nu^{2}-547\nu+222\right)r^{5/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8/3}+\left(1064\nu^{2}-12701\nu+3596\right)p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{6}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\left(9980\nu^{2}-25084\nu+11254\right)rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}+\left(2620\nu^{2}-7578\nu+3484\right)r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}-69\left(160\nu^{2}-547\nu+222\right)r^{2/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{14/3}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}\,\bigg[440i\left(2\nu^{2}-3\nu+1\right)r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}-4i\left(2093\nu^{2}-5999\nu+2168\right)rp_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{5}-2i\left(2438\nu^{2}-17569\nu+7398\right)r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-30i\left(160\nu^{2}-547\nu+222\right)r^{5/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{11/3}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{2}\bigg[6\left(160\nu^{2}-547\nu+222\right)r^{8/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{8/3}-18\left(348\nu^{2}+900\nu-467\right)r^{2}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{4}-2\left(566\nu^{2}+5035\nu-2433\right)r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+352\nu(2\nu-1)r^{4}\bigg]+\dot{r}^{3}\bigg[-4i\left(458\nu^{2}+1846\nu-907\right)r^{3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{3}-220i\left(2\nu^{2}+\nu-1\right)r^{4}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+\dot{r}^{4}\bigg[\left(-8\nu^{2}+1316\nu-560\right)r^{4}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}-88\left(2\nu^{2}+\nu-1\right)r^{5}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\Bigg\}\;\Bigg[132(2\nu-1)\,r^{4/3}p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{10/3}\left(23p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}^{2}+10ip_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\mathrm{N}}r\hskip 0.5pt\dot{r}-2r^{2}\dot{r}^{2}+4r\right)\Bigg]^{-1}\!.

(126f)

These expressions are also provided in the Supplemental Material.

We note that the eccentricity corrections with
\ell+m
odd have the leading-order contribution
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc},\,\text{LO}}=r^{2/3}/p_{\phi,\hskip 0.5pt\text{N}}^{4/3}
, instead of
h_{\ell m}^{\text{ecc},\,\text{LO}}=1
as the
\ell+m
even modes.
This discrepancy comes from the definition of
\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}
in Eq. (
63
).
With such definition, one has
\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}\sim 1+\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
in the
\ell+m
odd case
only
for QC orbits, for which
p_{\phi}\to 1/v+\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
.
This is not surprising considering that the factor
\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}
was introduced for QC binaries in Refs.
Damour and Nagar 2007
;
Damour and Nagar 2008
;
Damour et al. 2009
.
If one keeps the idea of having corrections in the mode factorization [Eqs. (
61
) and (
62
)] that become unity at Newtonian order, then one could consider the definition

\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}H_{\text{eff}},&\quad\ell+m\text{ even},\\
p_{\phi}/(r^{2}\hskip 0.5pt\Omega),&\quad\ell+m\text{ odd},\end{array}\right.

(127)

which satisfies
\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}\sim 1+\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
for generic planar orbits.
We do not implement this new definition in
SEOBNRv6EHM
since a change in
\hat{S}_{\text{eff}}
would propagate to the
f_{\ell m}^{\text{qc}}
factors.
We leave for future work the derivation of expressions consistent with the definition in Eq. (
127
) and the study of their impact on the accuracy of the modes.

A.5
Orbital frequency for circular orbits

The 5PN expression for the orbital frequency of circular orbits
\Omega_{\text{c}}
expressed as function of the separation
r
, discussed in Sec.
III.2.1
and used in the RR force (
42
) and waveform modes (
61
), is calculated by perturbatively solving the equation
\dot{p}_{r}=0=\partial H_{\text{EOB}}/\partial p_{\phi}\,|_{p_{r}=0}
for
p_{\phi}=p_{\phi}(r)
, and then replacing the solution into
\Omega_{\text{c}}\equiv\partial H_{\text{EOB}}/\partial p_{\phi}\,|_{p_{r}=0}
, followed by a further expansion.
This results in the following PN expression:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{c}}

\displaystyle\equiv\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{0}}+\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{1}}+\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{2}}+\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{3}}+\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{4}}\,,

(128a)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{3/2}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{r^{5/2}}\frac{\nu}{2}+\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{r^{7/2}}\left(\frac{3\nu^{2}}{8}-\frac{15\nu}{8}\right)+\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{r^{9/2}}\bigg[\frac{5\nu^{3}}{16}-\frac{13\nu^{2}}{16}+\left(\frac{41\pi^{2}}{32}-\frac{1585}{48}\right)\nu\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{8}}{r^{11/2}}\bigg[\frac{35\nu^{4}}{128}-\frac{33\nu^{3}}{64}+\left(\frac{17821}{384}-\frac{205\pi^{2}}{128}\right)\nu^{2}+\left(\frac{153211}{1920}-\frac{11375\pi^{2}}{2048}-32\gamma_{\text{E}}-64\ln 2\right)\nu+16\nu\ln r\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{10}}{r^{13/2}}\bigg[\frac{63\nu^{5}}{256}-\frac{45\nu^{4}}{128}+\left(\frac{6415}{768}-\frac{41\pi^{2}}{128}\right)\nu^{3}+\left(\frac{171571}{3840}-\frac{3629\pi^{2}}{4096}+\frac{16\gamma_{\text{E}}}{5}+\frac{32\ln 2}{5}\right)\nu^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{31511}{26880}-\frac{3\,a_{6}}{2}\right)\nu-\left(\frac{224\nu^{2}}{5}+\frac{3502\nu}{35}\right)\ln r\bigg],

(128b)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{r^{3}}\Bigg[-\delta\chi_{A}+\bigg(\frac{\nu}{2}-1\bigg)\chi_{S}\Bigg]+\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{r^{4}}\left[\frac{27\nu^{2}\chi_{S}}{16}-\left(\frac{45\delta\chi_{A}}{32}+\frac{63\chi_{S}}{32}\right)\nu\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{7}}{r^{5}}\bigg[\frac{279\nu^{3}\chi_{S}}{128}-\left(\frac{63\delta\chi_{A}}{128}+\frac{357\chi_{S}}{128}\right)\nu^{2}+\left(\frac{1413\delta\chi_{A}}{128}+\frac{357\chi_{S}}{32}\right)\nu\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{9}}{r^{6}}\bigg[\frac{1061\nu^{4}\chi_{S}}{768}-\left(\frac{359\delta\chi_{A}}{384}+\frac{1333\chi_{S}}{192}\right)\nu^{3}-\left(\frac{75\delta\chi_{A}}{128}+\frac{575\chi_{S}}{256}\right)\nu^{2}+\left(\frac{837\delta\chi_{A}}{64}+\frac{621\chi_{S}}{32}-2\,\hat{d}_{\text{SO}}\right)\nu\bigg],

(128c)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{r^{9/2}}\Bigg[\left(2\chi_{A}^{2}+\frac{25\chi_{S}^{2}}{8}\right)\nu^{2}+\left(-\frac{23\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}}{2}-\frac{11\chi_{A}^{2}}{2}-10\chi_{S}^{2}\right)\nu+2\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}+\chi_{A}^{2}+\chi_{S}^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{8}}{r^{11/2}}\Bigg[\left(3\chi_{A}^{2}+\frac{247\chi_{S}^{2}}{32}\right)\nu^{3}-\left(\frac{1139\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}}{64}+\frac{131\chi_{A}^{2}}{8}+\frac{1049\chi_{S}^{2}}{64}\right)\nu^{2}+\Bigg(\frac{71\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}}{4}+\frac{185\chi_{A}^{2}}{32}+\frac{383\chi_{S}^{2}}{32}\bigg)\nu\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon^{10}}{r^{13/2}}\Bigg\{\left(\frac{15\chi_{A}^{2}}{4}-\frac{3761\chi_{S}^{2}}{512}\right)\nu^{4}+\Bigg[\frac{79843\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}}{1024}+\frac{222349\chi_{S}^{2}}{2048}+\left(\frac{415701}{2048}+\frac{123\pi^{2}}{16}\right)\chi_{A}^{2}\Bigg]\nu^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{11267\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}}{512}+\frac{28235\chi_{A}^{2}}{512}+\frac{497\chi_{S}^{2}}{512}\right)\nu^{3}-\Bigg[\left(\frac{17155}{128}+\frac{123\pi^{2}}{32}\right)\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}+\left(\frac{6911}{128}+\frac{123\pi^{2}}{64}\right)\chi_{A}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\left(\frac{2561}{32}+\frac{123\pi^{2}}{64}\right)\chi_{S}^{2}\Bigg]\nu\Bigg\},

(128d)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon^{7}}{r^{5}}\left[\left(3\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}^{2}+\frac{3\chi_{A}^{2}\chi_{S}}{2}+\frac{3\chi_{S}^{3}}{2}\right)\nu-6\nu^{2}\chi_{A}^{2}\chi_{S}\right]-\frac{\epsilon^{9}}{r^{6}}\Bigg[\left(\frac{51\delta\chi_{A}^{3}}{4}+\frac{21\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}^{2}}{4}+\frac{403\chi_{A}^{2}\chi_{S}}{8}+\frac{21\chi_{S}^{3}}{8}\right)\nu^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\frac{115\delta\chi_{A}^{3}}{16}+\frac{229\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}^{2}}{16}+\frac{383\chi_{A}^{2}\chi_{S}}{16}+\frac{89\chi_{S}^{3}}{16}\right)\nu-\frac{21}{2}\nu^{3}\chi_{A}^{2}\chi_{S}+\delta\chi_{A}^{3}+3\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}^{2}+3\chi_{A}^{2}\chi_{S}+\chi_{S}^{3}\Bigg],

(128e)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{c},\hskip 0.5ptS^{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon^{10}}{r^{13/2}}\Bigg[3\nu^{3}\chi_{A}^{2}\chi_{S}^{2}-\left(30\delta\chi_{A}^{3}\chi_{S}+\frac{3\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}^{3}}{2}+6\chi_{A}^{4}+\frac{339\chi_{A}^{2}\chi_{S}^{2}}{4}+\frac{3\chi_{S}^{4}}{4}\right)\nu^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Bigg(\frac{21\delta\chi_{A}^{3}\chi_{S}}{2}+\frac{39\delta\chi_{A}\chi_{S}^{3}}{2}+\frac{3\chi_{A}^{4}}{2}+\frac{45\chi_{A}^{2}\chi_{S}^{2}}{2}+6\chi_{S}^{4}\Bigg)\nu\Bigg],

(128f)

where
\gamma_{\text{E}}\approx 0.577
is Euler’s constant.
In the Supplemental Material, we provide this expression along with its
(0,5)
Padé resummation,
P^{0}_{5}[\Omega_{\text{c}}]
, as used in
SEOBNRv6EHM
.
Since the
SEOBNRv5
Hamiltonian includes complete spin effects only up to 4PN order, along with partial 5PN nonspinning contributions
Khalil et al. 2023
, the 5PN expression for
\Omega_{\text{c}}
(
128
) is only partially determined.

\big(a_{6},\hat{d}_{\mathrm{SO}},\Delta t_{\mathrm{ISCO}}^{22}\big)
